# DEDICATION.

...the norm and exemplar, confirming the dictates of both his Ethical and Political doctrine: Hence, through this <note>referring to the "Art" or "Science" mentioned below</note> Kings reign, the Rights and Laws of Empires are established, peoples are strengthened by Religion, and the health of the World is stabilized. This very Art, the investigator of all Sciences, was begun under the Empire of **YOUR** most wise Father, **FERDINAND III**, while he moved among the living; now at last completed in all its parts, I lay it before the August Throne of **YOUR** Sacred Majesty. That which he had perceived only by reputation, **YOU**, **MOST GLORIOUS CAESAR**, as the genuine Heir to your Father's **GLORY**, have made entirely **YOUR OWN RIGHT**, as is due to the capacity of **YOUR** Great Mind. Nor is this without merit; for you have so fulfilled the sum of extraordinary virtues—as much as the youthful and vivid joy of **YOUR** nature was able—that in the foundations of those great Thoughts which you press deep within your breast for the good of the World, you seem to have made giant strides thus far. While you rise into strengths mature for the highest affairs, with such celebrity and clarity of fame, it seems the Roman Empire <note>The Holy Roman Empire</note> expects something greater from **YOUR** fortune than what it has already given **YOU**; with such prudence and fortitude, that having acquired it...

<meta>The catchword "negotio" indicates the beginning of the next page's discussion on the "business" or "matter" at hand. This page serves as the preface to the reader, establishing the historical conflict surrounding Ramon Llull.</meta>

# TO THE

## B E N E V O L E N T  R E A D E R.

<margin>The Lullist Factions.</margin>
<margin>The persecutions of Llull.</margin>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut drop cap 'C' begins the text, intricately decorated with foliate and scroll motifs.</image-desc>

Celebrated at all times has been the lawsuit and controversy between *Ramon Llull* <note>original: "Raymundum Lullum"; a 13th-century Majorcan philosopher and mystic</note> and the Peripatetic school <note>Followers of Aristotle</note>, driven by the sharpest skirmishes. Indeed, by those things which he promised in that *Art* which he calls *Great and Inventive of Truth*, he filled the world with such great admiration that there is scarcely any eager follower of such recondite learning found in the Academic wrestling-grounds who has not spent the powers of his intellect either in explaining or in refuting it. Some, having written vast commentaries upon it, believed—not without benefit to the Literary Republic <note>The "Republic of Letters," the international community of scholars</note>—that they had most deeply illuminated whatever was obscure and enveloped in Cimmerian darkness; yet they acted in such a way that they only added darkness to darkness. Certain others, swelling with a Thrasonic spirit <note>Boastful or bragging, after the character Thraso in Roman comedy</note>, boasted that they intimately mastered the Art rather than actually fulfilling it in practice. There were not lacking those who, since they either did not penetrate the more hidden recesses of the Art, or judged with difficulty that the Art could be converted to some human use desired until now, were driven headlong by an impulse of despair; they condemned the Author in public writings, exploded with a thousand kinds of reproaches, as if he were liable for the crime of imposture and false-craft <note>original: "ψευδοτεχνίας" (pseudotechnias)</note>. This went so far, indeed, that his most celebrated dogma was torn apart into open factional divisions, finding many and noble patrons and most eager defenders of this teaching; but on the other hand, it found even more—I will not say followers of a new doctrine—but rather most haughty cavilers and sharpest antagonists. Those Lullists who follow the dogmas of the Author do not only extol him to the Heavens with marvelous titles of praise as a man full of holiness and religion—inasmuch as he led a certain exotic life, removed from all necessity of human fellowship, on Mount *Randa*—but they assert that there, absorbed in the contemplation of divine matters, he acquired this admirable kind of learning more truly by celestial light and the dictation of the Holy Spirit than by intense study or innate sagacity of mind and labor. On this account, they assert him to be all-knowing <note>original: "πανεπίσημον" (panepisemon)</note> and God-taught <note>original: "θεοδίδακτον" (theodidakton)</note>; these things *Nicolas Caussin* <note>A 17th-century Jesuit scholar</note>, a supporter of Llull, pursues more fully in his life of the same, described in an elegant and polished style. Nevertheless, there arose in later times several "Aristarchuses" <note>Severe critics, named after the Greek grammarian Aristarchus of Samothrace</note> of a more bitter mind, who, attacking this innocent man with harsher critical marks, pricked him in such unworthy ways that, beyond the sin of imposture and trickery, they were not ashamed to imprudently accuse him also of the nefarious crime of heresy and Necromancy. I, in so perplexed a...

<summary>This preface introduces the intense historical debate surrounding Ramon Llull and his "Great Art," noting the divide between his devoted followers who view him as divinely inspired and critics who accuse him of heresy and fraud.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Ars Magna, Lullism, Scholasticism, Mount Randa, Nicolas Caussin, accusations of heresy, history of philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to a work on Lullism, establishing the historical and legal legitimacy of Ramon Llull’s teachings following initial ecclesiastical condemnation.</meta>

# PREFACE

<margin>
The doctrine of *Llull*, condemned through a deceitful papal bull.
</margin>
<margin>
*Llull* is restored by Apostolic Authority.
</margin>
<margin>
The privilege of *Emperor Charles V* in favor of *Llull*.
</margin>
...hesitant and uncertain in this business, I do not know which side to support. This I do know: *Llull* was a man devoted to GOD and most burning with zeal for spreading the Holy Roman *Church* (as the monuments he left to posterity more than sufficiently testify). But I proceed to our intended goal, which is the examination of the *Lullian* doctrine. I am not unaware that *Nicholas Eymerich* <note>A 14th-century Inquisitor General of Aragon known for his fierce opposition to Llull.</note> was the first of all who—whether he did so out of ignorance or spurred by the sting of envy, I know not—compiled up to two hundred propositions from Llull's writings which were contrary to the Orthodox faith (as he himself indeed thought). These were presented to *Gregory XI*, then residing at *Avignon*, and subjected to Ecclesiastical Censure. Having obtained a *Papal Bull* through deception <note>original: "subreptionem," a legal term for obtaining a favor by concealing the truth.</note>, he condemned the *Author* together with his *Books* in a public judgment as infamous with the mark of heresy. This most unjust sentence vehemently inflamed the minds of those who favored *Llull*; thus they left no stone unturned to bring the causes of the unjust condemnation and the wrongly explained propositions of the condemned doctrine back to the anvil for reshaping. This was finally achieved through the labor of *Bernard Ermengand* of the *Order of Preachers* <note>The Dominicans.</note>, a Provincial, through twelve most skillful *Doctors of Sacred Theology* assembled by him for this purpose, with the fairness that was right. Whence in the year 1419, by the authority of *Pope Martin V*, the *German Cardinal of the title of St. Eusebius* <note>referring to Cardinal Alemannus</note> of the Holy Roman Church, having maturely considered the matter, finally decided the pending lawsuit of the agitated cause in favor of *Llull* through a newly obtained *Bull* via *Bernard Castelli, the Bishop*. He willed that the Bull of *Eymerich*, deceitfully extorted from *Gregory XI*, be held by all as fraudulent, vain, and void. Subsequently, the *Council of Trent*, at the solicitation of the *Spaniards* and after the case was reviewed by new *Theologians* chosen for that purpose, ratified and confirmed this, granting that the *Lullian* Books could be safely read as being free from all suspicion of heresy. Added to these was the most ample privilege of *Emperor Charles V* in favor of the *Lullian Art*, along with other indulgences of the *Popes*, to which I refer the Reader, as they are attached everywhere to *Llull’s* works.

By such great favor of the Bulls, it happened that thereafter the *Lullian* dogmas were viewed with a slightly milder eye. Many, stimulated by a laudable curiosity while they probed the *Principles of the Art* a little more deeply, found them so far from being discordant with the truth that they instead perceived them as fertile seedbeds of all *Arts* and *Sciences*. From there, as if from a certain Trojan Horse, a huge crowd of Commentators soon emerged, although hardly anyone has been found who has been able to fully extricate himself from the hidden darkness of the art, partly because of its unavoidable difficulty, and partly because of its unpolished and obscure style of speaking.

<meta>catchword: Qua-</meta>

<summary>This preface recounts the legal history of Ramon Llull's works, detailing how initial charges of heresy by Nicholas Eymerich were eventually overturned by later Popes and the Council of Trent. It positions Llull's "Art" as a profound but difficult system of knowledge that has attracted many commentators despite its complexity.</summary>

<keywords>Ramon Llull, Nicholas Eymerich, heresy, Papal Bulls, Council of Trent, Charles V, Lullian Art, ecclesiastical censure, scholasticism</keywords>

<sig>** 2</sig>

# TO THE READER.

<margin>The Author is consulted regarding the Lullian Art.</margin>
*Because many have occupied themselves with experiments in this Art—not without a loss of time and frustrated effort—including most esteemed Princes of great authority and men illustrious in every branch of literature (among whom the most wise Emperor Ferdinand III <note>Ferdinand III (1608–1657), Holy Roman Emperor and a patron of the arts and sciences.</note> deservedly holds the first place), they have not disdained to earnestly entreat my judgment on the Lullian doctrine through these specific inquiries: Whether the said Art can provide the utility and desired fruit that Lull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher known for his "Great Art," a logical system intended to combine all knowledge through combinatorial diagrams.</note> promises in the acquisition of the sciences? Whether, if they spent some time learning it, I would judge it worth the effort? And whether another more prompt and ready method for operation could be found? To satisfy their laudable curiosity according to my modest ability, and to bring some light to an Art that was almost given up for dead, I immediately set upon Lull’s "Great Art," turning over every detail from start to finish, noting each point, combining each with the next with the highest mental reflection. But to confess the truth of the matter, I found the path so hindered by rocks, so entangled with thorns and thistles, and so rough with ruts and thickets, that I was very nearly ready to cast away both my pen and my spirit out of despair for the labor;* <margin>Difficulty in the Lullian Art.</margin> *for on one hand, the scarcity of terms reducible to principles deterred me; on the other, the exceedingly meager arrangement of an Alphabet composed of only nine letters struck me with fear of fruitless labor. Here, the truly labyrinthine entrances to the inner chambers of the Art made me wonderfully doubtful and hesitant; there, a mass of propositions involved in unusual terms, gathered together rashly without method or light, strangely tormented the impulse of my wavering mind. Nevertheless, a constant perseverance of mind—that steady companion—conquered the intricacies of this hidden study; an ardent desire for unknown learning mitigated the inexhaustible labors; and a truly burning affection, implanted in me by nature for promoting those studies nearly abandoned due to impenetrable difficulty, overcame all obstacles. Thus, through repeated reading and an intimate weighing of every detail, a higher light finally shone upon me, and I noticed what was commonly reported: that beneath the Lullian principles, as if beneath some rough Silenus <note>A reference to the "Sileni of Alcibiades," an ancient metaphor for something that appears ugly or crude on the outside but contains divine treasures within.</note>, lay hidden vast treasures of the sciences, by the weighing of which the Republic of Letters might be wonderfully enriched. Only the method and practical application seemed to lack an easy and probable adaptation of the principles to all arts and sciences, without which I found no hope remaining for learning the Art.*

<margin>What the Author has provided in this work.</margin>
*Therefore, having cast a new ground-plan <term>Ichnographia: a Greek-derived term for a floor plan or map, here meaning a structural outline of the system.</term> of the Art onto paper, I entered upon the design of a new work, in which, all difficulties and impediments having been removed, I might exhibit a clear, easy, and ready method, which this work...*

<summary>The author describes being commissioned by Emperor Ferdinand III and other scholars to evaluate Ramon Llull's complex logical system. He recounts his initial despair at the system's difficulty but claims to have discovered its hidden value and created a new, clearer method for using it.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Ferdinand III, Great Art, Lullian Art, logic, combinatorial method, pedagogy, intellectual history</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, detailing the structural plan of the work and how it improves upon the original Lullian system.</meta>

# PREFACE

*...whatever it may finally be, it encompasses. But let us unfold the plan of the work.*

<margin>The plan of the work and its arrangement. Book I: The Lullian Method. Book II: Explains the principles.</margin>
*Therefore, so that the Reader might more fully understand what Lullus <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher known for his "Great Art," a system of logic using rotating circles to combine concepts.</note> and what I myself have provided in restoring this Art, I thought it necessary to present the Lullian method immediately in the First Book. To this, I have subjoined my own method, set forth and explained in a parallel comparison within the same Book. I have changed the Lullian Alphabets <note>The sets of symbolic letters used in Llull's combinatory diagrams.</note> using newly invented characters and signs for easier progress in practicing the Art. The terms which could in any way be traced back to individual principles, I have reduced to a syllogistic form according to the usual laws of Combination; once reduced, I have established them with examples placed everywhere—the one thing that was uniquely lacking. In the Second Book, the Reader will find all the principles explained with a great variety of examples.*

<margin>Books III and IV: Teaches the Combinatory Art.</margin>
*But since I saw that the Reader could by no means reach the intended goal of reasoning about any subject without the precision of the Combinatory Art <note>The "Ars Combinatoria," a method of generating new ideas or truths by combining basic concepts.</note>, in the Third and Fourth Books I have treated extensively the method of Lullus as applied to our principles, and the laws and precepts of the Combinatory Art. There, I have also shown the way and the reason by which anyone can translate any proposed subject matter into innumerable expanded propositions and arguments; I have also constructed new Combinatory Tables to demonstrate the excellence of the Art.*

*And since I saw it was impossible for a Beginner, relying on the help of our Art, to progress with the hoped-for fruit in the application of Sciences whose principles, objects, and subjects he did not know; for this reason, I have immediately set forth a Synopsis of all Arts and Sciences in the Fifth Book. Following the footsteps of the Anatomist, I have distributed the individual Arts and Sciences into their smallest members, so that I might not seem to have left anything behind that could hinder the Reader in his affection for the Art.*

*Furthermore, since I judged it would be difficult to skillfully sketch any kind of picture and adorn it with the required differences of colors without a prototype (by the constant sight of which the Beginner might work correctly), I thought it necessary above all to describe the faculty of Theology—which holds the principal place among the sciences—using the paintbrush of our Art according to all its parts; this has been performed as extensively as possible in the Sixth Book.*

*There remained the way and method of our Art in proceeding through the rest of the Arts and Sciences, which the Seventh Book, which is entirely exemplary, demonstrates at length. Indeed, it provides such specimens in Metaphysical, Logical, Physical, Medical, Mathematical, Legal, Canonical, Ascetic, and Rhetorical questions, that they may serve as an Ariadne’s thread <note>A classical reference to the thread used to navigate the Labyrinth; here meaning a guide through complex problems.</note> for the diligent Beginner to deduce innumerable others. He will also find there...*

<meta>catchword: a new</meta>

<summary>The author outlines the seven books of his treatise, explaining how he has modernized Ramon Llull's combinatory logic by adding syllogistic forms, new symbols, and practical examples across all major academic disciplines.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Combinatory Art, syllogism, scientific classification, theology, logic, Ariadne's thread, pedagogical method</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, following the introductory remarks on the restoration of the Lullian Art. The catchword "R. P. FRAN-" likely refers to the beginning of the next section or a dedication to a Reverend Father Francis.</meta>

<sig>** 3</sig>

# TO THE READER.

...a new system and method for writing books, contained within this problem: Given any argument or subject, to distribute it according to the rules of our Art <note>The "Art" refers to the *Ars Magna*, a system of logic and memory developed by Ramon Llull</note> into sections, titles, chapters, and paragraphs so skillfully that anyone about to write may obtain an inexhaustible supply of material for writing and speaking, without the use of any other books and without any mental fatigue. Just as this matter has been handed down by no one until now, it can scarcely be expressed how much ease and utility it brings to the extemporaneous outlining of composed works. Finally, through long practice of this Art, I have uncovered many things that are utterly paradoxical and admirable beyond human belief; since I have reviewed these at the conclusion of the work, I did not wish to dwell on them longer here. Finally, regarding the practical method of applying the Art, the reader should not expect new opinions here, but rather those most well-worn in the schools and lecture halls, derived according to the rules of the Art in accordance with the mind of St. Thomas <note>St. Thomas Aquinas, the definitive Scholastic philosopher</note> and the rest of the Scholastics <term>Scholastics: Medieval academics who used a specific dialectical method of learning</term>. Furthermore, the terms which Llull uses in demonstrating his arguments—which are plainly barbaric and foreign to the ears of the Scholastics—we have more wisely converted into common terms known to everyone, lest anything should occur that might render the beginner <note>original: "Tironem"</note> averse to the practice of our Art.

You have here, then, Reader, in a few words, my plan for amending and restoring the teaching of the Lullian Art. If in this I do not seem to have satisfied the expectations of the learned, I would wish you to excuse the weakness of my talent in explaining the most difficult and abstruse of all Arts, mindful of that saying: *in great things, I will not say to have attempted, but merely to have willed is enough.* <note>A paraphrase of Propertius: "In magnis et voluisse sat est."</note> If, however, fair judges of these matters shall decide that I have brought some light to this nearly forsaken Art through my labor, they shall credit it entirely to the Father of Lights above, the source and spring of all inexhaustible Light. Farewell, Reader, and look with favor upon my endeavors.

<insert>
Z. Bauche, Philosopher.
</insert>

<summary>The author concludes his preface by explaining his method for organizing knowledge into a structured writing system and justifies his use of traditional Scholastic terminology over Llull's original "barbaric" vocabulary. He humbly submits his work to the reader, dedicating any success to God.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Thomas Aquinas, Scholasticism, writing method, logic, pedagogy, Z. Bauche</keywords>

# THE REVEREND FATHER FRANCISCO MACEDO
## Of the Order of St. Augustine, Theologian of the Province of Portugal,

->*CONCERNING THIS*<-

# JUDGMENT OF THE WORK.

> <image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'L' featuring floral scrollwork within a square border.</image-desc>The book which the Reverend Father Athanasius Kircher of the Society of Jesus <note>The Jesuits</note> has entitled *The Great Art of Knowledge*, I have both read attentively and examined with diligence. I have found nothing in it to cause offense; rather, I found everything explained within such a massive volume—such is the power of his method. Indeed, in reading it, I have found a most suitable thread for navigating and escaping that "Labyrinth" of the Lullian Art <note>referring to the complex combinatorial logic system of Ramon Llull, a 13th-century philosopher</note>, the difficulties of which have until now terrified minds from even entering. It is the offspring of a great genius, conceived in that most capacious mind of Athanasius’s learning, and it is worthy for the Society—that Great Mother of Authors—to receive and protect among the beautiful and marvelous offspring she is accustomed to produce. If my recommendation of this work can be of use to anyone: I perceive in it an ingenious invention, a fit arrangement, an easy method, distinguished erudition, and appropriate expression. I admire the information on many and great matters, the knowledge of almost all disciplines handed down by precepts, the innumerable arguments for proving individual positions, the suitable examples for illustrating the same, and the wonderful harmony amidst such variety. And so I have determined for myself: that while the author has gathered much fame from the many other works he has published, he shall gather the greatest glory from this one—glory which will undoubtedly redound upon the Society, the parent to whom he owes such great progress. This I confirm: that this work, if read diligently and examined accurately, will detract much from the arrogance of the Heterodox <note>those holding non-Catholic or heretical beliefs</note> and those men who seem to themselves to know and be too wise; but to the Orthodox, who are equally strong in wisdom and modesty, it will bring no small benefit. Let this be my judgment of the work—I who, though I may judge less than perfectly, am certainly accustomed to judge freely. At Rome, in the College for the Propagation of the Faith. February 24th, in the year 1661.

->**FRIAR FRANCISCO of St. Augustine Macedo,** *Professor of Theology and Retired Lecturer*<-
->*in the College for the Propagation of the Faith; Lecturer of Controversies and of Sacred*<-
->*History in the Academy of the Soul of the City <note>The University of Rome, "La Sapienza"</note>, and Qualifier of the Holy Office <note>An official who assessed the orthodoxy of books or propositions for the Inquisition</note>.*<-

<summary>A formal testimonial and "judgment" by the Portuguese theologian Francisco Macedo praising Athanasius Kircher's "Great Art of Knowledge" for its clarity and its ability to simplify the complex logical systems of Ramon Llull. Macedo emphasizes the work's utility for the Catholic Church and its potential to humble intellectual opponents while educating the faithful.</summary>

# THE JUDGMENT
## OF THE REVEREND FATHER THOMAS DE LEON,
### Professor of Sacred Theology in the Academy of Granada,
### CONCERNING THE
# GREAT ART.

<margin>Reverend Father</margin>
<image-desc>Large woodcut ornamental initial 'A' decorated with intricate vine and leaf patterns in a square frame.</image-desc> I have read, with all the diligence I could muster, the book on the *Combinatoric Art* <note>original: "Ars Combinatoria," Kircher's system for organizing all knowledge through mathematical combinations</note> by Athanasius Kircher of *our Society* <note>The Society of Jesus, or the Jesuits</note>, who is sufficiently well known throughout all lands for his exceptional reputation for teaching and recondite <note>hidden or obscure</note> erudition. Setting aside the right of friendship in which I glory, and putting away the illustrious reputation the author holds among all the learned—since these things might prejudice a censorship of the work—I first recognized the design and goal of the work: it is to deliver a *universal category and a catholic method* <note>In this context, "catholic" means universal or all-encompassing</note> for all disciplines; a thing attempted by many in the past and present, and desired by all the learned. *Aristotle’s system of categories* has displeased many great minds, so much so that they thought it unworthy of such a great Philosopher. And following *St. Thomas Aquinas*, who judged it so, slighting remarks are commonly brought forth concerning it. 

In previous centuries, the much-celebrated *Art of Lull* <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher who created a logical system to prove Christian truths through mechanical combinations of concepts</note> was introduced; it was born in a barbaric age but conceived with a good mind, and for that reason it gained a wonderful applause among some, while among others it met with a wonderful and indignant contempt because of its rough outer shell of words and the fact that such great effort yielded no rich harvest. Nevertheless, the study of it has been maintained in several Universities. In the first part of this book, that Art is drawn out from its own night into such open light that it can no longer be judged a fugitive from the day or an enemy of the light. Nothing more brilliant could have been expected in this area from any man, however great. But only he who compares these writings with the *Tarragonian Commentator on Raymond* <note>Referring to commentators on Ramon Llull</note> and the *Majorcans* will be able to judge how much difference there is between light and darkness. *Petrus Gregorius* <note>Pierre Grégoire (1540–1597), a jurist who wrote on the organization of knowledge</note> is much more learned than they, but he only cultivates the first face and appearance of the doctrine and does not penetrate into its depths; and since he takes everything from Lull, it was out of envy that he did not even once mention the man through whom he made progress. 

Finally, our Athanasius, through Combination, analysis, and analogy, makes the method rich and more wealthy with a wonderful supplement, and unless I am mistaken, he surpasses by a long interval those who are thought to have achieved great things in this field. The *Use of the Art*—which is lacking in the brief but accurate exposition of *Augustin Nuñez Delpadillo*, in the diffused digest of *Ivo of Paris*, and others—he explains clearly even to the unrefined. Therefore, I judge the book most worthy to be printed publicly for the common utility, since it is in no way discordant with good morals or the Catholic faith. Thus I judged. At Rome, in the Professed House of the Society of JESUS. The 18th day of September, the year 1661.

->THOMAS DE LEON.<-

<meta>This is the opening page of the work, establishing the foundational definitions and the author's intent to refine the Lullian method.</meta>

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,
## OR THE COMBINATORY ART,
### THE FIRST INTRODUCTORY BOOK,

->IN WHICH<-

*The Great Art of Lull <note>referring to Ramon Llull, a 13th-century philosopher who created a system of logic using rotating circles</note> is examined, the defects committed by him are pointed out, a new structure of the Great Art is set forth, the elements and principles are declared, the canons and precepts are prescribed, and finally the method and reasoning of the entire Art is placed before the eyes of the curious reader through various paradigms and specimens.*

### PART I.
## Concerning the method, definition, discovery, and propagation of the Great Art, or the Combinatory Art.

### CHAPTER I.
->*What the Great Art of Knowledge, or the Combinatory Art, may be.*<-

<margin>The excellence of the Art.</margin>

<image-desc>Large ornamental drop-cap letter 'A' featuring intricate floral and vine patterns within a square border.</image-desc>

As we begin this Great Art, or Combinatory Art, under the happy guidance of the Divine Will; it is, I say, the art of arts, the workshop of the sciences, the fertile nursery of minds, and the key to all human cognition, through which the widest access lies open to all things that pertain in any way to the notice and knowledge of the intellect; hence we have deemed that it should be addressed by no better name than that of the "Great Art." We entitle it the "Combinatory Art" because, by the shrewd scrutiny of the mind, it aptly compares all things to each, and each to all, beautifully composes them, and exactly distributes them into a most perfect edifice of the human mind. This is the <term>analytical art <note>original Greek: τέχνη ἀναλυτικὴ</note></term> of the noble mathematician Diophantus, by which, from a few principles known by the light of nature, he opens any of the deepest mysteries of Arithmetic and Geometry with such mental fecundity as his monuments demonstrate to us sufficiently and more. Our method, however, is not only easy, expeditious, and obvious; but it is also universal, which does not pride itself on the effort of a single art, but through an ingenious weaving of combinations, it seems to contemplate a most close marriage with the entire <term>Encyclopedia <note>the "circle of learning" or the complete system of knowledge</term>, by a truly admirable device—namely, through the doctrine of methodical ranks and circles fabricated according to the pattern of nature. By the aid of these, not only is there a method for discovering and handing down <term>Polypædia <note>universal learning or "many-fold" education</note></term>...

<summary>This introductory page defines the "Great Art" as a universal system of logic and combination intended to organize all human knowledge. The author acknowledges the influence of Ramon Llull and Diophantus while promising a more refined and comprehensive method using symbolic circles.</summary>
<keywords>Great Art, Combinatory Art, Ramon Llull, Diophantus, universal knowledge, logic, Encyclopedia, analytical art</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory framing of the "Great Art of Knowing," likely following a dedication or general preface. The author is establishing the philosophical foundations of his universal method.</meta>

# OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING,

Through this universal method, not only are any arts or sciences compared, but all the reasons of the most hidden things—taking their beginnings from nature—can be taught and investigated through various ascents, descents, and circuits of the mind. I did not think it worthwhile to display the full glory of this art immediately from the very entrance <note>original: "veſtibulo," referring to the porch or entry of a temple</note>, both because no one can easily survey its extent and widely stretched dominion unless they have entered more deeply or directed their mind to its very inner sanctum; and because its brilliance and unspeakable sharpness so strike every nerve of the soul, and so exceed all faculty, strength, and abundance of speech, that there is no flow of genius so fertile, no oration so studiously crafted, that could grasp the appearance or magnitude of such true praise. Hence it is better not to pursue it with far-fetched praises, but rather, as was customary in the Egyptian mysteries, to venerate it with silence alone, like a Deity <note>original: "Numinis inſtar"</note>, being content for now with a rough image of its use, until this Silenus <note>A reference to the "Silenus boxes" mentioned by Erasmus and Plato—plain on the outside but containing images of gods within.</note> opens himself entirely and bathes all things in a sudden ray of divinity. 

But, to return to our proposed purpose: I would have you know, Reader, that the logic of this entire invention turns as if on two Hinges. The first is the **Combination of principles** among themselves, which is done through **Synthesis** (which they call composition); this is nothing other than the reduction of many principles and predicates into a unity, or the diffusion from unity into a multitude of things, much like a spring diverted into innumerable streams, which we call **Analysis** or resolution. The second Hinge is the **Analogical Art**, by which—through various shadows of similarities—we discover the smallest things in the greatest, the middle in the lowest and highest, and the highest in the middle and lowest; in a word: we perceive that all things are in all things in their own way. Thus, there is nothing in the universal nature of things that does not correspond to all and each under a certain proportion and analogy. This follows such an order and progress that he who has mastered it exactly will find that nothing is denied to him in penetrating the inner depths of the <term>encyclopedia</term> <note>In the 17th-century sense: the "circle of learning" or the interconnectedness of all knowledge.</term> of arts and sciences, insofar as our rules allow. 

Carried aloft into the deep sea of contemplation by these two wings of the Combinatory and Analogical art—in a carriage far more sublime than that which they say Triptolemus <note>A figure from Greek myth who traveled the world in a winged chariot to spread the knowledge of agriculture.</note> once used—and observing the images and similarities of individual things, and the most firm laws of their agreements and disagreements, I saw that they were governed simultaneously by unity and multitude. I saw them pregnant with the **Sympathy** of acting and passive things, and quickly dissolved by the **Antipathy** of causes, bringing about the change of death. I saw also that the reasons for particular things lie hidden, expressed under the "bark" or outer shell of certain general principles; once the distinction of these principles is known, the distinction of particulars is no longer hidden. Thus, it became immediately known to me that the constitution of any art arises from the difference of its own specific traits from common ones. For where I first understood the commonality of many things, I thought I should not stop until I had unearthed all the differences in those things enclosed within species. I found by experience that the many shadowy images of dissimilarities in the multitude of things could not be brought into view and open light unless all things that enjoy a certain cognition were first enclosed within one cognition and property, clothed in the essence of a single genus. 

And this is our **Combinatory Art**, which we promise to the World in this work; which through nine <term>Erotematic</term> <note>From the Greek "erotema," meaning "question." These are the nine fundamental questions (Who? What? Where? etc.) used in the Lullian method.</note> propositions, eighteen principles (both absolute and relative), along with nine subjects encompassing the scope of all nature, we compose and arrange by such a method that from there, a knowledge of irrefragable truths may be obtained within the far and wide-stretched boundaries of the entire Encyclopedia. All of this will be made more clear in what follows.

---

## CHAPTER II.

->Whether a general Art can be given for the easy acquisition of all sciences.<-

<margin>Whether a universal science exists.</margin>
Since, according to the testimony of Plato, *nothing is sweeter than to know all things* <note>original Greek: "μηδὲν γλύκιον ἢ πάντα εἰδέναι"</note>, and little sparks of wanting to know are implanted in man by Nature; certainly, power would be given in vain unless it were reduced into action. Unless, I say, the intellect—variously cultivated from its potential by use and habit—should in the course of time know all things (that is, reach as far as possible a suitable knowledge of all things), I do not see how this would be contrary to the human condition, or why it should seem denied to the intellect, provided we do not transcend the limits of human knowledge. Who does not see that in all things one "genus" is always found, in which all species participate and agree? Whence, if the genus is fully known, the notion of the species cannot remain entirely hidden; just as the partitioning of rivers led into various branches of streams—

<meta>catchword: -ions</meta>

<summary>The author introduces the two pillars of his universal method: the Combination of principles (Synthesis and Analysis) and the Analogical Art (finding the macrocosm in the microcosm). He argues that a universal science is possible because all specific knowledge is contained within general principles.</summary>
<keywords>Universal Art, Combinatory Art, Analogy, Synthesis, Analysis, Sympathy and Antipathy, Encyclopedia of Knowledge, Plato, Triptolemus, General Principles</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory discourse on the "Combinatoric Art," moving from the philosophical justification of a universal method to a historical survey of its practitioners.</meta>

# or of the COMBINATORIC ART, BOOK I.

The sources can be easily recognized if we follow the stream from the spring to the branching channels of the section. Who will consider it absurd for diverse works of diverse arts to be perfected by a single instrument? Do we not reach a knowledge of innumerable mixtures from the single nature of the elements? Who does not know that through the single efficacy of vegetative nature, countless species of plants and trees subsist? Who is ignorant that by the single aid of the senses, the cognition of countless things is prepared for us, and by a single operation of the mind, the way lies open to us for the secret mysteries of arts and inventions? Do we not observe that in man, as a single subject, corporeal and incorporeal things harmonize? Is it not established that all things which shine in the majesty of nature—though separated by such great differences of powers and properties—are nevertheless bound by a wondrous concord and are held and sustained within the single circuit and influence of the world? Indeed, all these things, created by the single intellect of the Supreme Mind, are governed and preserved. Justice embraces the whole harmony of virtues; happiness, the influence of all goods; finally, in the one person <note>original: "hypostasi"</note> of Christ, divine and human nature agree. Since, therefore, common principles contain within themselves the reasons for particular things, who will doubt—unless he be irrational <note>original: "ἄλογος" (alogos)</note>—that the mind of man, by the guidance of reason, can extend itself to the knowledge of all the aforementioned things? This is done all the more easily as the method of teaching it is more expedited and the path for proceeding is smoother. Hence, whoever knows how many are in one, and one in many, will have the business finished. Many have indeed attempted this Art, but it has been granted to few "to reach Corinth" <note>A classical proverb meaning to achieve a difficult goal.</note>. Some hoped it could be achieved through Dialectic or Logic, as if through the most fitting instrument of knowing. Others through various arrangements <note>original: "Syntaxes"</note> of arts and sciences; some through methodical frameworks of Metaphysical speculations. There were even those who thought it could be accomplished under allegorical circles of the mind, fashioned after the likeness of "all-forming" nature. And although all these men, in a most praiseworthy effort, approached this second art of scientific unity, few executed it with a method that could either satisfy thirsty wits or possess ease joined with readiness. But so that all these things may be clear, let it be so.

---

### CHAPTER III.

->Concerning the illustrious men who professed the art of disputing about all things.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'V' at the start of Chapter III, containing a scene with human figures in a classical or mythological landscape with architecture in the distance.</image-desc>
Just as the human intellect, driven by the desire for knowing, leaves no stone unturned so that it might arrive at an extraordinary knowledge of all things by a short path; so there have not been lacking, in past ages, men illustrious in learning and wisdom who, by continuous labor and assiduous meditation of the mind, finally reached that art by which they could give an account of all proposed questions, however difficult. And in Academic discussions, they promised that they would discourse according to the rule of the art they had invented on whatever subject anyone wished to hear. Quintilian reports that some of these emerged from the ancient school and that of his own time, among whom Cicero numbers Corax, Lysias the Sicilian, Gorgias of Leontini (the rival of Socrates), Thrasymachus of Chalcedon, Prodicus of Chios, and Protagoras of Abdera, from whom Enathlus is said to have learned the art which Hippias of Elis sold for ten thousand denarii. Valerius Maximus reports that for Gorgias, because of his remarkable faculty for speaking on all subjects, the first statue of solid gold was placed in the temple of Delphic Apollo. In what the Gorgian Art actually consisted, Cicero hints when he expresses his marvelous skill in speaking and demonstrates that a wealth of facts and sciences was available to him; for he says that he brought forth "like joined to like" and "similarly distinguished"; likewise "contraries related to contraries," which for the most part would fall rhythmically of their own accord, even if one were not trying. Truly, as all these things have been buried by the forgetfulness of time, they have left us no memory of how to acquire it or of the established method.

<margin>Who cultivated this art.</margin>
Nevertheless, in later ages, there were men conspicuous in every kind of science who cultivated this art with the greatest zeal. Among these, Raymond Lull deservedly holds the first place; some call him, because of the excellent light of genius in which he excelled, the "God-taught" <note>original: "θεοδίδακτον" (theodidaktos)</note> or Divinely Illuminated Doctor.

<margin>Lull.</margin>
He composed a work in a style that was indeed unpolished, even crude and unrefined, but of a subtlety not to be despised. This work some call the *Great Art*, others the *Gateway of Arts and Sciences*; some the *Great Lullian Art*; others the *Inventive Art of Truth* (the name bestowed by the Author), in which, by a new method devised by himself, he opened the approach to all science even for the unlearned, and even for those of an age below...

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: puberty</meta>

<summary>This page argues for the possibility of a universal method of knowledge based on the unity of nature and God, then begins a historical survey of figures like Gorgias and Raymond Lull who claimed to possess such a "Great Art."</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's historical survey of the "Great Art of Knowledge" (Ars Magna Sciendi), specifically focusing on the successors and commentators of Ramon Llull.</meta>

# OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,

prepared for the youth; regarding which, just as Lupetus, Lavineta, **Cornelius Agrippa**, **Jordanus** <note>Giordano Bruno</note> and others have published vast commentaries, so I have judged it superfluous to describe it more extensively in this place, since I shall bring its principles back to the anvil <note>A metaphor for refining or re-examining</note> in the following chapter, where I will demonstrate what should be thought of it.

<margin>Gregory of Toulouse.</margin>
<margin>Syntaxes of the Wonderful Art.</margin>
Following the method of this art with equal pace, **Pierre Grégoire of Toulouse**, a noble writer and public Professor of Civil Law in the Academy of Cahors, composed a work boasting this title: *Syntaxes of the Wonderful Art*, through which one can dispute or treat any proposed subject by many and nearly infinite reasons, and a summary knowledge of all things can be obtained. It is indeed a splendid work, filled with an innumerable variety of things and sciences; but just as he did not hand down how one might reach the acquisition of those sciences—congested from everywhere—by a light, brief, and ready method through Lullian principles, so he left in the minds of the curious nothing but a most burning desire and an inflamed thirst for the art he so greatly praised.

<margin>Cornelius Gemma.</margin>
<margin>The Cyclognomic Art.</margin>
Contemporary to him, **Cornelius Gemma**, Royal Professor of Medicine in the Academy of Leuven and a man most learned in polite literature as well as the physiological art, exhibited to the world the *Cyclognomic Art*. In this, by a method entirely different from the Lullian one, through the principles of the analytic and synthetic arts, he promises to exhibit a universal reason for disputing about all things through the concatenated links of intellectual cycles; in a word, he promises a knowledge of all sciences. He proposes this in an elegant, flowery style, swollen with a variety of words and sciences, not without commendation for his genius. But as he nearly overwhelms the reader with the multitude and abundance of things to be said, and being carried away by the boldness of a luxuriant style, he creates no small amount of trouble even for men not of the lowest class of intellects to fully understand the intended method and its application, which leads to obtaining the desired knowledge of the sciences. Approaching most closely to the inner shrines of the Lullian art was

<margin>Pedro Sanchez.</margin>
**Pedro Hieronymo Sanchez**, in the book which he inscribed *The Miraculous and Easy Method for Easily Acquiring All Sciences*, and in his commentaries on the *Short Art* <note>Llull's *Ars Brevis*</note> which were indeed aptly composed; him we have followed in many things.

<margin>Yves the Capuchin.</margin>
These were followed by **Yves the Capuchin** of Paris, a man no less celebrated for his religion than for his all-encompassing learning; who in years past offered to the literary world a new work which he calls the *Digest of Wisdom* (*Digestum Sapientiæ*), composed with truly the highest and most relentless labor in two huge volumes. And although I do not deny that the work is filled with immense erudition and swollen with the embrace of all sciences—inasmuch as both preachers of the Divine Word and candidates of the faculty of Oratory may find there a most fertile "Horn of Plenty" (or better said, a new *Polyanthea* <note>A common title for general reference encyclopedias or florilegia</note>) prepared for the amplification and decoration of assumed arguments—nevertheless, in that truly laudable undertaking, a method easier and more ready than the opinion of all for achieving what is intended seems to be lacking. I do not deny that in the said work he reduced the entire series of things to the eighteen Lullian principles (partly absolute, partly relative); but, I know not by what motive he was driven to omit what has been uniquely sought until now. While the desired application for obtaining the goal was neglected, or at least only meagerly indicated, and the rules of the art touched only with the "tips of the fingers" <note>Superficially</note>, he translated those eighteen principles into a promiscuous variety of things by an application more difficult than is fair—especially for those whose minds burn to attain the genuine art through a brief, ready, and prepared reason. For as he mixed Theological matters with Canonical, Medical with Physical, Physical with Mathematical, Metaphysical with Ethical, and Political with Historical, mixing Sacred with Profane in no order, he could not avoid a Chaos of things; and nothing can be more contrary to this art than that. For since the scope of the Lullian Art is, given any subject or any proposed question drawn from the Encyclopedia of arts, to dispute about it amply and fruitfully, and to demonstrate the particular principles of the sciences from universal principles, I do not see how this can be performed—except with great labor and by a man already advanced in the sciences—through the method described in the *Digest of Wisdom*. Yet I add this to the commendation of that distinguished work: if someone were to reduce all things heaped up confusedly and promiscuously in the *Digest of Wisdom* into particular classes of sciences, he would reach the intended goal of the art nearly and safely by that aid. Whence I also confess that a work of this kind was of no small benefit to the perfection of the art, as I shall show a little later, after I have first opened my own judgment concerning the Lullian Art and what is desired in it. Succeeding in this last place was

<margin>Fr. Sebastian Izquierdo.</margin>
The Reverend Father **Sebastian Izquierdo**, currently the most worthy Assistant of the Society of Jesus in Spain, who published for the good of the literary Republic a huge volume which he entitles the *Pharus Scientiarum* (The Lighthouse of the Sciences), in which, although by a method for the arts and scien-

<summary>The author reviews several historical attempts to refine or expand upon Ramon Llull's universal art of knowledge, critiquing various scholars for their overly complex or disorganized methods. He highlights the need for a more accessible and systematic approach to mastering all branches of learning.</summary>

<keywords>Ramon Llull, Ars Magna, Cornelius Agrippa, Pierre Grégoire, Cornelius Gemma, Pedro Sanchez, Yves of Paris, Sebastian Izquierdo, universal knowledge, pedagogy, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Art of Combination, specifically evaluating the legacy of Ramon Llull (Raymundus Lullus) and his "Great Art."</meta>

# Or of COMBINATORICS, BOOK I.

...uses a concise acquisition of the sciences that differs from the Lullian method. Nevertheless, it is an admirable work, worthy of all praise, and a true image of the human intellect. However, just as it searches <term>untraceably<note>original: ἀνεξερεύνως (anexereunōs). Meaning in a way that cannot be tracked or scrutinized.</note></term> for hidden paths toward the acquisition of knowledge through the metaphysical and abstract recesses of intellectual combinations, so too is it not granted to everyone to "reach this Corinth," <note>A classical proverb meaning a difficult achievement not everyone can manage.</note> unless they have been equipped with a distinguished <term>full suit of armor<note>original: πανoπλίᾳ (panoplia). Referring to a complete set of intellectual tools or weapons.</note></term> of metaphysics and abstract cognitions. Therefore, I trust that some man of Herculean genius will eventually emerge to convert the hidden treasures lying beneath it to practical use and adapt them to the capacity of even mediocre minds.

## CHAPTER IV.

->What the Lullian Art is, in what it chiefly consists; and what the literary world finds lacking in it.<-

<margin>In what the Lullian art consists.</margin>
The goal of the Lullian Art is to teach a method through which a great abundance of attributes, propositions, questions, and arguments may be supplied to those using it. It differs from Dialectic <note>The formal logic of the schools.</note> because the latter completes the entire scope of Aristotelian Dialectic through a great diversity of topics across four types of problems: namely, the problems of definition, property, genus, and accident. It differs from the Art of Invention which *Aristotle proposes in Book 1 of the Topics*, because there Aristotle looks only toward the invention of Syllogisms and refers everything to three general places: that is, to antecedents, consequents, and repugnancies—which are terms of "second intention" regarding their naming, as they say in the Schools. <note>Technical scholastic terms referring to concepts that point to other concepts rather than physical things.</note> But the Lullian Art, besides teaching the invention of syllogisms, also supplies a great abundance and variety of places for other Orations, narrations, accusations, and indeed every kind of attribute. It differs from Rhetoric because the latter is concerned with three types of cases: demonstrative, deliberative, and judicial; this Art, however, is bound to no single type, pertains to any being whatsoever, and enfolds all the principles of the sciences.

Since, therefore, for a long time now various men—not only those skilled in Philosophy but also Princes—struck by a certain curiosity, have sought my judgment on the Lullian art, I have attempted to satisfy their laudable curiosity in every way. I have approached Lullus’s book on the *Great Art*, bringing all the principles of the Art back to the anvil <note>A metaphor for rigorous testing and reshaping.</note> to be discussed with singular zeal and the highest mental scrutiny. I have also tried to examine my own long-conceived principles of the art against them, as if against a touchstone. I found at last that the rumors spread about it were true: its assumed principles, through the precision of the Combinatoric Art, can be applied to all arts and sciences, so that from there, that which has been so greatly sought by everyone may finally emerge to the significant benefit of the learned. Many things, however, were detected in it which, unless corrected and handed down with a more exact method, can be of hardly any use. The first of these is a style full of barbarisms, retaining hardly any traces of proper Latinity, joined with an extreme obscurity which could deter an Artist <note>A practitioner of the Art.</note> from reading it at first sight. The second is that he reduces everything to the first nine characters of the Alphabet as signs for the subjects assumed—excepting the first letter A, which is the mark of God, the Best and Greatest. The remaining letters in order are applied first to nine absolute attributes, then to as many relative ones. To these same letters, he then attributes the circumstances of nine questions to be made, and again to the same, the first nine subjects of all Nature. Thus, the letter **B.** corresponds: first, to the question "Whether?"; second, to the principle of essential goodness; third, to the subject, which is God; fourth, to the relative principle of difference with all those predicates which can be reduced in any way to goodness, difference, or the nature of the assumed subject; fifth, to the Virtues; sixth, to the opposite Vices. This system is indeed entangled in so many difficulties and involved in such intricacies that one would need a divine memory and imagination to properly remember every single thing pertaining to each letter. And so, I have learned by my own experience that it is impossible for anyone—with so many mental reflections and such a hodgepodge of predicates and subjects—to acquire the readiness to dispute exactly about any matter unless they are equipped with other aids. For the mind usually gets stuck in choosing the means to demonstrate something, stumbles in selecting them, wavers, and vacillates; the Artist does not know how or by what reasons the proposed question should be met—difficulties which are entangled all the more the more unrefined one is in Theoretical and Scholastic studies.

<header>OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING,</header>

and more inexperienced; and so it becomes impossible <note>original: "ἀδύνατον" (adynaton)</note> for anyone to perform anything worthy of praise in any faculty, unless they first possess an excellent mastery of the science they are treating, and are not ignorant of the instruction of Logic <note>original: "Dialecticæ"</note>. For how someone ignorant of any discipline, with only this meager apparatus of principles, could find the appropriate means—fitted to that which is proposed to be demonstrated in that science—by his own "warfare" <note>A Latin idiom, "proprio marte," meaning by one's own efforts or courage.</note> as the saying goes, I do not understand. But so that we may look more closely at the proposed design of the Lullian Art, I place the goal of the entire matter before the eyes of the Curious Reader in a few words.

# PART II.
## A brief exposition of the Principles of the Lullian Art.
### CHAPTER I.
*Concerning the Theoretical elements of the Art, and the Alphabet, which is also called the Lexicon.*

<image-desc>Decorative initial letter 'V' (integrated with 'T' to form 'UT') featuring floral scrollwork and botanical motifs.</image-desc>

SO THAT the difference between the Art which we use in the doctrine of Combined sciences, and the Art which Llull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher known for his logical system of symbols intended to prove Christian truths.</note> hands down, may be more clearly revealed to the Reader; I have thought it best to set forth here in the first place the sequence and elements of the entire Lullian Art, together with its rules; for once these are brought into the open, I have no doubt the Reader will find that what I have said is true. The commentators cited above, like apes—the true imitators of Llull—seem to have fabricated many things to attest to the dignity and excellence of the Art, rather than handing it down as it truly is, in order to extol the Art above all the heavens. Perhaps in this they followed the Alchemists and Astrologers; for what do the former not devise to preach the excellence of their "never-before-seen stone" <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note>, or the latter to persuade men of simpler wit of the ineffable influxes of the powers of Astrology—the mistress, as they say, of divine and human affairs? What occurrences do they not fabricate? Just so, the Lullists report wonderful and utterly paradoxical cases concerning this Art, about which there is nevertheless a great silence among Historians, even though they ought not to be ignorant of the invention of such a memorable Art shown to the World. Indeed, they are not ashamed to say that youths, relying on this art, attained knowledge of all things, discoursed skillfully and elegantly on any proposed subject, and even turned out to be most learned Men. By youths, I mean boys who do not exceed the age of twelve or fourteen years, who it is well known are most unfit for the operations of a more abstract intellect (which suit a practitioner of this Art), nor do they possess sufficient judgment to handle metaphysical concepts and similar arguments of more subtle matter; unless perhaps they say that, in the manner of magpies and parrots, they blurted everything out without judgment rather than discoursing with true reason, which I will easily grant them. For if I, who have studied the Lullian Art by profession and have exactly perceived all the secrets of the art and the manner of proceeding in it, nevertheless find huge difficulties in reducing it to practice—notwithstanding that I have been accustomed to the proper handling of literary studies from my "tender nails" <note>A Latin idiom, "a teneris unguiculis," meaning from earliest childhood.</note> and have, by God's gift, attained no small apparatus of the sciences (let boasting be absent from my words)—nevertheless, in the application of the Lullian Art, I find myself everywhere still perplexed, not knowing so much what to say and feel, as how I ought to bring forth the concepts of my mind into the open through the application of said art. What should be thought of boys instructed by no use or exercise of things as yet, I leave to be estimated by the Reader who possesses judgment.

After the boys of youthful age, they mention Petrus Daguinus Mediatus and Jacobus Januarius, men who, according to the testimony of Agrippa <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous polymath and occult writer.</note>, were most celebrated throughout all Italy. The first of these, when at 37 years of age he had scarcely tasted the first elements of letters, by applying himself to this artifice for only six months, was held as a miracle by all the most learned Men. The other, however, when he had completed thirty-five years <note>original: "septem lustra" (seven periods of five years).</note> utterly estranged from letters, profited so much by this art that he was to be placed behind none of the learned men. It is a marvel that a man skilled in neither Grammar, much less Logic, Physics, Metaphysics, Mathematics, or Theology, could have attained the perfection of all these sciences even unto miracles, by the help of this art, in the space of only a few months. Let him believe it who will; he who knows with how much labor...

He specifically doubts stories of uneducated adults and young children mastering all sciences in a few months using Llull's system.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Art of Lull (Ars Lulliana), moving from a critique of its detractors and legendary practitioners into the formal definitions of its mechanics.</meta>

# ON THE COMBINATORY ART, BOOK I.

hardly can we claim perfect possession of even a single science through the labor of a whole lifetime, he will find these claims to be mere fictions and nonsense—unless perhaps they imply an infused knowledge <note>Knowledge granted directly by God rather than learned through study.</note>, of which we have no evidence.

They add to these claims that there were distinguished men relying on this same art, such as Ferdinand of Cordoba, Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples, Charles de Bovelles, and Peter and Jacob, surnamed Canter, German Frisians—who as ten-year-old children, along with their only sister, were eloquent in every branch of learning. Just as we have no evidence regarding them, nor does any spark of this Lullian Art or such a method shine forth in the books of Peter d'Étaples or Charles de Bovelles, so these stories should rightly be numbered among the fictions mentioned above. I am not unaware that Peter d'Étaples, Bovelles, and similar men were indeed conspicuous for their all-encompassing erudition, but this was before they were admitted to the secrets of this art, by which they were later greatly aided to philosophize more successfully and fruitfully. I also know that a boy can be so instructed within a few days from the principles of this assumed art that he may respond somewhat on a proposed theme; but that he should respond "to the point" <note>original: "ad Rhombum" - a technical term meaning precisely or exactly.</note> regarding the most difficult questions, whether Theological, Philosophical, or Mathematical, and draw difficulties out of darkness into light through a continuous sequence of syllogisms without hesitation: *here is Rhodes, here is the jump.* <note>A Latin proverb meaning "here is the proof; do it now."</note> But that all these things are as I have said will become more than clear from the hidden constitution of the art, which we now begin.

->**The Lullian Art is twofold: Theoretical and Practical.**<-
<margin>
The Lullian Art is twofold. Theoretical & Practical.
</margin>

The Lullian Art is therefore twofold, *Theoretical* and *Practical*. The former considers the Alphabet, or the first elements of the Art; the latter reduces them to practice through a manifold tradition of rules, using the intellect to apply the theory of the elements.

<margin>
What the Alphabet of this art is.
</margin>

The Alphabet is a collection of several and diverse terms of this art into one, in which any letter has various meanings represented under its single name, and contains within itself nine signifying letters, namely B C D E F G H I K. Their meanings exist in manifold difference; for some are absolute principles, such as Goodness, Greatness, Duration, Power, Knowledge, Will, Virtue, Truth, Glory. Others are relative, such as Difference, Concordance, Contrariety, Beginning, Middle, End, Majority, Equality, Minority.

<margin>
Principles.
</margin>

All of these are primitive principles, because each one is in itself that which is supreme in its kind—indeed, the principle of all other beings considered under such a state of being. I also confess they are true, real, and necessary, because they are predicated truly, really, and necessarily of any being; that is, they are such in themselves without any intervening operation of the intellect. When they are established, whatever is established exists; and when they are removed, all things are removed. They are most general and transcendental, because they deal with all things, and in them everything that exists is comprehended. They are called "Absolute" because they imply no relation; for Goodness is only goodness, etc. But the "Respective" <note>Relative</note> principles argue for plurality and multitude, from which a mutual relation arises toward those things that differ.

<margin>
The meanings of the Questions.
</margin>

There are also meanings in this art called questions or rules, which are certain circumstances of things to be examined, such as: Whether, What, Why, How, From what, How much, Of what kind, Where, When, With what; by which the intellect is directed to respond aptly and in the proper manner.

<margin>
Subjects of attribution.
</margin>

In the Art, nine subjects of attribution are likewise considered, to which all things occurring in the breadth of beings can be reduced; such as God, Angel, Heaven, Man, Imaginative, Sensitive, Vegetative, Elementative, Instrumentative; for there is no being in the nature of things that cannot be referred back to one of these.

<margin>
Meanings of virtues and vices.
</margin>

There are also other meanings in this art: Virtues, such as Justice, Prudence, Fortitude, Temperance, Faith, Hope, Charity, Patience, Piety; along with as many sins or vices, such as Greed, Gluttony, Lust, Pride, Sloth, Envy, Wrath, Lying, Inconstancy. All of these are treated explicitly in the Art, where their opposites are also discussed. Since, according to the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note>, the logic and discipline of opposites are the same. Implicitly, however, it treats those things which can in any way be reduced to them. The Alphabet explained above is subjoined in the order and manner that follows.

---

It is necessary to have this Table impressed upon the memory so that one can discern its parts with readiness and without any hesitation, together with everything that can be referred back to these terms. Once these are mastered, one proceeds to the practical Figures of the Art; the first of which is called Figure A, and it indicates primarily God and His attributes; it is that which follows.

<note>Figure A is the primary "Divine" circle in Llull's system. It represents the "Dignities" or attributes of God, showing how they are all interconnected and interchangeable.</note>

<image-desc>A circular diagram labeled "A" in the center. The outer rim is divided into nine sections, each containing a letter from B to K and two corresponding English terms (one abstract, one concrete):
- B: Goodness / The Good
- C: Greatness / The Great
- D: Duration / The Enduring
- E: Power / The Powerful
- F: Wisdom / The Wise
- G: Will / The Willing
- H: Virtue / The Virtuous
- I: Truth / The True
- K: Glory / The Glorious
Inside the circle, every point on the circumference is connected to every other point by straight lines, forming a complex geometric web of intersections.
</image-desc>

The Figure is divided into nine parts, in each of which one of the aforementioned letters of the Alphabet is placed. Then, in a second circle, the meanings of the letters are placed—that is, the nine essential principles, both concrete and abstract. From the division points of each of these, lines are drawn to the other points of the divisions placed in order. A manifold construction of triangles emerges according to the laws of combination, by which it is suggested that any principle assumed in Divine matters can be predicated of any other in order. This will be declared more fully later in the practical application. And just as these absolute principles shine with the exaltation of a certain transcendental excellence, so they are always interchangeable among themselves, so much so that the subject becomes the predicate, and conversely the predicate becomes the subject. This occurs through a certain cyclic demonstration using <term>convertible terms</term> <note>Terms that can be swapped in a logical proposition without changing its truth value, e.g., "God is Good" and "The Good is God."</note>, as I have said. By their help, Llull endeavors to show not only the unity and Trinity of the Divine essence, but also the other articles of faith. Hence, not without reason did he wish this first Figure to be circular: both to fashion an archetype of the super-mundane sphere bounded by no limits, and to skillfully express the circular motions of the human mind in reasoning. For there are three steps by which the intellect—as if ascending and descending by a certain ladder—is diffused from unity into multitude, and poured back again from multitude into unity. The first step is specific; the second is subaltern; the third is indeed most general; in which, whether by ascent...

<meta>catchword: contra- (against/opposite)</meta>

<summary>This page presents the "Lullian Alphabet," a mnemonic and logical grid where letters B through K represent various philosophical and theological concepts. It introduces Figure A, a circular diagram illustrating the interconnectedness and interchangeability of God's attributes.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Alphabet of the Art, Figure A, Divine Attributes, Combinatorial Logic, Mnemonics, Scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Combinatorial Art," a system of logic and philosophy developed by Ramon Lull and expanded upon here, focusing on how universal concepts are organized and related.</meta>

# or of the COMBINATORIAL ART, BOOK I.

All things are either contracted <note>brought together into specific groups</note> through ascent, or expanded through descent; from individuals we ascend to species, from species to genera, and from these to the most universal genera. Finally, through these, we descend into the ultimate numerical differences of things. These are the primary mysteries of this Sacred Art. Thus, for example, Goodness—which occupies the most general level for contracting or expanding—is specified by the nature of those principles to which it is bound when contracting, or from which it is removed when expanding. The same must be said for all nine essential principles.

It should be noted, however, that a "subalternate principle" <note>a middle-ranking category that is both a species of a higher genus and a genus to lower species</note> has a wide scope, since all those things indicated between an entirely general principle and an entirely specific principle, according to their final specification, are called subalternate principles. But more on these matters in the *Combined Metaphysics*.

| :--- | :--- |

| Between Substance and Accident | **Triangle 2:** Beginning, Middle, End |
| Between Accident and Accident | **Triangle 3:** Majority, Equality, Minority |
| Between the Sensual and the Sensual | |
| Between the Sensual and the Intellectual | |
| Between the Intellectual and the Intellectual | |
| **Other Triads:** | |
| Cause, Quantity, Time | |
| Perfection, Termination, Privation | |
| Conjunction, Commonality, Extremities | |

<margin>
1. What the types of the triangle contain.
</margin>

The Second Figure, marked with the letter **T**, which Lull places in his Art, contains a group of nine <term>ennead</term> relative principles; these are Difference, Accordance, Contrariety, Beginning, Middle, End, Majority, Equality, and Minority. These are encompassed by a threefold entanglement of triangles, as appears in the present symbol. Furthermore, he distinguished other compartments in the circumference, in which three specific general types are contained for each triad of principles. For example: to one triangle of relative principles—which are Difference, Accordance, and Contrariety—he has attached the universal distinctions of things in the outer compartments: the sensual and the sensual, the sensual and the intellectual, and the intellectual and the intellectual. By these, it is shown that the distinction of all things in nature is exhausted by this threefold positioning of differences. Indeed, the difference given between the sensual and the sensual <note>physical objects perceived by the senses</note> regards the widely extended scope of corporeal and sensible nature. When these are compared with one another in various ways, they supply a vast material of things to be skillfully distinguished, whether you look at numerical, specific, or generic levels; for example, the "sensible and sensible" provides the distinction between Heaven and Earth, between a pear tree and a pine tree, between Peter and John, etc.

<margin>
2. Types of the triangle.
</margin>

The second type of the first triangle is the difference between the sensible and the intellectual, by which a new method of distinction is established, while the material is compared with the immaterial, the sensible with the intellectual, and the corporeal with the incorporeal; such is the difference between the soul
