<meta>This page continues the systematic explanation of the "Great Art of Knowing," specifically detailing the symbolic triangles (trigons) that categorize all of reality. It follows the previous discussion on the first principles of existence.</meta>

# OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING

between soul and body, between form and matter, between corporeal subjects and their material virtues, and finally between man, Angel, and God, assigned through a manifold combination of distinctions.

<margin>3. Species of the Triangle.</margin>
The third general species of the first triangle is the difference between the intellectual and the intellectual, established through a manifold reasoning of distinctions between the soul and Angels, between Angels and Angels, between hierarchical orders and orders, and finally between ourselves and God. For through these, it will become known how individual things differ, agree, and are opposed to one another, from which a universal proposition is born: every being is either sensible only; or sensible and intellectual; or intellectual Angelic or divine; or sensible intellectual divine, such as Christ; the differences of which are explained by a ninefold kind of distinctions, concerning each and all of which we shall treat in the following sections.

<margin>2. The triad of principles.</margin>
The second Triad of principles is comprised by the triangle of the Beginning, the Middle, and the End, whose species corresponding to the individual points of the angles are Cause, Quantity, and Time. Cause belongs to the beginning; for every cause is a beginning, but not vice versa, since "beginning" extends more widely than "cause," as is evident from the nine accidental categories <note>The "predicaments" or categories of Aristotle (e.g., quality, relation, place).</note> which are called beginnings, yet are not called causes, with the sole exception of substance, in which they inhere. For every most general accidental genus in its coordination is a beginning, insofar as it initiates all things contained under it by means of substance, yet it cannot be called a cause, because no activity belongs to it of itself, but only by reason of substance, according to whose specification—as Aristotle witnesses—all accidents are specified and individuated. But let us return to explaining the species of the second triangle, which are Cause, Quantity, and Time.

<margin>The manifold Cause.</margin>
Cause is twofold: essential <note>original: "per se"</note> and accidental; the essential causes are four: *Material, Formal, Efficient, and Final*; the accidental cause is *Chance and Fortune*; from which results a vast variety and multitude of causes. The same applies to Quantity and Time, which, as they are all reduced to Beginning, Middle, and End, supply a not-to-be-despised abundance of arguments, especially if they are applied to the nine subjects; for example: whether God, Angel, Heaven, Elements, Man, etc., are beginnings and causes, or what or how they cause, the application of all of which the Art teaches, albeit confusedly and intricately; but of these we shall speak most abundantly in the following.

<margin>Species of the Middle.</margin>
<margin>The End and its species.</margin>
Furthermore, the Middle, likewise a most general principle, has its own species, and they are three: *the Middle of Conjunction, of Measurement, and of Extremities*. The middle of conjunction is investigated in every thing which "transcendent being" embraces in its circuit, and it belongs not only to Creatures, but also to God internally. The middle of measurement and of extremities belongs to all things prevailing in magnitude; for in all things, not only those with quantity, there is a Middle with respect to the extremes, but also in Moral and Physical matters. *The End*, a general principle, has three species: the *first end* is the *end of perfection*, for the sake of which something is done, since according to the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note>, the end and the good are interchangeable, and elsewhere, the end is the best of a thing; and therefore it is considered in essentially subordinated things. For that end which is through the "first intention" <note>The primary goal or purpose.</note> is nobler and more perfect than that which is through the "second intention," as is clear in a blacksmith, who intends the nail by a second intention, and money by the first; and intends money by the second, and bread from it by the first and second, so as to live by the first and second, and to understand and love God by the first; and thus God is the most perfect end, and the only one desired through the first intention; for God, according to Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist.</note>, turns all things toward Himself as the ultimate end; and Aristotle in the 4th book of *Physics* says:
> *Every good, according to itself and its own nature, is an end.*
The second species of end is that of *termination*; because every created Being is terminated by its own limits; but in God it is considered insofar as those who enjoy Him are said to have already attained the term of their desire. The end of *privation* <note>The end as a ceasing to be.</note> belongs most especially to created things; for it is necessary that every single thing which passes into the past is finally ended and consumed, as life by death; sight by blindness; cities and Kingdoms by devastation; in God it only has a place denominatively, insofar as the reprobate <note>Those destined for damnation.</note> ordered toward Him are deprived of the vision.

<margin>Triangle III and its contents.</margin>
The Third Triangle contains within itself likewise three relative principles, which extend to all things; and they are, *Majority, Equality, and Minority*: for Majority is drawn through all nine subjects, through all categories, through all sciences, and things of the World; for magnitude is in God essentially, and in the remaining created things by participation. *Equality* likewise belongs both to God and to Created things; but *Minority* belongs only to Created things, according to the degrees of dignity and excellence by which one exceeds another. These recited principles have three species; for either substance is compared with substance, or substance with accident, or Accident with accident, by which comparison, as Majority, Equality, and Minority always intervene, so these also deservedly exhaust the entire scope of created things. But see all these things expressed in the following table.

It emphasizes that all existence, from God to physical objects, can be analyzed through these logical structures.</summary>

# or of COMBINATORICS, BOOK I.

<margin>
The First Triangle of Relative principles.
</margin>

<note>The following diagram represents the "Trigoni" or Triangles, which are sets of three concepts used in the Lullian Art to analyze the relationships between all things in the universe.</note>

| **Triangle I: Principles of Relations** | **Difference, Concordance, and Contrariety.** <note>These explore how things are distinct, how they agree, or how they oppose one another.</note> These are analyzed as occurring **Between** sensible <note>perceived by senses</note> and intellectual entities, and specifically between cosmic and biological categories, from Heaven and Earth to Animals, Souls, Angels, and God. |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Triangle II: Principles of Species** | **Beginning:** encompassing Cause, Quantity, and Time; considering the fourfold genus of causes <note>Material, Formal, Efficient, and Final causes</note> and the dimensions of magnitude, extension, and duration.<br>**Middle:** encompassing Junction, Measurement, and Extremities; investigating the union of subject and predicate, centers of figures, gravity, or virtue, and the proportions between extremes in elements and heavens.<br>**End:** encompassing Perfection, Termination, and Privation; examining things that lead to an end or goodness, results like joy and fruition, and negations or evils in both physical and moral senses. |
| **Triangle III: Principles of Comparison** | **Majority, Equality, and Minority.** <note>Greater than, equal to, or less than.</note> These are used to compare Substance to Substance, Substance to Accident <note>A property that is not essential to a thing's nature, like color</note>, and Accident to Accident according to nine universal subjects and predicaments. |

<margin>
Difficulty in these sayings.
</margin>

And these are the first foundations of the Lullian Art <note>The "Great Art" of Ramon Llull, a 13th-century system of logic using symbols and diagrams to find universal truths</note> contained in these two figures, by which he expresses the theory of the entire Art. Indeed, unless they are delivered by another method, it seems impossible for any beginner to arrive at any knowledge of the sciences from these pages. This is because he proceeds through the most abstract principles of all things, and requires an intellect that is already excellently instructed and accustomed to abstractions. This seems arduous and exceedingly difficult for boys, beginners, and others who have scarcely tasted the principles of the sciences with the tips of their lips—unless they are assisted and promoted by other modes and reasons accommodated to their capacity in forming propositions according to the rules of the art and properly fashioned reasonings. This we promise to do, once we have first explained the practice of the Lullian Art.

---

### -> C H A P T E R  II. <-

-> *On the practice of the aforementioned Theory prescribed by Llull.* <-

-> §. I. <-

-> *The Third Lullian Figure.* <-

Llull proceeds from the aforementioned Theory to the practice, which he shows in the figure he calls the "Third," composed of the first and second figures, and applied only to created things. It is a triangular Table in which the essential principles are indicated by pairs of individual letters, as is evident in the following figure.

This Figure consists of 36 "Chambers" or compartments; in it, one proceeds to form propositions in a threefold way: descending, transverse, and diagonal—that is, either from L to M, or...

one proceeds either from L to N, or from M to N. In the three aforementioned modes, one principle is predicated of them; the letters added to this designate the predicate. Furthermore, what the letters signify is already clear from the Alphabet provided above; for according to Lull <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher who created a system of logic based on combining concepts using rotating wheels and alphabetic codes.</note>, in each Chamber <note>A "chamber" or "cell" refers to the individual boxes in his diagrams where letters meet.</note>, according to the meanings of the letters, 24 questions, 12 propositions, and as many "middle terms" can be formed. However, he explains how these are applied in only a few words; for example, **BC** indicates here the proposition, *Goodness is great*, because it is shaped by greatness; and **BD**, *Goodness is enduring*, because it is shaped by duration; and **BE**, *Goodness is powerful*, because it is shaped by power, and so on for the rest of the propositions in order, indicated by each pair of letters, as is evident from the following Table, in which the letters occurring in each Chamber are set forth.

### COROLLARY I.

<margin>Explanation of Lull's Figure III.</margin>
From these things it is clear that the Figures in the Lullian Art are nothing other than a mixture or composition of terms; moreover, in any given Figure, two are joined together, sometimes three, and it hardly extends to more, since three terms are sufficient for a Syllogism <note>A form of reasoning in which a conclusion is drawn from two given or assumed premises.</note>. From this arise two figures, of which only two letters belong to the first, as is the present case, and it consists of nine Columns, because there are nine letters of the Lullian Alphabet, and each letter has its own Column; such as B having the first Column; C the second, and so on. In the first Column there are nine cells, because the letter B is joined with itself and with the eight following letters. In the second Column there are eight cells, because the letter C is connected with itself and with the seven following. In the third Column there are seven cells; in the fourth, six; in the fifth, five; in the sixth, four; in the seventh, three; in the eighth, two; in the ninth, one: for there is no cell in which a letter earlier in the Alphabet is placed after a later one, lest the same conjunction of letters be placed twice: for example, there is no cell **CB** in the second Column, because it would be the same as cell **BC** which is placed in the first.

Each Cell contains many attributes and propositions; for each letter is taken either from the first or from the second Column of the Alphabet; whence four modes of this figure arise. For in any cell, either both letters are taken from the first Column of the Alphabet, or the first letter is taken from the first Column and the second from the second; or on the contrary, the first letter is taken from the second Column and the second letter from the first. Moreover, each of the letters in the same Column of the Alphabet signifies many terms: such as B signifying goodness, the good, that which makes good, that which is made good, that which can be made good, and the act of making good. Finally, each letter is either attributed to or subjected to the second; for example: in cell **BC** the following attributes are contained, as follows.

**BC.**

### COROLLARY II.

From these things it is clear, secondly, that BC in this table denotes not only "Good, Great"; but furthermore the question "Whether," and "Difference, God, Justice, Avarice," and all those things designated by the letter B. Through C, however, are noted "What," "Concordance," "Angel," "Prudence," "Gluttony"; indeed, everyone sees that all these things, when confused together into one, produce inextricable difficulties and bring more torment than any benefit to the intellect.

Thirdly, it is clear from this Table how difficult it will be for a Beginner <note>Latin: Tyro</note>, equipped with only these few things, to promise himself the knowledge of debating all subjects; for although all things that exist in the nature of things are contained in it implicitly and by virtue, I nevertheless consider it altogether arduous—not to say *impossible* <note>original Greek: αδύνατον</note>—for anyone not trained in theoretical sciences to perform anything worthy of praise in this Art. For if some Artist asks: "Is a tree good?" He will answer in the affirmative. Why? Because its goodness is great, it is durable, it is powerful, etc. What then? You say nothing, for we knew this already; unless, therefore, you demonstrate the proposed question through other terms that more closely relate to the nature of a tree, you will provide nothing worthy of novelty. But to find new and appropriate terms by which this may be proved, through time and industry, is not the task of a boy or the inexperienced, but of a Philosopher, already long and much exercised in the scholastic arena. I am not ignorant of the huge jumble of things, arguments, and questions exhibited in the Great Art of Lull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher who created a system of logic based on combining fundamental concepts</note>; but I have always disapproved of it being without application and an easy method, which everyone desires. Added to this is a barbaric style and an inextricable obscurity in explanation; hence it is no wonder that the practice of the Art still lies hidden and has been fully understood by no one until now. I confess, however, that Lull's design is admirable and most ingenious, if there had been someone to hand down a method of application joined with ease. Therefore, in this Art, an easy and ready method is sought by which you may respond without hesitation to any proposed question, which we promise to hand down in our Art, God willing; where we trust we shall also exhibit another table, richer and more convenient for forming questions. But let us proceed to the fourth Figure.

### §. II.

### Concerning the Fourth Lullian Figure.

The Fourth Figure of the Lullian Art consists of three circles, of which the uppermost is immovable or fixed; the two inner ones are mobile, as appears in the present Figure. But in this place, I will only set down the first seven revolutions for the Reader's guidance in the manner they are extracted from the circles, so that the logic of the Combination may be more easily understood.

<image-desc>A circular diagram representing the "Fourth Figure" (Quarta Figura) of the Lullian Art. It is a volvelle consisting of three concentric circles, each divided into nine segments. Each segment contains one of the letters from the Lullian alphabet: B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K. The circles are offset relative to each other: at the bottom segment, the outer ring is 'B', the middle ring is 'C', and the inner ring is 'D'. Proceeding clockwise, the next segments align C-D-E, D-E-F, and so on, illustrating the systematic combination of these fundamental concepts.</image-desc>

# OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,

->### The Fourth Lullian Figure.<-

| First Revolution. | Second. | Third. | Fourth. | Fifth. | Sixth. | Seventh. |
|------------------|----------|---------|---------|---------|--------|----------|
| B C D            | B D E    | B E F   | B F G   | B G H   | B H I  | B I K    |
| B C E            | B D F    | B E G   | B F H   | B G I   | B H K  |          |
| B C F            | B D G    | B E H   | B F I   | B G K   |        |          |
| B C G            | B D H    | B E I   | B F K   |         |        |          |
| B C H            | B D I    | B E K   |         |         |        |          |
| B C I            | B D K    |         |         |         |        |          |
| B C K            |          |         |         |         |        |          |

The origin <note>original: "Genesis"</note> of these small columns is as follows: If you apply the letter B of the immobile outer circle to the letter C of the middle circle, and then move the letters of the lower circle in order starting from D toward the letters B C, the first Column of seven chambers <note>The "chambers" or *camerae* are the individual three-letter combinations used for logical deduction.</note> will be born. This being completed, if you move B under D in the second circle, and in the third circle you move the letter E toward the letters B D, the second Column will be born from the revolution of the lowest circle. If again you move B to E in the second circle, and in the third circle you move the letter F to them; from the revolution of this circle the third Column will be born: and so on for the rest; And there are 28, which multiplied by 3 give 84, and these again multiplied by 3 give 252 Chambers, which is to say, just as many seats for arguments. And this is the method prescribed by Lull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the philosopher who invented this system of logic.</note>, truly beautiful, and one that tastes of genius, if only he had taught the application of reducible terms more clearly and with more balanced heat <note>original: "συμμετρο-θερως" (symmetro-theros). The author suggests Lull's explanation lacks a certain proportional clarity or "tempered" delivery.</note>. So that this may be achieved, we shall exhibit another combinatorial table in the following pages, in which everything that can be desired within this Art is contained. But let us now proceed to its practice.

Lull prescribes a twofold practice for this figure: one, which is done through the revolution of the circles; the other through the Table. He believes that through the circular figure, all knowable and foreign questions can be determined and examined, and many reasons can be brought forward to form a conclusion; and in this it consists: that implicit terms are reduced to explicit terms, which are the eighteen principles (partly absolute, partly relative) and the nine subjects, concerning which we spoke in the first place.

Secondly, through the Table, which he calls "General," and as distinguished above, it is constructed from the revolution of the three circles with the addition of T, which denotes that the relative principles are to be taken after it, and it is that which follows.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of combinatorial logic, specifically focusing on the transition from Lull’s traditional methods to the author's improved system.</meta>

# or of COMBINATORICS, BOOK I.

This is that celebrated Lullian Table <note>The "Tabula Lulliana" is a central tool in the "Great Art" of Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher who sought to create a universal system of logic using rotating circles and tables.</note>, which serves as the subject in which the intellect—as he says—renders itself universal. It understands and abstracts many particulars of all subject matters from this table by running through the principles, applying twenty reasons to any given question, and clarifying its inquiry; from every "chamber" <note>original: "Camera"; refers to the individual cells or compartments within the combinatorial table.</note> of the column itself, one reason is abstracted. The Table has seven columns, in which are implied the 84 columns explained in the preceding sections. In this Table, the letter **T.** signifies that the letters preceding **T.** pertain to the First Figure, and those following **T.** pertain to the Second Figure. Through the Table, as Lull says, the "ascent and descent" of the intellect occurs: the **Ascent** is when it rises toward prior and more general things; the **Descent**, however, is when it moves down from universals to particulars. There is also a **Copulative** process, because it joins columns together—for example, **B C D** with **C D E**, and so on for the rest. To these he then joins another method of practice, which he calls the "multiplication of the fourth Figure," and to these subjects he adds rules for the discovery of principles and rules, which he teaches how to apply to nine universal subjects. Finally, he adds to these the application of 100 forms; when all these are properly understood, he claims that anyone can arrive at a knowledge of all things.

I demonstrate the practice and the secret, freed from all obscurity, in Book III; for although he sometimes reveals the mysteries hidden beneath it, he nevertheless proposes them so obscurely and confusedly that his meaning can hardly be understood except by the most highly practiced student. Therefore, lest I immediately pour a mist over the reader here at the beginning of this work, I have deliberately postponed the elucidation of the secret method to Book III. For our task here is not to teach the Lullian method, but to build a new one using principles borrowed from him—a method much easier and more efficient than Lull’s; which we are confident we shall now perform, God willing.

->THE ART<-

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,
->OR<-
# THE COMBINATORY ART,
## BOOK TWO.
->IN WHICH<-
->*The new Lullian method, rendered easier, more suitable, and more efficient, is reduced into a better form.*<-

## PART I.
## The manner and reasoning of the new doctrine is explained.

### CHAPTER I.
->*Concerning the Cause and Occasion of the new method.*<-

---

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'D' depicting a landscape with a building and figures within the frame of the letter.</image-desc>

We have shown above that the difficulty of the Lullian method <note>The system of logic and memory developed by Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), which used rotating discs and alphabetic codes to combine concepts.</note> consists of three things. The first of these consists in the Alphabet of nine letters—B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K—of which each one denotes various meanings of things. For example: B denotes Goodness, "Whether" <note>original: "Utrum" - a logical inquiry into the existence or possibility of a thing</note>, God, Difference, Justice, and Greed. C denotes others, as was clear above from the Lullian Alphabet. It is impossible to say how much confusion this produces in the minds of beginners, given such a great multitude of absolute principles and other reducible, implied terms attached to these nine letters. 

The second difficulty consists in this: that it is extremely difficult and arduous to reduce the terms of the six Enneads <note>Groups of nine; in this context, the sets of nine categories or principles used in Llull's system</note> to their individual corresponding letters and their meanings. 

The third is that one would need to possess a divine memory to keep the rules, terms, propositions, distinctions, definitions, the significance of the Figures among other things, so many rotations of the Figures, and the positions of the hundred forms perfectly impressed upon the mind without confusion, and thus reach the hoped-for fruit of performing anything in the Art. 

Hence, Llull himself openly confesses that no one can reach the perfection of the Art unless they are imbued with a most subtle wit, a most tenacious memory, and a certain natural mental sagacity. Since many have pointed out this extreme difficulty of penetrating the art to me—both those established in excellent ranks of dignity and men from the more celebrated Academies, adorned with every kind of literature—and have asked me whether such an intricate business could be reduced to a easier method; and since I am so constituted by nature that I think nothing more worthy of praise than to bring back to the anvil <note>A metaphor for refining or reworking an idea</note> those arguments that occur everywhere in the Schools in every kind of science; and if indeed the powers of my talent do not fail, to adorn them with a new heap of inventions; so I also present the Lullian art, adorned with a new and more convenient method, for the public good, so that the sublime invention, which was begun with laudable effort by the "Illuminated" Llull <note>Ramon Llull was traditionally known as "Doctor Illuminatus"</note>, may be completed by my labor. Let us therefore begin the matter from the egg <note>original: "Ab ovo" - from the very beginning</note>.

<summary>The author introduces Book Two by identifying three primary flaws in Ramon Llull's original mnemonic system—its confusing alphabet, complex categorization, and impossible memory requirements—and announces his intention to simplify it for the public good.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Combinatory Art, Lullian Alphabet, Enneads, mnemonic systems, logic, pedagogical reform, Book Two</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Combinatoric Art," specifically refining the methods of Ramon Lull for a new audience.</meta>

# or OF THE COMBINATORIC ART, BOOK II.

## CHAPTER II.

### *On the method and reasoning of the New Art, and on the new Alphabets which have been assigned to each of the different classes of the Enneads.* <note>Ennead: a group or set of nine. The author uses this to organize concepts into nine-part categories.</note>

Since I had learned through my own experience the difficulties in the Lullian Art <note>Referring to the system of Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1316), a philosopher who created a logical system to prove universal truths through combinations of concepts.</note> described above, I seriously considered how the inventions of such a talented man might finally find some use in the Republic of Letters. Therefore, to avoid confusion and to clarify everything with greater distinction, we have first changed Lull’s Characters; that is, we have prescribed new Alphabets. However, we have retained the Enneads—both of the interrogations (or questions) and of the principles (both absolute and relative)—in the same order in which Lull placed them, as the sole foundation of the entire Art. We have made only a few changes, merely arranging the Figures in a different way; from this, Circles and Tables (or Combinatoric Abacuses) <note>term: Abaci Combinatorii (Combinatoric Abacuses). These were rotating paper wheels or grids used to calculate logical combinations.</note> have emerged that differ from those of Lull. We have also added specific predicates to each triad of letters as a sort of middle ground for arguing, so that nothing would be lacking that might provide the greatest ease to the task in every respect. I shall proceed through all of these in order, touching upon them with as much brevity as possible.

---

## CHAPTER III.

### *On the Interrogative Ennead, or on Questions.*

Since nothing in the business of Literature can be accomplished without a Question, we have rightly placed the Interrogative Ennead in the first position, as the foundation of all things.

Lull inserted this Ennead into his general Alphabet, not without causing confusion for his readers, such that he assigns to the letter B the question *Whether*; to C, *What*; to D, *Of what*; and so on through the rest of the circumstances of the questions, as is clear from his Alphabet placed above. To avoid confusion, we shall adorn this Ennead with a specific Alphabet—namely, through the nine Figures of numbers proceeding in their natural order—so that the question *Whether* is indicated by 1; *What* by 2; *Why* by 3; *How much* by 4; and so on for the rest. We retain here the Interrogative (or questioning) Ennead, but we have only changed the order; the Ennead follows, contained in a single short verse.

| 1. | 2. | 3. | 4. | 5. |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| *Whether.* | *What.* | *Why.* | *How much.* | *Who.* |
| 6. | 7. | 8. | 9. | |
| *What kind.* | *Where.* | *When.* | *With whom.* | |

Whatever can fall under a question is comprised within this Interrogative Ennead, so that there is nothing in the nature of things knowable that is not exhausted by this Ennead of questions. Hence, it is of great importance in this new Art and deservedly earns the first place among the Enneads explained a little earlier. For the investigation of things has the circumstances of these questions naturally implanted within it before all else. For example, if an inquiry is to be made about Plato: before all else, it is usually asked *whether* Plato was truly such as he is described; *what* he was; *what* he did; *why* he was called Plato; *how great* and *of what kind* he was; *when*, *where*, *how*, and *with whom* he lived. In this manner, one must proceed in every question established concerning any matter whatsoever. Whoever has rightly mastered this Ennead will find prepared for himself innumerable seats of arguments and new reasons for pressing a proposed debate in many and varied ways, as will be seen later in practice. But now let us describe the definitions, divisions, and distinctions of each principle in order.

C

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,

## CHAPTER IV.

### On the definition of the principles of the Interrogative Nine, and on the Rules to be observed in questions.

Every question necessarily requires some subject about which the debate may be established. In the preceding sections, we have established nine such subjects following Lullus <note>Raymundus Lullus (c. 1232–1316) was a Majorcan philosopher who created a "Great Art" using logical diagrams to combine concepts.</note>, and they are: God, Angel, Heaven, the Elements, Man (consisting of soul and body), Animals, and those things which encompass the whole scope of nature—Plants (or the vegetable nature), Minerals (and whatever arises from their mixture), and finally Material or Instrumental things. To these nine subjects, whatever is contained within the entire Machine of the World <note>original: "Mundi Machinâ"; a common Renaissance metaphor for the universe as a divinely designed mechanism.</note> is referred. However, the nature and reason of these subjects cannot be known except through a prior examination of the questions placed here; nor can all these things be understood except through the addition of the nine essential principles, both absolute and relative. And by this fourfold set of Nines <note>original: "Enneadum quaternario"; referring to the four groups of nine concepts used in this system.</note>, that true knowledge of the Tetractys <note>original: "Tetractios"; a mystical triangular figure representing the number ten, sacred to the followers of Pythagoras.</note> intended by Pythagoras can be perfectly acquired. I have seen fit to display all these things here in a single diagram.

->*A universal diagram, suitable for forming questions about any proposed thing.*<-

### CHAPTER V.
->Concerning the Rules of Questions.<-

A "Question" is here called not a doubtful proposition, but the very sign of inquiry, or the term itself through which we seek knowledge. We call a "Rule," however, the right method of responding to a question; for by a Rule the intellect is directed so that it may be able to define aptly. Furthermore, these questions are distinguished among themselves; some are essential, by which the essence of a thing is sought; others are accidental, by which some accident <note>A non-essential quality, like color or size.</note> of the thing is sought, and the former must necessarily precede the latter, lest time be wasted wrongly on the rest.

### RULE I.
->Through the question: Whether or If?<-

The question *Whether, or If*, always has the possibility of the thing implied; hence one must always respond under doubt, either affirmatively, negatively, or distinctively.

->*It is asked therefore first.*<- | ->*Universal Subjects.*<- | ->*It is answered through doubt, affirmation, or negation.*<-
---|---|---
**Whether it is** { Simply, or conditionally. / By itself, or in another subject. / Through the composition of parts. / Through addition. / Possibly. / Impossibly. / Necessarily. / Contingently. | **God. Angel. Heaven. Elements. Man. Animal. Plant. Mineral. 9 Categories <note>The Aristotelian Predicaments or classifications of being.</note>.** | **Through** { Doubt; when the doubtful effects of a thing are proposed, if reasons can be produced for either side, or if propositions are brought forward that strip away each other's credibility. / Affirmation; If the necessity, contingency, or authority of the presented thing is brought forward, whether from sacred or secular histories. / Negation; when impossibility is shown by authority, reason, experiment, or proof of fact—whether technical or conjectural.

### RULE II.
->Concerning What. Who. Which. That.<-

Once knowledge is obtained that the proposed thing truly and really exists, it is rightly answered *what* that thing is, and thus the question immediately explains the quiddity, or the definition of the thing.

### E X A M P L E I.

-> As if it were asked: <-

What is man? $\Re$. To be a rational Animal.
What is he in himself formally? $\Re$. That he derives every operation from his rational form.
What is man in himself essentially? $\Re$. To be a composition of matter and rational form.

And so concerning the rest, which shall be declared more at length in the following sections.

### E X A M P L E III.

What is God in himself? $\Re$. To be His own being.
What is He in another? $\Re$. Lord, Creator, Omnipotent, Savior.
What is He in himself formally? $\Re$. To be good, great, eternal, true, etc.
What is He essentially and co-essentially? $\Re$. To be through Himself and essentially; to be triune through notional acts <note>Refers to the internal activities of the Trinity, such as begetting and proceeding.</note>.

In this way, action and passion <note>In this context, "passion" means being acted upon or receiving an action.</note> in God are not categorical, but perfecting; thus the first active principle in God is fatherhood, and sonship is the passive principle of generation.

What is He containingly? $\Re$. To be all in all, embracing all things without end or limit.
What is He compositely? Does He admit any composition? <note>The implied answer in scholastic theology is no, as God is "Simple."</note>
What is He eminently? $\Re$. To contain all the perfections of creatures with the most eminent perfection of all.
What is He potentially? $\Re$. To be omnipotent.
What is He unitively? What is He identically? $\Re$. To be one simple perfect being, by essential unity. And so concerning the rest.

### RULE III.
->Concerning the Why and Wherefore.<-

The question (*Why*) <note>original: "Quare"</note> rightly follows the essence of a thing and involves within itself all Causality; it is called the key by which the gates of the sciences are opened.

->It is therefore asked:<-

<note>The following diagram illustrates how a question is resolved through the four Aristotelian causes.</note>

| Questions | Central Resolution | The Four Causes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Why is the thing proposed?<br/>Why is it this, and not another?<br/>Why is it from this?<br/>Why is it of this? | The third question is resolved through: | The efficient cause.<br/>The motive, impelling, or final cause.<br/>The material cause.<br/>The formal cause. |

->It is asked, for example:<-

Why is Christ a "Good Shepherd" and called such? **Answer:** Through the **efficient cause** <note>The agent or force that brings something into being.</note> of this pastorship, which is the preordination and anointing for this kind of office.
Why did he wish to be the Good Shepherd? That is, what is the **motive cause**? **Answer:** Because it was the pure love of God, the good pleasure, and the will of God.
Through the **material cause** <note>The "matter" or substance of the action.</note>. **Answer:** Because he governs, nourishes, and defends us; he restrains the wolves and protects the sheep.
Through the **formal cause** <note>The essential nature or pattern that makes a thing what it is.</note>. **Answer:** Because he nourishes with the spiritual food of his own flesh, and rules by the Word and Spirit to attain eternal life.
Through the **final cause** <note>The ultimate purpose or goal.</note>. **Answer:** Because the glory of God and of Christ follows from it, etc.

---

### RULE IV.
->Concerning the Question: How Much?<-

<column-break/>

In this Rule, the inquiry concerns *absolute or comparative quantity; continuous or discrete*, which is understood so generally in this place that it encompasses any formality, such as the finite and the infinite.
Thus, it is first asked: Whether the thing being proposed is great extensively or intensively; whether it is long, wide, or deep; and the rule of this is *quantity*.

<column-break/>

Secondly, one asks about the multitude, and the question is made through the sign *How many?* <note>original: "Quot"</note>; the rule of which is *multitude*.
Thirdly, one asks about the order, and the question is made through the sign *In what order?* <note>original: "Quotus"</note>; the rule of which is *Order*.
Fourthly, one asks about the multiplication, and it is made through the sign *How many fold?* <note>original: "Quotuplex"</note>; the rule of which is *multiplicity*.
Fifthly, one asks about the distribution, and it is made through the sign *How often?* <note>original: "Quoties"</note>; the rule of which is *distribution*.

---

### RULE V.
->Concerning the Question: Which, or In What Manner?<-

This question involves every modality, or the various modes of being.

->It is therefore asked, in what manner is the proposed thing in another?<-

**Whether as:**

1. Genus in a species? <note>e.g., "Animal" within "Human."</note>
2. Species in an individual? <note>e.g., "Humanity" within "Socrates."</note>
3. Species in a genus?
4. An individual in a species?
5. A part in an integral whole?
6. The whole in its parts?
7. An accident in a subject? <note>e.g., the color "red" in an "apple."</note>
8. Form in matter?
9. A cause in the efficient agent?
10. In the final cause? In a place?
11. In time?
12. In a vessel?
13. In a governor?
14. According to a certain excellence?
15. A natural form, such as immortality in the soul, or reason in man?
16. In a mixture?
17. The thing known in the one who knows it?
18. The thing painted in a painting?
19. Equivocally? <note>Using the same word for different things.</note>
20. The equivocal and the analogous in the thing named or compared?

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING

## RULE VI.
### Regarding the Question: *Of what sort?* <note>Latin: *Quale*. This refers to the category of Quality in Aristotelian logic.</note>

The question, *Of what sort?*, is established concerning a twofold quality, and it is a rule directing the intellect toward recognizing the qualities existing within any given subject; these are either substantial, accidental, or moral. A **substantial quality** is that which flows immediately from the essence of a thing, such as the "ability to laugh" <note>Latin: *risibilitas*</note> in a human, or the "ability to whinny" <note>Latin: *hinnibilitas*</note> in a horse, etc., as is evident from this syllogism:

Everything that is considered apart from any later attribute is in reality identified with everything included within it.

But a human, insofar as they are human, is considered apart from any later attribute.

Therefore, a human is in reality identified with everything included within "human."

But the "ability to laugh," a property of humans, is included within "human."

Therefore, it is identified with the human in reality.

The major premise is true; the minor is likewise true through "reduplication" <note>A logical term for considering a subject strictly according to its definition</note>, by which only that which belongs to the essence of the thing is posited. Regarding the second consequence, it is also evident; for a human, insofar as they are human, possesses the potential for the act of laughing, nor could God give them that property if they were incapable of it. From this it is clear that all properties emanating immediately from the form of a thing are essential and substantial; however, they are joined to the number of **accidents** <note>Non-essential characteristics</note> when they are directed toward an external act and have an outward action as their goal, namely, the act of laughing. Such a quality is not only distinguished in reality from the subject according to the mind of St. Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential Dominican philosopher</note>, but also essentially from the subaltern genus; yet according to *Scotus* <note>Duns Scotus (c. 1266–1308), a Franciscan philosopher who often disagreed with Aquinas</note>, it is distinguished "formally" as substantial and intrinsic.

An **accidental quality**, or a common one, belongs to many things, such as whiteness or blackness; just as in the elementary qualities, the dryness of the earth is appropriated to fire; the heat of fire to air; the moisture of water to air; and the coldness of water to the earth. In God, however, quality or property is entirely substantial, of a different order from the categories of logic; for whatever attributes are considered in Him—greatness, infinity, eternity, etc.—are His own substantially, and are identified with the very nature and essence of God. Insofar as they concern "notional acts" <note>Acts that distinguish the persons of the Trinity</note>, Paternity is proper to the Father; Filiation <note>Sonship</note> to the Son; and to the Holy Spirit, there is "Passive Spiration" <note>The theological term for the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father and the Son</note>.

->It is therefore asked first concerning any proposed subject:<-

| | |
|---|---|
| Of what sort is it intrinsically and essentially? | **Answer:** Through all those qualities which concern the essence of the thing. |
| Of what sort is it, insofar as it is a subject for accidents? | **Answer:** Either by extensive or intensive quantity. |
| Of what sort is it regarding action and passion <note>In this context, "passion" means being acted upon</note>? | **Answer:** To be "of a sort" by receiving all qualities, whether the primary elements, or secondary, or tertiary, or intrinsic or extrinsic operations, or those depending on the senses. |
| What sort of respect or relation does it have to others? | **Answer:** By comparing individual qualities to others, considering and combining them with respect to one another. |
| Of what sort is it in place, time, position, or state? | **Answer:** According to those conditions which the circumstances of place, time, position, and state require. |
| Of what sort is it morally? | **Answer:** To be "of a sort" through moral virtues and their opposite vices. |

---

## RULE VII.
### Regarding: *Where?* <note>Latin: *Ubi*</note>

The question regarding *Where?*, involving the locality of a thing, is threefold: **Immeasurable**, **Definitive**, and **Circumscriptive**. For the sake of this, everything is subjected to reasoning through the rules of magnitude and duration, and the question of *Where?* must be answered according to the judgment found in them. For example, if it is asked: Where is God? One must first see what God is according to the preceding rules of questioning; once this is done:

<margin>How God exists.</margin>
I answer: God is everywhere, because He is an immeasurable being, greater than which cannot be imagined, and more intrinsic to all things than things are to themselves. And thus He is in every indivisible point, not as a being contained by a place, but as a being containing both the place and all things concluded within the scope of nature.

<margin>How an Angel is in a place.</margin>
An Angel, however, is in a place "definitively," because their locality is not immeasurable, but is [contained] within its own limits...

<meta>The catchword "bus" at the bottom of the page indicates the start of the next word on the following page, likely "finibus" (limits/boundaries).</meta>

<summary>This page defines the logical rules for questioning the "Quality" (Rule VI) and "Location" (Rule VII) of any subject, distinguishing between essential traits and accidental ones, and explaining how God, humans, and angels occupy space differently.</summary>
<keywords>logic, Aristotelian categories, quality, substantial vs accidental, Thomas Aquinas, Duns Scotus, theology, omnipresence, Scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the categories of existence, specifically focusing on the concepts of Place (Ubi), Time (Quando), and Means (Cum quo) within the framework of the "Combinatory Art."</meta>

# or of the COMBINATORY ART, BOOK I.

<margin>Man, and corporeal things.</margin>
...is enclosed by the boundaries and limits established by God, whole in the whole, and whole in any part; for where it acts, there it is. If it is in the East, it is not in the West; if in this place, it is not in another; which things are to be understood by the same reasoning regarding the rational Soul. Man, however, being corporeal, is in a place *circumscriptively* <note>original: "circumscriptivè"; meaning the body is limited by its physical boundaries and occupies a specific volume of space.</note>, and such a place is the relationship of the container to the thing contained—that is, of the location to the man placed within it.

->It is therefore asked:<-

Where is the proposed thing? **Response:** By the definition of its place.
How much "where" does it have? **Response:** Through three types: the *unmeasurable* in God; the *definitive* in Angels; and the *circumscriptive* in corporeal things.
What kind of "where" does it have? **Response:** Through the shapes of the container and the thing contained.
How long does it last there? **Response:** Through the duration of the thing in a changeable place (in Angels and man) or an unchangeable place in God, who is in Himself without a place.
Is there imaginary space in a place? **Response:** No, by "nowhere"; because where there is no place, all things pertaining to place are removed, such as "elsewhere," "there," "somewhere," etc.

---

### RULE VIII.
### Regarding the Question: When?

<margin>3. Types of duration.</margin>
The question of *When* asks about the duration of a thing according to its permanence; and it is threefold: namely *eternal, eviternal* <note>original: "æviterna"; a philosophical term for things that have a beginning but no end, like souls or angels, distinct from the "eternal" which has neither beginning nor end.</note>, and *temporal*. The first belongs to God; the second to Angels, Souls, and the World; the third belongs to things subject to birth and decay. For if it is asked of God, *when* He is: after you have examined, first, *what* He is and *from what* He is; then *when* He is and *where* He is, according to the judgment found in those categories, the answer must be given. And since He is a being everywhere present and immense, than which nothing greater can be imagined, you shall therefore infer that He is eternal, and that His duration is the "total, simultaneous, and perfect possession of an unending life" <note>This is the classic definition of eternity by the philosopher Boethius.</note>. In this way, He embraces all duration existing always in Himself; thus the flow of all time, compared to Him, is nothing but an instantaneous moment in eternity.

<margin>Eternal duration.</margin>
<margin>Eviternal.</margin>
Indeed, all immediate effects of the First Cause are measured by *eviternal* duration, such as Angels, Souls, and other beings subject to time at least regarding their beginning, but not regarding their end.

<margin>Temporal.</margin>
Finally, all other things are measured by *temporal* duration, being subject to time and liable to birth and decay, such as animals, plants, and bodies composed from the mixture of the elements.

->It is therefore asked:<-

When is or was the proposed thing? **Response:** Through the circumstances of time: years, months, weeks, hours, minutes, etc.
For how much time did it remain in such and such a state? **Response:** In such a monarchy, for so many years; under the rule of such a King or Prince, for such a time.
Under what condition of time was it? **Response:** In a happy or unhappy time; under such and such a King, dominion, principality, or empire.

---

### RULE IX.
### Regarding the Question: With what? and From what?

<margin>The threefold instrument. Which is the natural one?</margin>
The question *With what* has "instrumentality" attached to it, as it asks with what instrument a thing acts. Now, there is a threefold *instrument* by which all things are carried out: *Natural, Moral, and Artificial*. The natural instrument, so to speak, by which God acts, is the Divine Intellect, through which He generates the Son; while the Father and the Son by their Will produce the Holy Spirit. The Father, Son, and Holy Spirit all produce creatures, and the humanity of Christ was the instrument of divinity for the redemption of the human race. In Angels, by the same token, the instruments of acting are the intellect and the will, as they are in man, each in its own way. Moral instruments in man...

<meta>The text cuts off at "man" (homine), which is the catchword for the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page defines the metaphysical categories of Place and Time, distinguishing how God, angels, and physical bodies occupy space and persist through duration. It also introduces the concept of "instruments" (natural, moral, and artificial) used by divine and created beings to act.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, eternity, eviternity, temporal duration, circumscriptive place, divine intellect, instruments of action, scholastic philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Great Art of Knowledge," specifically focusing on the instruments of action and the nature of matter, before introducing the author's new mnemonic alphabets which improve upon the system of Ramon Llull.</meta>

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE

<margin>Moral.</margin> Within man are the moral virtues, by which the rational creature directs his acts. <margin>Artificial.</margin> Artificial instruments are varied according to the variety of external operations; for some belong to the soldier, others to the farmer; some are the instruments of the goldsmith, others of the weaver; some belong to the Physician, others to the Mathematician, and others to other arts.

->*And so it is asked:*<-

With what does the proposed thing operate? **Response:** According to the reasons of the aforementioned threefold division of instruments: natural, moral, and instrumental.

What is the instrument of its operations? **Response:** Either according to the internal or external reasons of the instrument.

What kind of instrument is it? **Response:** Of internal cognition, or of external cognition or making.

<margin>Materiality.</margin> To these, some join the question, *From what;* <note>Latin: "De quo"</note> which question implicitly signifies materiality, and indicates the matter from which something is made. To this, there is a threefold response: for either the proposed thing is not from anything, nor of anything, such as God. Secondly, not of anything, but from someone, such as every created thing—an Angel, or the soul. Or it is of something and from something, of which kind are all corporeal things, generated, produced, made, or formed. Moreover, something is made from another, such as from sensible matter, or from spiritual matter.

Either *by mixing*, as when mead is made from water and honey, etc. <gloss>mixing-ly</gloss>
Either *by aggregating*, as when a house is made from lime, wood, and stones. <gloss>aggregating-ly</gloss>
Either *by composing*, as when a human is made from soul and body, a composite of form and matter. <gloss>composing-ly</gloss>

<margin>One thing is made from another, either Materially or Conversively.</margin> From sensible matter, something is made in various ways, either *Materially* by a change of form, as when a form is drawn out from matter to constitute an animal.
Or *conversively*, as when food and drink are converted into the substance of the one nourished; one metal into another; or stone into lime.

From spiritual matter, something is made from another when a treatise is established concerning some subject, such as the arts and sciences of this work; or when images or "phantasms" <note>mental images or perceptions</note> are apprehended, or when notions of things constitute a discourse, a speech, or a counsel. Finally, this question embraces primary and secondary matter. Truly, this "Ennead" <note>a group of nine</note> of questions will be dealt with professionally and didactically in the following sections.

---

### CHAPTER VI.
->*Concerning the new Alphabets, set down by the Author of this work.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'C' showing a landscape or floral scene within the letter.</image-desc> Since I recognized that the Alphabet of Llull <note>referring to Ramon Llull, the 13th-century mystic and logician</note>—that is, the Ennead of letters B C D E F G H I K—would be an impediment due to the great multitude of things in discriminating so many rules, principles, questions, subjects, virtues, and vices; I thought the letters should be arranged in another way, so that anyone might immediately recognize the essential principle from the letter itself. Indeed, we have marked Goodness with its initial letter, **B**; Magnitude with the initial letter **M**; Duration with the initial letter **D**; and so with the rest, as is evident in the subject schema.

### TABLE *of the Alphabets of our Art.*

| First Column. *First Alphabet of Questions.* | Second Column. *Alphabet of Absolute Principles.* | Third Column. *Alphabet of Relative Principles.* | Fourth Column. *Alphabet of Universal Principles.* |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1. Whether? | 1. B. Goodness. | 1. = Difference. | 1. Δ God. |
| 2. What? | 2. M. Magnitude. | 2. ♡ Concordance. | 2. <image-desc>Bust of a winged angel</image-desc> Angel. |
| 3. Why? | 3. D. Duration. | 3. ∞ Contrariety. | 3. ☉ Heaven. |
| 4. How much? | 4. P. Power. | 4. α Beginning. | 4. ◻ Elements. |
| 5. Who? | 5. S. Wisdom. | 5. ⊙ Middle. | 5. <image-desc>Standing human figure</image-desc> Man. |

| 7. Where? | 7. Vi. Virtue. | 7. M Majority. | 7. <image-desc>Flowering plant or small tree</image-desc> Plants. |
| 8. When? | 8. Ve. Truth. | 8. Æ Equality. | 8. <image-desc>Three rounded stones or mountains</image-desc> Minerals & all mixed things. |
| 9. With what? | 9. G. Glory. | 9. Mi. Minority. | 9. ::: Materials; Instrumentals. |

# or COMBINATORICS, BOOK II.

<margin>Figures or characters by which the respective principles are noted.</margin>
For once a letter is seen, the principle cannot remain hidden; thus these three letters B. M. D. indicate that **Great Goodness** is **durable**; likewise M. Vi. Vo. note that there is **great force of Will**, and so on for the rest. <note>The author uses a shorthand system where B=Bonitas (Goodness), M=Magnitudo (Greatness/Magnitude), D=Duratio (Duration), Vi=Virtus (Power/Force), and Vo=Voluntas (Will).</note>

Furthermore, to express the relative principles more clearly, we have ordained other signs so that if they are intermingled with letters and tables anywhere, the relative principles might be better distinguished from the absolute ones. First, we have marked the principle of *Difference* with two parallel lines, in this manner = as appears in the second column. *Concordance* <note>original: Concordantiam</note> we marked with the figure of a heart <image-desc>heart symbol</image-desc>, which indicates conjunction, agreement, and union. *Contrariety* or *Opposition*, however, we expressed by the sign used by astronomers, which notes the opposition of the stars, as follows <image-desc>astronomical symbol for opposition: two small circles connected by a horizontal line</image-desc> where one small circle is black and the other white to denote contrariety. We noted the *Beginning* <note>original: Principium</note> with the Greek letter $\alpha$, Alpha; the *Middle* with a circle with a center <image-desc>circle with a central dot</image-desc>. The *End* we exhibited through $\omega$ Omega, as can be seen in the second column. *Majority* is shown by a large M. A. suffix, so that it may be distinguished from "Greatness" <note>original: magnitudinem</note> which is an absolute principle. *Equality* is shown by the diphthong Æ, the initial letter of equality; *Minority* by "Mi," in lowercase with the letter "i" added. But see these in the diagram of the third column.

<margin>Signs of universal subjects.</margin>
We have thought it right to mark each of the universal subjects with their own signs, such that: by a triangle <image-desc>triangle symbol</image-desc> is meant *God the Triune*; by a winged figure <image-desc>winged figure/angel symbol</image-desc> an *Angel*; by circles <image-desc>symbol of two overlapping circles</image-desc> the *Heaven*; by a square <image-desc>square symbol</image-desc>, the *Elements*; by a human sign <image-desc>stylized human figure symbol</image-desc>, *Man*, and those things which can in any way pertain to man; by a quadruped <image-desc>quadruped animal symbol</image-desc>, *Quadrupeds*, including aquatic animals, birds, and insects; by a tree <image-desc>tree symbol</image-desc>, *Plants*, including types and species of trees and shrubs; by a stone <image-desc>mineral/stone symbol</image-desc>, *Minerals* and all mixed substances; by a nine-dot grid <image-desc>nine-dot grid symbol (3x3)</image-desc>, the nine accidental Categories <note>The "Prædicamenta" or Categories of Aristotle, such as Quality, Quantity, Relation, etc.</note>; instrumental things, primary and fertile matter, and whatever can be recalled here are also designated, as is evident from the fourth column.

And to this trinity of Enneads <note>Groups of nine; the author organizes his system into three sets of nine principles.</note>, whatever is knowable is reduced; they are the fertile nurseries of all human and divine science, as we shall demonstrate in its proper place and time.

We shall exhibit the use of these principles of nature for the knowledge of all things first through circles, and then also through combinatory tables, which in Greek are called *symplokas* <note>original: συμπλοκὰς; meaning "interlacings" or "combinations"</note>. Before we perform this, we will first explain the individual principles of these Enneads, so that their transcendental logic and their breadth, stretched far and wide into every circuit of nature, may be more clearly revealed.

---

<margin>Note that B. M. D. P. etc. signify nothing other than the initial letters of Goodness, Greatness, and Duration; so that the reader might immediately recognize the predicates or principles from the initial letter.</margin>

## PART II.

### On the definition of the absolute principles, which are B. M. D. P. S. Vo. Vi. Ve. G. <note>Goodness, Greatness, Duration, Power, Wisdom, Will, Virtue, Truth, and Glory.</note>

->CHAPTER I.<-
->On the definition of Goodness.<-

<margin>What goodness is.</margin>
Since the principle of demonstration is the definition of the thing, we shall begin with those things which, if ignored, make it necessary to be ignorant of art and science.
The definition of *Goodness* is as follows: *Goodness is that being by reason of which good is good*; thus to be is good, and not to be is evil. Since every good definition consists of a genus and a difference, in the aforesaid definition "being" <note>original: ens</note> is placed in the position of the genus, yet not as something that can be restricted to Goodness; for Goodness not only abstracts from being, but from entity itself. Therefore, it cannot receive a formal predication from it; rather, "being" is said of goodness only by an external naming. Others define goodness as if it were the "being of the good," the source and origin of all good, of essence, and of actions, which are not separated by privation in the same subject and time.

<margin>How many types of goodness. Uncreated goodness.</margin>
*Goodness* is twofold: *Uncreated*, which is goodness simply and of itself, absolutely essential and necessary, from which all things are good by participation, namely God.
*Created* goodness is again twofold: *Entitative* and *Moral*, or *substantial* and *accidental*. Insofar as it is entitative, it comprehends under itself everything that exists in any way or act; and to this all things of nature are recalled, and it is interchangeable with unity.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of "Goodness" as a fundamental principle in Athanasius Kircher's universal logic system. It transitions from definitions to the practical application of syllogisms.</meta>

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE
<note>Original title: *Ars Magna Sciendi*. This was Kircher’s 17th-century attempt to create a universal symbolic system to categorize all human knowledge.</note>

<margin>Moral.</margin>
**Moral Goodness** is that which is caused by several acts elicited from the will, insofar as they conform to the right dictates of reason; to this, vices and sins are opposed.

<margin>Accidental.</margin>
**Accidental Goodness** is that by which everything that exists in actuality is determined, such as accidents <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a property that isn't essential to a thing's nature, like the color of a shirt.</note> of every kind; and this is the most general division of goodness.

Therefore, all things are either good, or evil, or indifferent <note>original: *adiaphora* — things that are morally neutral.</note>: and indifferent things can be good or evil; such as Leisure, Will, Pleasure, etc.

<margin>Goods of the end, or for the sake of the end.</margin>
There are also some goods of the **End**, others **for the sake of the End**; others are **simple**, and these are the natural faculties without which nature could not operate.

<margin>Goods of the soul.</margin>
There are again other **necessary** goods, such as Life, prudence, liberty, parents, and virtues. **Non-necessary** goods include honors and all free actions which can bring about some advantage.

<margin>Goods of the body.</margin>
There are also **goods of the body**, such as health, beauty, and every perfection of the corporeal essence. **Goods of the soul** are all virtues and immanent actions <note>Actions that stay within the performer, like thinking or feeling.</note> free from malice, such as Wisdom. **External goods** are those of fortune, such as riches, power, and dignities.

Goodness is therefore a certain abstraction, and a most general, primitive, true, and necessary principle; because all things, insofar as they exist, are good, since without this goodness all things would be evil.

And since goodness would be of little use in this art unless the terms which can be reduced to it in any way were assigned: I say those terms are nothing other than those predicates which can be summoned to the concept of goodness; and these provide us with vast material for propositions and syllogisms <note>A form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises.</note>; they are also called Comparatives and Synonyms; some of which pertain to entitative <note>Relating to the sheer existence or "being" of a thing.</note> goodness, others to moral and accidental goodness. All of these we shall propose in this place in such an order that the first Column always denotes the **Major** proposition in a Syllogism, and the second denotes the **Minor** with the **Conclusion**.

->In a very similar way, by assuming contraries, you will argue concerning those things that are opposite to substantial goodness: as follows:<-

***

<margin>Explanation of Goodness.</margin>
Goodness is that being by reason of which a good thing acts for the good; and thus, to be is good, and not to be is evil. <note>This reflects the medieval philosophical idea that "being" and "goodness" are convertible; existence itself is a gift.</note> Goodness is therefore a principle outside of which no other thing can be a principle; and so every being existing through itself is essentially good. This goodness is considered from the lowest grade of being up to the final and highest good, which is the supreme and infinite goodness—the supreme principle of all other goods, goodnesses, and beings, moving all other good things as the Ultimate End of all, needing nothing other than itself.

### RULE I.

All these terms reducible to Goodness hold the place of the **subject** in a proposition, while "Good" holds the place of the **predicate**. For example, if someone wishes to show that Justice is good, he will have a Major premise ready in many and various ways. For whichever of the terms placed in Column II and IV he chooses, he will always have a Major premise prepared in every respect. The Minor premise, however, will always be that which is taken up to be proven—in this case, Justice—as is clear from the following.

<summary>This page provides a logical framework for identifying moral evils by contrasting them with the nature of Goodness. It defines Goodness as the fundamental principle of existence and provides a "syllogistic machine" for proving why specific vices are inherently bad.</summary>
<keywords>syllogism, moral goodness, vices, virtues, logic, being, ultimate end, justice, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructional framework of the "Great Art of Knowledge," focusing on the application of the first two fundamental principles: Goodness and Magnitude.</meta>

# OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,

> Everything acceptable, desirable, and fitting is good.
> All justice is acceptable, desirable, and fitting.
> Therefore, all justice is good.
---
->But through contraries thus:<-
---
> Nothing unaccepted, undesirable, and unfitting is good.
> All injustice is unaccepted, undesirable, and unfitting.
> Therefore, no injustice is good.
> Therefore, it is intrinsically evil.

### Axioms of Goodness.

1. There is one Goodness, by nature and essence, and that is uncreated: God; all other things are good by participation or through creation.
2. There are as many species of evil as there are of good, and vice versa; those things which are stated affirmatively of one are judged to be stated negatively of its contrary, and vice versa.
3. Within Goodness there is "being good" and "doing good," and within that same goodness, that which "can be made good." <note>This refers to the Lullian triad of agent, patient, and action: bonificativum, bonificabile, and bonificare.</note>
4. Good is the remedy for evil, while evil is the disease.
5. Good is to be embraced, evil is to be fled.
6. An animal can be made good and virtuous by nature, by character, by habit, by reason, and by instruction; by nature, when it is born; by character, when it imitates; by reason, when it is taught and reasons for itself.

<margin>Recapitulation.</margin>
You have here, therefore, in four examples—each of which is again distributed into three columns—a specimen so that the Beginner <term>Tyro: a novice or student of the Art</term> may learn the method of arguing from the goodness of things. The first of these contains predicates reducible to "entitative" Goodness <note>Goodness as it relates to the very being or essence of a thing</note>; the second contains their opposites or contraries; the third contains moral virtues; the fourth contains natural virtues. These are the subjects about which the reducible terms in the first column are predicated. Indeed, the first column always has the force of a Major Proposition and serves as the seat of the Middle Terms; whereas the terms of the third and fourth columns are taken for the Minor Proposition. But since a more copious amount of material for discussion will be given regarding these in the following sections, we shall not linger on them here.

# CHAPTER II.

->On the definition of Magnitude.<-

**M**agnitude is that by reason of which Goodness, Eternity, etc., are great. Or, Magnitude is the extension or intensity of any thing, from which it is called "great." It is also called the fullness of the great, or the extension of the great, without which all things would be small, and the privation of which would be a "great smallness." It is a universal, most general, primitive, and necessary principle, since there is no being in the nature of things that does not have its own reasons for magnitude—which consist either in extension, as in corporeal <note>physical</note> things, or in intensity, which applies to incorporeal <note>spiritual or mental</note> things.

<margin>Various magnitude: uncreated, created.</margin>
Furthermore, *Magnitude* is either *uncreated* or *created*; the former applies to God, the latter to created things. It is also *substantial* or *accidental*. Substantial or entitative magnitude is the sufficiency of any thing according to its own capacity, or the "being great" that is consistent with each thing.

<margin>Accidental, moral, and of virtue.</margin>
*Accidental* magnitude is again twofold: accidental magnitude [of quantity] and magnitude of virtue. The former belongs to corporeal things affected by quantity; the latter belongs to incorporeal things, as was said a little earlier.

<margin>Potential, Actual.</margin>
One type of *magnitude* is *potential*, another is *actual*. The former is that which is said to be "in the becoming"; the latter is in that which "is." Again, one magnitude is *perfect*, another *imperfect*. The former is called the magnitude of bodies, which consists of three dimensions; the latter is spoken of in relation to another more absolute body—thus a line is smaller than a surface, and a surface smaller than a solid body.

<margin>The threefold magnitude of bodies.</margin>
*Magnitude* is also divided into those things which belong to the body, the soul, and external things. In the *Body*, magnitude is considered as height and proportionate thickness. In the *Soul*, it is magnanimity <note>greatness of spirit</note> and boldness. In *external goods*, wealth, lordship, and command are considered.
The following table shows how these may be applied.

<meta>catchword: TABU- (Table)</meta>

### Application TABLE of Magnitude.

The composite reducible terms are the following, reduced to a syllogistic form <note>A syllogism is a form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two assumed premises (major and minor).</note>.

Everything that is either enclosed within natural boundaries, or is devoid of boundaries, is great.

| **Everything** {<br/>B. D. P. S. Vo. Vi. Ve. G. <note>These letters refer to the author's specific combinatory alphabet representing principles like Goodness, Greatness, Power, etc.</note><br/>prevailing either by infinite extension or # THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE

It must be noted first, in this place, that not all "middle terms" <note>In logic, the middle term is the link that connects the subject and predicate in a syllogism.</note> can be applied to all subjects. For example, terms like "composite," "measured," and similar descriptions apply neither to God, nor to Angels, nor to the Soul; nor do terms for animate things apply to the inanimate. Therefore, those middle terms must be selected which, once a subject is chosen, can properly belong to it—such as propositions that correspond to one another in a parallel fashion.

It must be noted secondly: since the logic of opposites is the same, a vast field for argumentation is provided by a similar process through the assumption of opposites. For example:

> Everything unextended cannot be great.
> But a point is unextended:
> Therefore, a point cannot be great.

We have placed two columns here; the first contains the terms or predicates of simple magnitude. The second contains composite terms; whichever of these you choose, you will always have a "Major" proposition ready for a syllogism, along with the discovery of the middle term. This serves to demonstrate whatever was proposed in the "Minor" proposition concerning a subject placed in the third column (or anything reducible to it).

->The Axioms of Magnitude are:<-

1. Everything "intense" <note>original: "intensum"; referring to internal power or spiritual depth</note> is more perfect than its "extended" counterpart, because the former properly belongs to incorporeal things, while the latter belongs to physical bodies.
2. Great and small are not opposites, but dissimilarities, for they are always relative to something else.
3. Every essence of spiritual magnitude, which enjoys "intensity" alone, cannot be divided into parts because it is indivisible; the opposite must be said of extended (physical) things.

---

## CHAPTER III.
### *On Duration.*

Duration is nothing other than the permanence of any thing in its own being; for that which perseveres in what it is, is said to "endure." It is a principle most general, universal, primitive, and necessary, inasmuch as without it nothing would exist; if duration did not exist, nothing would be, nor could anything be established. It is threefold:

<margin>Eternal duration.</margin>
**Eternal**, which properly belongs to God, because He is God Himself; since it is the total, simultaneous, and perfect possession of an unending life.

<margin>Everlasting (Aeviternal).</margin>
**Everlasting** <note>original: "Æviterna"; a philosophical term for the duration of beings like angels, which have a beginning but no end.</note>, which belongs to Angels, souls, and matter; it is found in the immediate effects of the First Cause, which have a beginning but no end.

<margin>Temporal.</margin>
**Temporal** is the duration of things that can be generated and corrupted <note>meaning things that are born and die, or are created and decay</note>; this cannot exist forever, but lasts only until a certain time.

<meta>catchword: Terms</meta>

<keywords>logic, syllogism, magnitude, intensity vs extension, duration, eternity, aeviternity, temporality, metaphysics</keywords>

# OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,

*Argue thus through the assumption of contraries or opposites:*

> No temporal thing is lasting, or forever durable, or eternal.
> But everything subject to generation <note>The process of being born or coming into existence</note> is temporal.
> Therefore, it is not eternal.

You shall proceed in the same way in all other matters.

### Axioms.

1. Everything immobile and eternal is nobler, more perfect, more abundant, and more stable than that which is everlasting <note>original: "æviterno" - referring to things that have a beginning but no end, like angels</note> or mobile.
2. Everything nobler, more perfect, and more stable than the everlasting is superior to the temporal and successive.
3. Every temporal thing, all else being equal, is more stable and firm in itself the more perfect it is.
4. In every duration there are four things: *Now. Before. After. Always.*

Always and Now belong to God alone.
Before, Now, and After belong to Angels, Souls, and incorruptible things.
Before and Now belong to corruptible things.
5. Every thing is more long-lasting the more effective its efficient cause is.
Each one of these Axioms can take the place of the Major Proposition in a Syllogism. The universal subjects take the place of the Minor; as in the example:

> Every immobile and eternal Being is more perfect and noble than the everlasting and the temporal.
> But God is an immobile and eternal Being.
> Ergo, He is more perfect and noble than the everlasting Angel and the temporal [being].

In like manner, the everlasting is nobler in duration than the temporal, such as man, beast, etc.
And so, by descending step by step, you will show that the higher is always more durable than the lower.

---

### Chapter III.
-> On Power. <-

<margin>Triple Power.</margin>
Power <term>Potentia: the capacity to act or be acted upon</term> is a most general, primitive, substantial, and necessary principle, insofar as without it no Being can act or operate; and it is called that by which all things are fit for working. It is threefold:
*Uncreated*, such as the infinite power of God, from which the origin of all power flows, and from which all things have power by participation; either permissively or effectively.
*Created or Finite* is again fourfold. | *Either Natural*, comprising all the virtues and faculties of natural things. *Preternatural*, all those things which depend on God (understood in a sound way), fortune, chance, fate, or necessity; such as miracles, wonders, oracles, etc. *Political and Moral*, containing all power of Monarchy, Aristocracy, Democracy, and the private dominion of anyone. Extrinsic and Intrinsic things pertain to it. *Extrinsic* are strength, arms, etc. *Intrinsic* are all voluntary actions.

-> *Compound terms reducible to Power in Syllogistic form.* <-
-> *All Virtues and Powers of things belong to universal subjects.* <-
