--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the "Combinatory Art," moving from the definitions of power to the classification of knowledge and wisdom. It maintains the scholastic structure of axioms followed by systematic categorization.</meta>

### Axioms.
<note>Axioms are self-evident principles that serve as the foundation for further reasoning.</note>

1. All power is from God, either by permission or by direct effect. <note>original: "permissivè, vel effectivè"</note>
2. Everything eternal, abstract, and necessary is an act <note>an "act" here refers to a state of full realization or being</note>, not a passive potential.
3. Power in a state of actuality is always better than power in a state of becoming.
4. The sign of power is to tend toward being; the sign of weakness is to tend toward non-being.
5. Nothing can be simultaneously in a formal act and a formal potential at the same time.
6. In evil things, the act is evil, and therefore worse than the potential for it.
7. There is no passive potential in the nature of things to which some active power does not correspond.
8. Power, natural aptitude, and weakness are species of quality.

All these Axioms again suggest an immense variety of arguments, which will be shown more fully in their proper place.

---

# CHAPTER V.
### Regarding Wisdom and Knowledge, or regarding the Absolute Cognitive Principle.

<margin>Various divisions of knowledge.</margin>
Wisdom is the most general, primitive, and necessary cognitive principle, inasmuch as without it nothing can be known. And it is defined in the Art <note>referring to the "Great Art" of Ramon Llull</note> as the principle by which each thing knows those things which are necessary for its own preservation; for without this, no science would be given, but all things would be unknowable and would not be understood, and thus the World would have been created by God in vain.

Knowledge is, first of all, *uncreated*; and God, the Best and Greatest, is called *Eternal Wisdom*: a knowledge that is super-essential, substantial, and necessary. The other kind is *created* knowledge, which spreads itself into the following branches.

<margin>Created Knowledge is fivefold.</margin>
*   **Intellectual** belongs to the Angels, and is without discursive reasoning, as is also infused knowledge.
*   **Rational** is either:
    *   Theoretical. <note>original: "Speculativa"</note>
        *   Real <note>concerning actual things</note>.
            *   Metaphysics, which deals with supernatural things.
            *   Physics, dealing with Natural things.
            *   Mathematics <note>original: "Mathesis"</note> concerning the demonstrative sciences: Arithmetic, Music, Geometry, and Astronomy.
        *   Rational <note>concerning the tools of thought</note>.
            *   Grammar.
            *   Dialectic <note>Logic</note>.
            *   Rhetoric.
            *   Poetics.
            *   History.
            *   Jurisprudence <note>The study of Law</note>.
                *   Divine Law.
                *   Natural Law.
                *   Law of Nations.
                *   Pontifical <note>Church/Canon</note> Law.
                *   Civil Law.
                *   Law of Custom.
                *   Law of Honor <note>original: "Honestatis"</note>.
                *   Law of Necessity.
                *   Positive Law <note>Man-made statutes</note>.
    *   Practical.
        *   Active.
            *   Politics.
                *   Monarchical.
                *   Aristocratic.
                *   Democratic.
            *   Ethics.
            *   Economics <note>In the classical sense of household management</note>.
    *   Productive. <note>original: "Factiva"</note>
        *   Illiberal <note>Manual</note> Mechanical Arts, such as:
            *   Military Service.
            *   Hunting.
            *   Wool-working.
            *   Agriculture.
            *   And all manual trades.
        *   Liberal <note>Fine</note> Arts, such as:
            *   Architecture.
            *   Painting.
            *   Sculpture.
*   **Estimative** is knowledge from real distinction and the comparison of those things which are apprehended.
*   **Sensual or Fantastic**, which is born from the senses, both exterior and interior, from the proper apprehension of an object.
*   **Instinctive** belongs properly to beasts, and in its own way to plants and inanimate things, caused by actions of Sympathy and Antipathy; it is called instinct or a natural inclination and impulse toward acting.

<summary>This page outlines eight axioms regarding the nature of power and potentiality, followed by a comprehensive taxonomic classification of knowledge (Sapientia) into five categories: Intellectual, Rational, Estimative, Sensual, and Instinctive.</summary>
<keywords>Axioms, Wisdom, Taxonomy of Knowledge, Jurisprudence, Metaphysics, Rationalism, Natural Law, Mechanical Arts, Instinct</keywords>

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING,

## Terms Reducible to Cognition.

To Wisdom, or the Cognitive principle, are referred all rational and intellectual Beings, such as God, Angels, and Man. Secondly: All arts, both liberal <note>The "liberal arts" traditionally included grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy.</note> and illiberal <note>Also known as "mechanical arts," involving manual labor or trade.</note>. Thirdly: Everything that savors of learning and wisdom. Fourthly: All degrees of knowledge, such as Doctor or Master. Fifthly: All particular sciences contained in the previously established scheme of division. The terms follow.

# or of COMBINATORICS, BOOK II.

->*Argue through Opposites in this way.*<-

Nothing unknown is subject to a cognitive principle.
Ignorance is such a thing. Therefore <note>Therefore, ignorance is not subject to a cognitive principle.</note>.

->*Axioms.*<-

1. Natural matters are to be investigated by reasons.
Moral matters are to be acquired by practice.
Divine matters are to be understood through sacred oracles.
2. It is characteristic of any intellectual Nature to understand according to the mode of its own substance.
3. Every human being naturally desires to know. <note>original: "Omnis homo naturaliter scire desiderat" — a famous opening line from Aristotle's Metaphysics.</note>
4. Logic is the art, or instrument, of knowing.
5. Regarding Cognition, a conclusion is always reached affirmatively according to the comparative measure of perfection; just as every intellect is better, more durable, nobler, and more perfect than reason; Reason, than the estimative faculty; the estimative, than sense; sense, than instinct. The reason is because the estimative faculty exists for the sake of the intellect; the common sense, for the sake of the estimative; the instinct of brutes, for the sake of sense.

---

## CHAPTER VI.
### On the Appetitive Principle.

Appetite <term>Appetitus: in this context, a general term for any internal drive or inclination toward a goal, not just hunger.</term> is an incitement or a certain impulse flowing from a natural faculty, by whose force a thing is excited toward something, either by itself or from elsewhere; it is a general, primitive, and necessary principle, without which nothing is lovable, nothing desirable, and nothing hateful. Its division follows below.

| Appetite is either | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **1. Rational, or the threefold Will** | **Divine** | Twofold: of the Sign or of Good Pleasure <note>Scholastic terms distinguishing between God's revealed commands and His hidden, absolute will.</note>; the latter is further defined as absolute, conditional, or permissive. |
| | **Angelic** | |
| | **Human** | |
| **2. Irrational** | **Natural Faculty (fivefold)** | Attractive, Retentive, Alterative, Concoctive <note>Relating to digestion or the "cooking" of nutrients in the body.</note>, and Expulsive; to which are joined the **Sympathy and Antipathy of things**. |
| | **Sensual or Animal Appetite (twofold)** | **Concupiscible:** <note>The faculty concerned with simple longing or aversion.</note> relating to **Good** (e.g., love, desire, joy) or **Evil** (e.g., hatred, abomination, sadness). |
| | | **Irascible:** <note>The faculty concerned with overcoming obstacles to obtain good or avoid evil.</note> including hope, despair, anger, impulse, fury, and audacity. |

->*Axioms.*<-

1. The greater the good, the more excellent is the Will that desires it; just as God is more lovable than an Angel, an Angel more than a man, a man more than a brute beast, etc. For the Charity of God, by which He is loved for His own sake, is more excellent than Charity toward one's neighbor, by which God is loved <note>original: "Charitas Dei... Charitate erga proximum"</note>.
2. Everything that is desired for its own sake is more excellent than that which is desired for the sake of another: For the Charity of God, by which He is loved for His own sake, is more excellent than Charity toward one's neighbor, by which God is loved.
3. All things are moved for the sake of the good, as the end of desire: as a stone desires the center [of the earth] so that it may rest; a plant so that it may flourish and grow; a beast so that it may feel; and so with the rest.

---

# CHAPTER VII.
## On the Principle of Virtue.

Virtue, considered in this place, is a general, primitive, and necessary principle, without which all things would be vicious; and it is defined as the faculty that reconciles and constitutes good things, the beneficent force of things affected by goodness; and therefore of all things, and through all things; it is the reason why the good, the great, the durable, etc., is vigorous and equipped with the virtues and strengths necessary for operation.

<meta>catchword: Virtue</meta>

<summary>This page uses syllogistic logic and tables to categorize the "Appetitive" principle (desire and will) and introduces "Virtue" as the fundamental force that enables goodness and action in all things.</summary>
<keywords>syllogism, desire, free will, axioms of love, charity, principle of virtue, logic of passions, scholastic philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of universal concepts, specifically focusing on the definition and categorization of "Virtue" or "Power" (Virtus) as part of the author's combinatorial logic system.</meta>

# or COMBINATORICS, BOOK II.

Virtue <note>original: "Virtus"; in this context, it refers to both moral virtue and inherent power or efficacy</note> is of two kinds,
- **Spiritual; and it is fivefold.**
    - **Super-intellectual;** this is the unknown Power of God to us; such as the Power of miracles through Angels and men; and the deceptive wonders <note>original: "præstigia"; often used to describe illusions or demonic magic</note> permitted through demons.
    - **Intellectual;** these are Wisdom, science, prudence, and those things which relate to cognition.
    - **Rational;** Self-control, gentleness, fortitude, justice, liberality.
    - **Moral;** which concerns the formation of character.
    - **Theological,** such as Faith, Hope, Charity, grace, and the gifts of God, which direct the soul intrinsically toward God.
- **Inherent in bodies; and it is fivefold.**
    - **Animal,** which is again threefold: Sensitive, and this is either interior or exterior; the latter encompasses the five external senses, the former the internal senses. The Motive power is again either Natural or appetitive.
    - **Vegetable** moreover is that which operates and consists in generation, growth, and nutrition, which occur by means of the attractive, retentive, digestive, and expulsive powers.
    - **Mineral** considers all those powers which are proper to each specific thing.
    - **Elemental,** fourfold; Heating, drying, moistening, cooling.
    - **Celestial,** such as all the influences and properties of the stars.

---

| Regarding the act in respect to the habit <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, a 'habit' is a stable disposition or quality, while an 'act' is the exercise of that quality.</note>, the conclusion is always affirmative; but regarding the habit, it is negative; as every good act is better and more desirable than its habit; the reason: because the habit stands in the manner of a form, but its act is the operation; and the form, according to Aristotle, exists for the sake of the operation, and not the reverse. # THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,

## CHAPTER VIII.
### *On the Principle of Truth.*

Truth, according to the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle, known for walking while lecturing.</note>, is the correspondence of a thing to the intellect; according to us, Truth is the reason by which all things are true; for if Truth did not exist, all things would be false, which is a contradiction. Truth is therefore a general, primitive, and necessary principle; for by the very fact that something exists, it is also true.

<note>The following diagram classifies Truth into Divine and Created forms.</note>

> [ Uncreated Truth is God, the source and origin of all Truth, just as the Sun and light are the sources of brightness.
> 
> Truth is otherwise:
> 
> Created Truth is threefold,
> - **Of the Thing.** { The Truth of a thing is in its essence, character, and actions.
> - **Of the Intellect.** { The Truth of the intellect is the conformity of the thing to the mind.
> - **Of Speech.** { It is the conformity of the thing to the mind expressed through speech or a proposition.

->Compound Terms reducible to Truth expressed in Syllogistic form.<-

<note>This table functions as a logical machine. By matching a premise from the "Major" column with one from the "Minor" column, the reader arrives at the "Conclusion" that the subject is True.</note>

| Major (General Premise) | | Minor (Specific Case) | | Conclusion |
| :--- | :---: | :--- | :---: | :--- |
| Everything that has attained intellectual correspondence and rectitude. | | All operations of the intellect achieved through Logical rectitude. | | |
| Everything approved by the dictate of right reason. | | All moral Virtues which are approved by the dictate of right reason. | | |
| Everything confirmed by legitimate authority. | | All laws and statutes supported and confirmed by public authority. | | |
| Everything established by true testimony or oath. | **Is True.** | All confirmations, Jurisdictions, wills, and similar acts of law. | **Are such.** | **Therefore they are true.** |
| Everything that has being and essence. | **But** | All universal subjects with everything reducible to them that have their own essence. | | |
| Every definition of things. | | Every "whatness" <term>quidditas: the essential nature of a thing</term>, matter, and essence of things known by definition. | | |
| All supernatural faith. | | All orthodox doctrine, articles, Sacraments, etc. | | |
| Every law or statute that is binding. | | All Rules, constitutions, precepts, and binding ceremonies. | | |
| All positive things and whatever gives the actuality of being. | | All Beings <term>Entia: things that exist</term>. | | |
| All formal actions of being. | | All substantial and accidental forms of things. | | |

---

### -> R U L E <-
Concerning a positive Being <term>Ens: an existing entity</term> in respect to a privative <note>A "privative" refers to the absence of a quality, like blindness being the absence of sight.</note> or less positive one, the conclusion is always affirmative; conversely, it is negative regarding the privative or less positive. Just as every positive thing is more perfect than a privative Being. Thus, every seeing thing is more perfect than a non-seeing thing, light than darkness, the just than the unjust. All Life is better, greater, more lasting, more powerful, better known, more desirable, more virtuous, truer, and more glorious than death and privation.

### -> *Axioms.* <-
1. Truth is not to be rejected because of the difficulty or conflict of arguments.
2. Truth gives birth to hatred. <note>A reference to the proverb "Truth begets hatred, flattery begets friends."</note>
3. Truth eventually rises above all adversity.
4. Truth is to be sought in all things.
5. Truth is the friend of simplicity.

# CHAPTER IX.

->On the Principle of Glory.<-

Glory is a general, primitive, and necessary principle, because without glory all things would be exposed to labors, reproaches, and punishments. Glory is defined as the reason by which all things are glorious in their own way—that is, all things glory in their own end <note>Latin: *finis*; the purpose or goal of a thing's existence</note> and in their own rest. Others define Glory more strictly as the praise celebrated by the mouths of many good people, occurring when someone possesses, or is thought to possess, what many or all upright people desire. This latter definition is spoken only regarding human beings; the former is more universal and encompasses all things.

It is divided into the Adequate and the Inadequate; or into the Uncreated and the Created. The former is, in the ultimate sense, God the Blessed, toward whom all things are ordered and whom all things desire as their ultimate end. The latter is the delight and the enjoyment of the good in which each thing finds rest: just as a stone rests and glories in its stillness once it has reached the center <note>referring to the Aristotelian idea that heavy objects seek the center of the earth as their natural place of rest</note>; any mineral in its appropriate mine; a tree in its fruiting; a plant in its possession of seeds, flowers, and leaves; an animal in the state of conserving its life and propagating its species; the wealthy man in his riches; and in splendor and light, by reason of which that which was first hidden appears with praise.

Furthermore, Created Glory is either True or False. True glory is again either Perpetual or temporal. Perpetual glory is in the world to come and Eternal Life for the Blessed and the Angels. Temporal glory is in this world, subject to changes, and is either Essential or Active. False or "vain" glory arises either by reason of the thing from which it is taken, or by reason of the one who bestows it, or by the occasion of the one who usurps it for himself. It is false by reason of the thing if the object is non-existent, or if it is shameful and more worthy of reproach. It is false by reason of the bestower if, for example, a demon, a wicked man, or a criminal should offer it. It is false by reason of the recipient if he is unworthy, if he usurps it without merit, if he ambitiously seeks it out of pride, or if he ascribes it to his own merits rather than to Divine grace.

---

A final bracket on the right points to the conclusion "Ergo Gloriosa sunt."</image-desc>

| Major (The Rule) | Minor (The Case) | Conclusion |
|-------|-------|-----------|
| **Everything** <br> [represented by the letters] B. M. D. P. S. V. V. V. G. <note>These letters likely refer to the "Dignities" or attributes in the Lullian combinatorial system: Goodness, Greatness, Duration, Power, Wisdom, Will, Virtue, Truth, and Glory.</note> <br> which affects one with joy; <br> which is Magnificent; <br> which is honorable or honor-bringing; <br> which is desired as an end; <br> by which we are led to a perfect state worthy of man; <br> which is enjoyment produced by labor; <br> which rejoices in the quiet possession of just goods; <br> which confers happiness; <br> which prevails with quiet power; <br> which enjoys the truth once found; <br> which is content with its lot; <br> which operates virtuously; <br> which shines with truth; <br> which is preserved in its own being; <br> which excels in vital motion; <br> which enjoys the rest of its own nature; <br> **is Glorious.** | **But** <br> Blessedness, both natural and supernatural, is such. <br> All celebrity of fame and name is such. <br> All true dignity is such. <br> Every good is such. <br> All wisdom and science is such. <br> Every distinguished deed is such. <br> All just riches are such. <br> All honor and tranquil dignity that lasts securely is such. <br> All sovereignty produced by well-performed deeds is such. <br> Every new discovery of things is such. <br> Everyone ready to sustain prosperity and adversity alike is such. <br> Every laudable endeavor is such. <br> All sciences and disciplines are such. <br> All nine universal subjects and those things annexed to them are such. <br> Every living thing is such. <br> The whole of nature, which has as its end the enjoyment and conservation of its own being, is such. | **Therefore, these things are Glorious.** |

Finally, all those who have alacrity in their face, gladness in their mind, jubilation in their heart, solace in their reasoning, joy in their soul, and pleasantness in their spirit, possess the true praise of glory.

->Axioms.<-

1. All happiness and blessedness is better, more sufficient, more durable, more powerful, more well-known, more desirable, more virtuous, truer, and more glorious than mere delight and joy; because happiness is more perfect than delight, since the latter is something that follows as a consequence of happiness.

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,</header>

# PART III.

## Definition, Division, and Application of the Respective Principles.

### CHAPTER I.

->Concerning Difference, the most general Principle.<-

Having explained the absolute principles, we proceed to the exposition of the Respective principles, so that it may be known what benefit can flow to us from them in our Art.

Lullus <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), the Majorcan philosopher who invented this system of logic.</note> distinguished these into three triangles; but we, neglecting those intricacies in favor of methodical traditions, will teach in this place those things which can both promote the Art without confusion and lead to greater ease. There are, therefore, nine Respective Principles, as appeared from the preceding sections: *Difference*, *Concordance*, *Opposition*, *Beginning*, *Middle*, *End*, *Majority*, *Equality*, and *Minority*, which we display by these signs: = . ♡ . ∞ . α . ☉ . ω . M. Æ. Min. They are called "Respective" because intrinsically, regarding their relation, they speak to another thing and imply a certain plurality. But all these things will appear more clearly in the following expositions.

| Respective Principles. | Alphabet. |
|-----------------------|-------------|
| Difference. | = |
| Concordance. | ♡ |
| Opposition or Contrariety. | ∞ |
| Beginning. | α |
| Middle. | ☉ |
| End. | ω |
| Majority. | M |
| Equality. | Æ |
| Minority. | Min. |

Difference is the first most general, substantial, primitive, and necessary respective principle, including all differences, because without this all things would be the same, all things one, all things indistinct, which is impossible. Indeed, Difference is ordained not only to rescue the variety of Metaphysical concepts from confusion, but also to rightly distinguish substance from substance, genera from species, individuals from species, the classes of natural things, one from another, and each from each. However, distinction and difference relate to each other as the higher to the lower <note>In logic, "distinction" is a broader category than "difference."</note>; for wherever there is difference, there is distinction, but not vice versa. For between "quidditative" concepts <note>Concepts relating to the "what-ness" or essence of a thing.</note> there is distinction, yet difference has no place there. Hence, in the Art, it is defined as clarity without any confusion of concepts; for by this, goodness is distinguished from magnitude, duration, power, and whatever else is referred to them.

<margin>3. Species of difference.</margin>
There are, moreover, three species of Difference. The first is between *Corporeal and Corporeal*, such as between a man and a horse; between a *Plant and a Stone*, or between *Heaven and Earth*. The second is between *Corporeal and Intellectual*; such as between a man and an Angel, between soul and body, between form and matter, or between the exterior and interior senses. The third is between *Intellectual and Intellectual*; such as between soul and soul, Angel and Angel, between Angel and soul, or between God, the Angel, and the soul of man.

<margin>The origin of differences.</margin>
These species must be explained through a ninefold series of distinctions to avoid all confusion; before we assign these, two things must be presupposed. The first of these is that every distinction or difference takes its beginning from the "extremes" <note>The two terms or objects being compared.</note> of denomination or specification. For if the Extremes are "beings of reason" <note>Mental constructs rather than physical objects.</note>, then it is necessary that a distinction of reason intervenes; if it occurs formally between the Extremes, a formal distinction will immediately arise; and if the Extremes are real, the distinction is real; and so it must be determined for the remaining degrees of distinctions.

<margin>Difference is not joined to another without a change of the whole.</margin>
The second thing to be presupposed is that no greater difference can be added to a Difference or distinction without that distinction itself being immediately varied. Indeed, it is established from Aristotle that the species of a definition and the forms of things are compared to numbers; just as a unit cannot be added to a number without an essential variation of that number, so also no greater or lesser difference can be added to a difference and distinction without a variation of the Extremes, as will later be evident from practice.

<summary>This page introduces the nine "Respective Principles" of the Lullian Art, focusing on "Difference" as the essential tool for distinguishing between substances, species, and concepts. It categorizes difference into three types: physical-physical, physical-intellectual, and intellectual-intellectual.</summary>
<keywords>Difference, Respective Principles, Ramon Llull, Aristotle, logic, classification, corporeal vs intellectual, formal distinction</keywords>

# *Composite Terms reducible to Difference, expressed in syllogistic form.*

<note>The following diagram represents a logical argument (syllogism) where the Major premises are linked to types of distinction, applied to specific subjects (Minor), leading to a Conclusion.</note>

**Major Premises:**
1. Everything that expresses a relation to another.
2. Everything that expresses a tendency toward another.
3. Everything that coalesces into one through disparate degrees.
4. Every mixture of many things.
5. Every Antipathy and Sympathy of things.
6. Everything that is separated from another by reality or by reason.
7. Everything disparate, whether in itself or in relation to another.
8. Everything that is distinguished by reality or by reason.
9. Everything that involves comparison.
10. Everything that is compared as dissimilar, whether in itself or in relation to another.
11. Everything that admits diversity.
12. Everything subject to division.
13. Everything that can be removed from another by reality or by reason.
14. Every composite thing.

**[The Middle Term]:**
**Differs by a Distinction, either:**
Reasoning <note>original: "Ratiocinante"; purely mental</note>, or Reasoned <note>original: "Ratiocinata"; based on the object</note>, Formal, Modal, Real, Numerical, Specific or Essential, Generic, By itself (subjectively whole), and By itself (objectively whole).

**But [Minor Premises]:**
**Such things are:**
1. A triangle ($\triangle$).
2. A globe or world sphere.
3. A circle with a central dot ($\odot$) <note>Often a symbol for the Sun or Gold</note>.
4. A square with a central dot ($\boxdot$).
5. A human figure.
6. A four-legged animal (lamb/sheep).
7. A flower or plant.
8. A pentagonal house or shield shape.
9. A 3x3 grid of nine dots.

**Conclusion:**
**Therefore.**

# E X P L A N A T I O N
## *Of the Nine Distinctions.*

<margin>Distinction of reason.</margin>
1. *A Distinction of Reasoning Reason* <note>original: "Rationis ratiocinantis"</note> occurs when, for example, the intellect is predicated identically of the intellect; for in this proposition: "The Divine Intellect is intellect," the intellect in the subject is distinguished from the intellect in the predicate by the pure operation of the intellect, which is the first degree of distinction.

<margin>Formal.</margin>
2. *A Formal Distinction* <note>original: "Distinctio Formalis"</note> is given when an addition to another varies the nature of the thing constituted, and it results when something is affirmed of one and denied of the other; and such a contradiction in the same thing is not of the name only, but of the name and the thing: for in divine matters, something is said of the Intellect that is not said of the Will; as the Father formally generates the Son by the intellect, not by the will; the nature of goodness is formally distinguished from the nature of magnitude, duration, power, etc. And a distinction of this kind is found not only between formalities, but also between intrinsic concepts, namely: between essence, matter, quiddity <note>the "what-ness" or essential nature of a thing</note>, simplicity, and existence.

3. *A Modal Distinction* <note>original: "Distinctio Modalis"</note> is when one Mode, added upon another, does not vary the nature of the thing constituted; such as finitude, excellence, or contingency; for men are indeed called finite, but they do not differ by their finitude; they are called excellent, yet they are not distinguished by their excellence.

<sig>F 2</sig>
<meta>Catchword: 4. Distin-</meta>

<summary>This page categorizes the vocabulary of "Difference" and provides a complex syllogistic diagram illustrating how various entities are distinguished. It begins a detailed explanation of the different types of logical distinctions, such as those of reason, form, and mode.</summary>
<keywords>logic, syllogism, distinction of reason, formal distinction, modal distinction, ontology, classification of terms, quiddity, essence</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic classification of "distinctions" (ways things differ) within the framework of the Great Art of Knowledge. It moves from metaphysical definitions to logical examples applied to theology.</meta>

# OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,

<margin>Real.</margin> 4. *Real Distinction* exists even in Divine matters, because the persons are truly and really distinguished among themselves; for the Father acts in respect to the Son, and the Son in respect to the Holy Spirit; for we presuppose these actions <note>original: "supponimus"; a technical term in scholastic logic regarding how a term stands for a thing</note>.

<margin>Numerical.</margin> 5. *Numerical Distinction* pertains to individuals; in Divine matters, this Distinction has no place, since in them there are only three numerical discretions which constitute a trinity <note>original: "numerum ternarium"</note>, and thus it differs from every other number in the external world. Indeed, individuals of a certain species do not differ in species, but in number.

<margin>Specific.</margin> 6. *Specific Distinction* or essential distinction is that which intervenes between animals of a different species; as there is a greater difference between Socrates and an Ox than between Socrates and Plato; for those things are said to have a different essence whose species are different.

<margin>Generic.</margin> 7. *Generic Distinction* is the name used when the distinction falls between those things which differ by a subordinate genus; for an Angel and a Lion differ more than a Lion and a donkey. But a Lion and a donkey differ in species, therefore an Angel and a Lion differ in genus—not the most general genus, but a subordinate one, insofar as both the Angel and the Lion are substances.

<margin>Subjectively in their entirety.</margin> 8. *Distinction subjectively in their entirety* <note>original: "se totis subjectiva"</note> occurs when a thing is distinguished by the most general genus alone, such as the ten categories <note>The "Prædicamenta" of Aristotle, like Substance, Quality, Quantity, etc.</note>, which are distinguished by the most general genus according to their entire underlying subject; because whatever things differ by a subordinate genus agree in the most general genus, namely in substance; but those which differ by the most general genus (or "subjectively in their entirety") do not agree in any limited thing, but only in transcendental being.

<margin>Objectively in their entirety.</margin> 9. *Distinction objectively in their entirety* <note>original: "se totis objectiva"</note> exists between individuated differences; for all things outside the predication of "being" admit this, except those which do not formally include "being"; such are the ultimate individual differences which are not found in all individuals of the species, such as "Peter-ness" <note>original: "Petreitas"</note>, "Paul-ness" <note>original: "Pauleitas"</note>, etc. Just as these terms merely express non-identity, so they cannot be subjected to some third thing; for if they included "being" in their own definitions, those definitions would necessarily be distinguished by their own further definitions, and thus there would be a progress to infinity, which is absurd.

And these are the distinctions which measure the entire breadth of differing things, so that there is nothing in the nature of things that does not fall under one or more of these distinctions; they are, however, collected here in such an order that the first is the smallest of all, the second greater than the first; the third greater than the second; the fourth greater than the third; the fifth greater than the fourth; the sixth greater than the fifth; the seventh greater than the sixth; the eighth greater than the seventh; the ninth greater than the eighth.

---

### E X A M P L E  I.
#### *On the Distinction of Reason.*

Wherever beings of reason <note>mental constructs rather than physical things</note> intervene between Extremes, there a distinction of reason is given.
But in God, beings of reason intervene as far as we are concerned, as in this proposition: "The Intellect of God is intellect," and similar ones.
Therefore, in God, a distinction of reason is given, as far as we are concerned.
For a distinction between subject and predicate is given even in identical propositions, according to the testimony of Aristotle.

### E X A M P L E  II.
#### *On the Formal Distinction.*

Wherever different formalities are given, there a formal distinction is given.
But in God, different formalities are given.
Therefore, etc. The major premise is certain. The minor is proved:
Wherever concepts that are formally different are given, there different formalities are given.
But in the intellect and will of God, different formal concepts are given: for the Father generates not by will but by intellect; Goodness is not formally Greatness, Duration, Power, and so with the rest.
Therefore.

### E X A M P L E  III.
#### *On the Modal Distinction.*

Whenever something added to another does not vary the definition of the thing constituted in its direct parts, then a modal distinction is given.
But when, for example, a union is added to the parts, then it does not vary the definition of the thing constituted, as above.
Therefore, a modal distinction is given.

### E X A M P L E  IV.
#### *On the Real Distinction.*

Wherever several established and incommunicable things are given, there a real distinction is given.
But in God, three established and incommunicable things are given: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.
Therefore.

<margin>Which distinctions apply to God.</margin>
And this fourfold system of distinction applies not only to created things, but also to God; however, the other five distinctions—Numerical, Generic, Specific, subjectively in their en-

subjective, and entirely subjective, do not apply to God, but such things belong to the <term>predicaments</term><note>The "predicaments" refer to Aristotle’s ten categories of being, such as substance, quality, and quantity.</note>.

## E X A M P L E V.
### Concerning the Entirely Subjective Distinction.

Wherever a division of a being into its lower parts occurs, there exists a real, entirely subjective distinction.
But whenever being is divided into substance and <term>Accident</term><note>An "accident" is a property that can change without destroying the essence of the thing, like the color of a shirt.</note>, there Being is divided into its lower parts.
Therefore.

## E X A M P L E VI.
### Concerning the Generic Distinction.

Wherever there is a division of a <term>most general genus</term><note>The highest category in a hierarchy, such as "Substance."</note> into its subordinate genera, there exists a real generic distinction.
But whenever substance is divided into animate and inanimate body, into sensible and insensible living things, into rational and irrational animal, the division is of a most general genus into its subordinate genera.
Therefore, there also exists a real generic distinction.

## E X A M P L E VII.
### Concerning the Specific Distinction.

Whenever a division of species into individuals occurs, a real specific distinction necessarily results.
But whenever "human" is divided into Peter and Paul, there occurs a division of species into individuals.
Therefore.
Wherein you see that the difference between these proposed distinctions is greater the further they recede from a <term>Being of Reason</term><note>An "Ens rationis" or Being of Reason is something that exists only in the mind, like a square circle or a memory, rather than in physical reality.</note>, as we taught a little while ago. Note also that the predicate must always be broader than the subject, or at least equivalent to it, to constitute a definitive and <term>apodictic</term><note>A statement that is demonstrably or necessarily true.</note> proposition.

<margin>N. B. <note>Latin: Nota Bene, "Note well."</note></margin>
Having established the universal accounts of distinctions, nothing remains except that, descending a little more into particulars, we teach how to apply the various terms of distinctions used in the <term>Schools</term><note>Refers to "Scholasticism," the medieval and early modern method of learning used in universities.</note> to our Art; if anyone masters these thoroughly, I would dare to say that nothing in resolving any questions, even the most difficult, is denied to him, mindful of that saying worn thin in the Schools: *he who distinguishes well, teaches well.* <note>original: "qui benè distinguit, benè docet."</note> Therefore, we shall speak first of distinctions, and afterwards also of division, proceeding with everything in <term>syllogistic form</term><note>A logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises (e.g., All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal).</note>, so that the beginner may find a vast forest of argumentation ready-made.

A threefold distinction is given in the Schools: Real, of Reason; and this latter is again twofold: of <term>reason reasoning</term> or of <term>reason reasoned</term>. <note>These are technical terms: "Reason reasoning" (ratiocinantis) is a distinction made by the mind with no basis in reality; "Reason reasoned" (ratiocinatæ) is a distinction the mind makes based on an actual complexity in the object.</note> Let us define each one.

## E X A M P L E I.
### Of the Real Distinction.

<margin>A real distinction is when one can exist without the other.</margin>
Whenever the extremes are so constituted that one can exist without the other, and one can be separated from the other, those are certainly distinguished really.
But Soul and body; Peter and Paul; Substance and common Accidents are so constituted that one can exist without the other, and one can be separated from the other.
Therefore they are distinguished really.

## E X A M P L E II.
### Of the Modal Distinction.

<margin>Example of a modal distinction.</margin>
Whenever a certain proximate entity is understood as ultimately determining a subject, without which it cannot exist, then a Modal distinction is given.
But "Sitting" in respect to the "one sitting" is the ultimate determinative entity of the subject, without which it cannot exist insofar as it is in such a posture.
Therefore, between the one sitting and the act of sitting, a Modal distinction is given.

## E X A M P L E III.
### Of the Distinction of Reason Reasoning.

Whenever the intellect divides a most simple thing into many without a foundation for inventing a distinction, there is a distinction of Reason reasoning, or of "pure reason," as the Schools say.
But when it is understood that the same thing is distinct from itself, a most simple thing is divided into many without a foundation.
Therefore, when the same thing is understood to be distinct from itself, then a distinction of Reason reasoning is given.

## E X A M P L E IV.
### Of the Distinction of Reason Reasoned or Formal.

Whenever the intellect distinguishes a most simple thing into many with a foundation, then a distinction of Reason reasoned is given, or as it pleases <term>Scotus</term><note>Duns Scotus (c. 1266–1308), a major medieval philosopher who developed the concept of the "formal distinction."</note>, a "formal" distinction.

<summary>This page outlines the various types of logical and metaphysical distinctions—Real, Modal, and of Reason—using syllogistic examples to demonstrate how to categorize the relationships between objects, properties, and concepts.</summary>
<keywords>logic, Scholasticism, real distinction, modal distinction, Duns Scotus, syllogism, substance and accident, genus and species</keywords>

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE

But when we distinguish in God Goodness, Greatness, Wisdom, Power, Duration, and Intellect, we are distinguishing a most simple thing <note>In Scholastic theology, "simplicity" means God is not made of parts.</note> into many parts with a foundational reason.
Therefore, between Goodness, Greatness, Duration, Power, Wisdom, and Intellect in Divine matters, there is a "Distinction of Reasoned Reason" <note>original: "distinctio Rationis ratiocinatæ" — a distinction that exists in the mind but is based on the reality of the object.</note> or a formal distinction.

From these arise various terms of distinction in the Schools <note>Referring to Scholasticism, the medieval method of learning taught in universities.</note>, without the knowledge of which no one can excel in Philosophical study. So that nothing may be lacking from our Art, I shall explain them briefly. It is necessary in every science that, given a certain proposition which has a double meaning, we unravel it through the following terms.

---

Something is distinguished, first:
{
**Materially.** When a certain Predicate is said to belong to something by reason of the subject in comparison to the matter; as in "the white thing is sweet." <note>The sweetness belongs to the object (the matter), not the color white itself.</note>
**Formally.** When a certain Predicate belongs to a certain form, either by way of the form or by its shape; as in "white light scatters the sight."
}

Something is distinguished, 2.
{
**Reduplicatively.** When in a proposition only the form is considered.
**Specifically.** When only the matter is considered; as in "The Musician is a blacksmith." For it is true that the Musician, insofar as he is a Musician—whether specifically or materially—is a blacksmith. Reduplicatively or formally, it is false: because a blacksmith can exist without Music.
}

Something is distinguished, 3.
{
**Formally.** When something affects another through its own being; as heat does by informing a subject with its own warmth.
**Efficiently.** When it affects a subject not immediately through its being, but by means of something else; as fire makes a hand hot, not by placing its own essence into the hand, but through the heat produced in the hand.
}

Something is distinguished, 4.
{
**Formally.** When a Being contains the same form which is in the effect produced by itself; as happens in "Univocal causes." <note>Causes that produce an effect of the same nature as themselves.</note>
**Eminently, or Virtually.** When an Agent does not have the form within itself which is in the product, but contains it by power; as pepper heats the stomach, in which, however, no form of heat is visible in the pepper itself.
}

Something is distinguished, 5.
{
**In the signified act.** <note>original: "In actu signato"</note> When only the boundary of an action is signified; as happens when I define the division of a thing, it is called division in the signified act.
**In the exercised act.** <note>original: "In actu exercito"</note> When one proceeds to practice; as when I actually divide a whole, then it is called an exercised act; and so for all others.
}

Something is distinguished, 6.
{
**In Act.** When a thing exists actually and simply.
**In Potential.** When it is in the process of becoming—that is, it is able to be.
}

Something is distinguished, 7.
{
**Adequately.** When it is distinguished according to everything that a certain thing contains: thus God is the adequate object of Theology.
**Inadequately.** When only partially; as the Incarnation of Christ is an inadequate <note>Meaning "incomplete" or "partial" in this context.</note> object of Theology.
}

Something is distinguished, 8.
{
**Collectively.** When the parts are taken together.
**Distributively.** When they are taken one by one.
}
As in:
{
Matter and form are said to cause only distributively, not collectively.
}

Something is distinguished, 9.
{
**Directly.**
**Indirectly.**
}
As in:
{
The Intellect knows being directly, but negations indirectly. In Morals, the will desires the delight of an evil act directly, but the malice indirectly.
}

{
**Extensively.**
**Intensively.**
}
As in:
{
If someone studies without great effort for an hour, and another for a quarter-hour with great effort; the latter studies more intensively, the former more extensively.
}

These terms allow a student to analyze complex propositions where a single subject might have multiple, overlapping definitions.</summary>

And since every distinction arises from the division of things: First, the Divisions of things must be examined, so that you may have a complete knowledge of real Differences, which we set forth here: for without an exact knowledge of these, you will labor in Philosophy in vain.

# OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,

### III.

<margin>Of a whole into its parts, real.</margin>
**Whenever** a division of a whole into its parts occurs, then a real difference necessarily results.
**But** whenever a body—for example, a human one—is divided into head, neck, arms, hands, and feet, a division of a whole into parts occurs.
**Therefore** whenever a body—for example, a human one—is divided into head, neck, etc., then a real difference necessarily results between the parts.

### IV.

<margin>Of a cause into its effects, real.</margin>
**Whenever** a division of an Efficient Cause <note>The "Efficient Cause" is the agent or force that actually brings something into being, such as a carpenter making a chair.</note> into its effects occurs, then a real difference necessarily results.
**But** whenever the various effects of God, an Angel, a Human, the Sun, or the Elements are recognized, then a division of the Efficient Cause into its effects occurs.
**Therefore** whenever the various effects of God, an Angel, a Human, etc., are recognized, then a real difference necessarily results between them.

### V.

<margin>Of a Mixture into its Elements, real.</margin>
**Whenever** a division of a Mixture into those things from which it is made occurs, a real difference necessarily results there.
**But** whenever any plant is resolved into the four elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note>, there is a division of a Mixture into those things from which it is made.
**Therefore** whenever any plant or Mineral is resolved into the four elements, a real difference necessarily results between them.

But let these suffice for the Beginner <note>original: "Tyroni"</note> to understand the method. Indeed, you will proceed at an equal pace through the remaining series of divisions, which I hope will be by no means difficult given these preceding examples; note here that two members always occur in every division, the first of which is always placed as the subject in the Major premise, and the second in the Minor premise, as is evident from these five examples.

---

## CHAPTER II.
### Concerning the Relative Principle of Concordance.

<margin>Concordance proceeds by the opposite path to difference.</margin>
Concordance is a Relative Principle that is entirely general in its coordination, primitive, and necessary; for without it, all things would be discordant, dissolved, and unsuitable, which cannot be said; since nothing is found in the nature of things which does not agree with something in some respect. First, there is Concordance in God through the Concordance of the Divine Persons in the unity of Essence; it is in the Angels through diverse operations agreeing in one essence; and it is also in man, in beasts, in plants, elements, the Heavens, Minerals, and artificial things, as will be seen later.

Moreover, Concordance is divided by the same division by which we divided Difference; yet by an opposite reasoning; for just as one thing, or a whole, is divided into distinct parts; so distinct parts are again reduced to one thing or a whole, in which they are said to agree; so that just as one divided thing is distinguished into divided parts, so the divided parts agree in the one or the divided whole; as can be seen in the following diagram.

->*They agree, therefore, as:*<-

| | |
|---|---|
| 1. Species in a Genus. | 8. Effects in causes. |
| 2. Individuals in a species. | 9. Accidents in a subject. |
| 3. Parts in a whole. | 10. Operations in a Power. |
| 4. Powers in a substance. | 11. Simple things in a Composite. |
| 5. Compared things in absolutes. | 12. Continuing things in a Continuum. |
| 6. Various attributes in one. | 13. Components in a Composite, etc. |
| 7. Simple things in a Mixture. | |

->*Whence one must argue thus:*<-

### I.

<margin>Concordance of species in a genus.</margin>
**Whenever** there is a uniting of species in some genus, then a concordance of diverse things in one necessarily results.
**But** whenever a human and a beast are traced back to "animal," there is a concordance of species in a genus.
**Therefore** whenever a human and a beast are traced back to "animal," then a concordance of diverse things in one necessarily results.

### II.

<margin>Of species in individuals.</margin>
**Whenever** individuals are known to agree in one species, then a concordance of diverse individuals in one necessarily results.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

# or COMBINATORICS, BOOK II.

But whenever Peter and Paul are recognized to meet in "man," or in human nature, then individuals are recognized to meet in one species <note>original: "species"; here meaning a logical category or class of being</note>.
Therefore, whenever Peter and Paul are thought to meet in "man," then necessarily there results a concordance of diverse individuals in one.

A Concordance of diverse parts in one results.
But whenever hands, feet, and head are united to the whole or the entire body, then the parts meet in the whole.
Therefore, whenever hands, feet, and head are united to the whole or the entire body, then necessarily there results a Concordance of parts in the whole, the entire, or the integrated one.

<margin>Of parts in the whole.</margin>
### III.
Whenever diverse parts are thought to meet in a whole, then necessarily you shall proceed in the same way regarding the remaining types of Concordance in order.

## Compound Terms reducible to Concordance under a syllogistic form.

<margin>The Same.</margin>
### I.
Wherever there is a specific or generic Concordance, there is a generic or specific identity.

But whenever many individuals meet in one species, or many species meet in one genus, there is given either a generic or specific Concordance.

Therefore, whenever many individuals meet in one species, or many species meet in one genus, there is given either a generic or specific identity.

**Simple Terms.**
The Same.
Similar.
Equal.
Near.
Proportioned.
Peer (or Like).
Joined.
One.

</insert>

<margin>Similar.</margin>
### II.
Wherever Concordance intervenes between any parties, there is necessarily given some similarity of character or temperament.

But this occurs in Friendship, in which "like rejoices in like" <note>A common Latin proverb: "similis simili gaudet"</note>.

Therefore, in Friendship there is given a similarity of character or temperament. Therefore, Concordance.

<margin>From the Equal.</margin>
### III.
Wherever there is a fitting Concordance, there is equality and parity.

But in a Democratic State there is a fitting Concordance, by which all things are carried out by common votes and ballots.

Therefore, in a Democratic State there is equality and parity.

<margin>From Harmony.</margin>
### IV.
Wherever consonant things are aptly and skillfully mixed with dissonant things, there is a harmonic Concordance.

But in every harmonic proportion, whether true or analogous, consonant things are aptly mixed with dissonant things.

Therefore, in every harmonic proportion, whether true or analogous, there is harmonic Concordance.

To these are recalled all things that signify union, such as Marriages; those that signify agreement, such as pacts and contracts; those that signify parity, such as friendship and peace; all Abstracts, univocal terms, and universals; all universal propositions; all unities or gatherings, such as an Assembly, a City, a people, or the common folk.

---

## From which you shall argue thus:

### 1.
Wherever there is a union of two individuals, there is concordance.

<margin>From marriage.</margin>
Marriage is the union of male and female in an inseparable Society.

Therefore, it is concordance.

### 2.
Wherever there is mutual agreement, there is concordance.

<margin>From Contracts.</margin>
In pacts and contracts there is mutual agreement.

Therefore, it is concordance.

### 3.
Wherever there is parity, there is concordance.

<margin>From friendship.</margin>
In friendship there is a parity of reciprocal benevolence.

Therefore, in friendship there is concordance.

### 4.
<margin>From abstracts.</margin>
Wherever an abstraction is made by the intellect from lower things, there is concordance.

But this occurs in universals <note>General concepts that apply to many things, like "redness" or "humanity"</note>.

Therefore, in all universals there is a concordance of the lower things with the higher.

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING

## Chapter III.
### *On the Relative Principle of Contrariety or Opposition.*

Contrariety is an entirely general, primitive, and necessary principle to which all other contrarieties are reduced. Without it, all things would be uniform and identical, which is an impossible claim. Indeed, the "Machine of the World" <note>A common Baroque metaphor for the universe as a complex, divinely-designed mechanism.</note> consists of contraries: of strife and friendship, of peace and discord, of the dissonant and the consonant. No thing in the nature of reality can escape this contrariety. They are also called "opposites," as if placed against one another; these are sometimes distinct in substance, sometimes only in reality and reason, and occasionally by reason alone, as was discussed above. Here it must be noted that all things were made by nature in identity, but nothing in opposition. Each thing made and established by nature exists in itself, but nothing exists in its opposite. God established each thing in its own proper and natural place, in which it is stabilized and born to be. That which is natural is identical to itself; however, that which is violent and against nature is judged to be an opposite. For example: for fire to tend upward is natural to it, but to tend downward is violent. Conversely, for the Earth to tend downward is natural, but upward is violent and against nature. Now, the "natural" and the "violent" are opposites. From this it follows that all things, as situated by nature, exist in identity, peace, love, and concord, and nothing is

<margin>Opposition comes from the intellect.</margin>
opposite. If things are considered strictly by their nature, there is no opposition; therefore, all opposition arises from the intellect. For that which is natural to things is their identity; but that which brings force upon them, and which is beyond nature, is their opposition. Nature is "natural" to all things, establishing each in its appropriate place; she is violent to none, nor does she displace things from their seats. The intellect alone brings force to all things, displacing all things from their seats. For all things that exist outside the intellect possess this peace and identity; where they are distant from one another and separated in reality, once they are drawn in by the intellect, they are joined within it and become present simultaneously. This conjunction and simultaneous opposition is their 

<margin>The immortality of the intellect.</margin>
difference and opposition. Indeed, from this indivisible nature of the intellect, its very immortality may be concluded. Thus the intellect divides all things, distinguishes them, separates one from another, and beautifully unfolds the confused and indistinct chaos of things. It is the judge of the discrimination of all things and of every difference and opposition—the light of all Nature. Because the intellect, due to its restless nature, cannot receive two things simultaneously <note>The author argues that the mind processes things one by one, creating "difference" by comparing them.</note>, the distinction, difference, and number of all things are born within it. For it does not apprehend a multitude as a single multitude (that is, the whole at once), but as many units. The logic of a single multitude is one thing; the logic of many units is another. And after all things have passed through the intellect and been stored in the memory, they are then restored to the original "chaos" of nature through the species <note>term: species (mental images or concepts used in medieval/renaissance psychology)</note> we have conceived.

<margin>The fourfold logic of opposites.</margin>
The Dialecticians <note>Logicians, specifically those following the Aristotelian tradition.</note> establish only four species of opposites: Contraries, Contradictories, Sub-contraries, and Subalterns. We, however, in this Art, extend the general name of "Opposition" to all things; I mean to all those things which are set against each other in any way—whether against nature or the intellect—and which are referred to one another in any manner, being placed against each other by their origin. For although many beings seem to have been granted no natural contrariety or combativeness, they are nevertheless called "opposites" when compared to one another for the sake of mutual relation. For example, unity and number are neither contraries nor combatants; indeed, unity is a part of every number. No contrary is the beginning or part of its own contrary. Yet, unity and number are called two opposites, like extremes turned toward themselves and looking at one another. You should know that the same is to be felt regarding the intellect and nature, act and potency and its object, beginning and end, matter and form, and similar pairs, as is evident in the following diagram.

### Table of Opposition.

| Opposites | | Opposites | |
|----------|----------|----------|----------|
| *Perfect* | *Imperfect* | *Perfect* | *Imperfect* |
| Light | Darkness | Truth | Falsehood |
| Act <note>Actualization or reality</note> | Potency <note>Potential or capacity</note> | Whiteness | Blackness |
| Form | Matter | Unity | Division |
| Day | Night | Nearness | Distance |

<margin>The art of opposites is most useful.</margin>
Whoever learns the art of opposites will easily, through a certain analogy, reduce the entire logic of Opposites to only two categories. For of any two opposites, one is more perfect, the other less perfect. Therefore, all things that display a certain perfection are to be placed on one side, such as Light, Act, Form, Day, Truth...

<summary>This page explores the philosophical principle of Opposition, arguing that while nature exists in a state of identity and peace, the human intellect creates "opposition" by comparing and distinguishing things. It provides a table classifying various concepts into "Perfect" and "Imperfect" opposites.</summary>
<keywords>Contrariety, Opposition, Intellect vs Nature, Identity, Dialectics, Table of Opposites, Metaphysics, Logic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Art of Combination" (Ars Combinatoria), specifically focusing on how opposites are categorized into two primary groups: the perfect and the imperfect.</meta>

# OR OF COMBINATIONS, BOOK II.

Truth, Whiteness, Unity, Nearness; on the opposite side, however, are the imperfect things, such as Darkness, Potentiality <note>original: "Potentia" - here meaning the state of being unfinished or having only the possibility of being, rather than the power of action</note>, Matter, Night, Falsehood, etc. In this manner, all perfect things are to be considered under the classification of one opposite, while imperfect things are likewise considered under the classification of the other; for in this way, all opposites are viewed under only two headings. For example, Light and Darkness are opposed to one another; and Light indeed belongs to the genus of perfection, while Darkness—the deprivation of perfect light—exists among the number of imperfect things, to which all things can be reduced according to a certain analogy. For just as Light is act <note>original: "actus" - the state of being fully realized or active</note> and darkness is potentiality, so every form is a kind of Light and act, while all matter is a kind of darkness and potentiality. In this way, Day can be called Light, act, and form, the opposite of which is Night, which can be considered under the classification of darkness, potentiality, and matter. In no different a way, Truth is a kind of light, act, form, and day, the opposite of which is falsehood, which holds the classification of darkness, potentiality, matter, and night. One may reason in the same way concerning countless other things, as is evident in the diagram placed above.

### COROLLARY.

From these classifications of opposites is born the **Logical Tetragonism** <note>A "four-angled" or square-based logical system used to analyze propositions</note>, by which individual opposites are enumerated through a fourfold kind of affirmation or negation; and thus affirmation can be expressed through negation, and negation through affirmation. This is a beautiful invention for expressing logical propositions in various ways; we have therefore decided to clarify it with several Examples (having first set down the axioms).

1. Whatever can be said of one of the opposites can also be said of the other, by the opposite reasoning.
2. Whatever things are different in substance are varied in both reality and reasoning.
3. Of whatever things the reality is varied, the reasoning is also diverse.
4. Of whatever things the reasoning is unique, the reality and substance are one.
5. Whatever things are joined in reality must also be joined in substance.
6. Those things whose substances are joined through a modal union are distinct in both reality and reasoning.

## Example from Morals.

<margin>Each item is to be read in transverse order according to the 4 Columns.</margin>

| | | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Everything praiseworthy is worthy of praise. | 1. And unworthy of blame. | 1. But everything blameworthy is unworthy of praise. | 1. And worthy of blame. |
| 2. Virtue is Virtue to the good and the diligent. | 2. But vice to the wicked. | 2. But vice is Virtue to the wicked. | 2. But vice to the good. |
| 3. The diligent and upright choose virtue. | 3. And flee from vices. | 3. But the base avoid virtue. | 3. And pursue vice. |
| 4. Knowledge is a friend to the Learned. | 4. An enemy to the Unlearned. | 4. Ignorance is a friend to the ignorant. | 4. An enemy to the Learned. |

---

In no different a way will you be able to proceed in all things opposed by any reasoning whatsoever. But so that you may see a more brilliant specimen of this matter, it seemed best to place here diagrams of proper opposites specific to each science, so that it may become clear how they can then be applied in acquiring a Universal knowledge of things.

# THE ART OF COMBINATION, BOOK II.

### Examples of Opposites in Metaphysics, and first,

#### Concerning Being, and Non-Being, or nothingness.

<margin>Being.</margin>
Everything that can be conceived by the intellect either is, or is not; therefore, Being is the most well-known of all things, while non-being is the most unknown. That Being is the most well-known to the intellect is proven thus:

1. That which is conceived first by the intellect as the most well-known is that which has nothing prior to it and nothing simpler.
But nothing is prior to or simpler than Being.
Therefore, Being is first conceived by the intellect as the most well-known.

<margin>Non-Being.</margin>
You shall prove the major premise through its opposite, *Non-Being*, in this manner:
If anything prior and simpler were conceived by the intellect as the most well-known, that other thing would be nothing else than non-being.
But it is impossible for that which does not exist to be prior and simpler.
Therefore, that which is first and simplest conceived by the intellect as the most well-known cannot be anything other than Being, and least of all, non-being.

->Otherwise.<-

That is first conceived by the intellect which is the first principle of operations and properties, and possesses the act of existing.
But such is the essence proper to each thing.
Therefore.
You shall prove the major premise through its opposite, *Non-essence*, thus:
If the first thing conceived by the intellect is not the first principle of operations and properties, it will necessarily be a "nothing" possessing no act of existing.
But this is absurd.
Therefore. Nor will it be something posterior following the act of existing; because it cannot happen that what follows the act of existing is the first principle of operations, since that always presupposes a thing already having actual existence.

You see, therefore, how through the negative opposite, the affirmative opposite can be proven, and through the imperfect, the perfect can be proven in a certain universal and demonstrative way. Now let us see how we are able to demonstrate that Being is "one." Proceed thus.

<margin>The One.</margin>
2. Everything undivided in itself, and divided from every other thing, is one.
Every substantial Being is undivided from itself, and divided from every other thing.
Therefore, every substantial Being is one.
You shall prove the major premise through the opposite thus:
If everything undivided in itself and from every other thing is not one, then it would be simultaneously one and many.
But this is absurd. For it would be divided within itself, which is impossible in a substantial Being.
And it is proven:
Wherever there is an indivisibility of being, there is the "whatness" <note>Latin: *quidditas*, the essential nature of a thing</note> of the one.
But in a substantial Being, there is an indivisibility of being.
Therefore, there is the whatness of the one. Therefore, the one is necessarily indivisibility. (We are speaking here always of transcendental Being and the One) <note>Transcendental terms in medieval philosophy are those that apply to all things, such as Being, One, True, and Good.</note> For unity adds nothing above Being, except only the negation of division.

3. Another example through the *Infinite* and the *Finite*.
Everything that is infinite lacks every limit.
God lacks every limit.
Therefore, He is infinite.
The minor premise is proven through the opposite (*The Finite*).
If God did not lack every limit, then He would be limited.
But this is impossible.
Therefore, that which is infinite lacks every limit.

<margin>The Powerful.</margin>
4. Another through *Power* and *Impotence*.
Every powerful thing has the power for the act of being.
But every Being has the power for the act of being, which is its actual existence.
Therefore, every Being has the power for the act.
The major premise is proven through the opposite (*The Impotent*).
If every powerful thing does not have the power for the act, then the power which is not brought into act will be frustrated <note>meaning "in vain" or "pointless" in a philosophical sense</note>, and therefore impotent.
But this is absurd in Philosophy.
Therefore, every powerful thing has the power for the act. Therefore, it is not at all impotent.
But let these few examples out of many suffice; for in the remaining opposites, the method of proceeding is the same.

---

### Example of Opposites

#### In Theology.

<margin>One. Many.</margin>
Whatever is necessarily and essentially one, cannot be many.
But God is necessarily and essentially one.
Therefore, He cannot be many.

<summary>This page demonstrates the logical method of proving metaphysical and theological truths—such as the nature of Being, Unity, Infinity, and Power—by examining their opposites. It argues that fundamental concepts like "Being" are the most basic and "well-known" to the human mind because their negatives lead to logical absurdities.</summary>

# OF THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING,

<margin>*Eternal. Temporal.*</margin> Whatever is eternal cannot be temporal.
But God is eternal.
Therefore <note>The conclusion "God is not temporal" is implied by the logical structure.</note>.

<margin>*Uncreated. Created.*</margin> Whatever is uncreated cannot be created.
But God is uncreated.
Therefore.

<margin>*Infinite. Finite.*</margin> Whatever is infinite cannot be finite.
But God is infinite.
Therefore.

You will always prove the major premise through one of those things which are opposed in absolute terms, as was evident in the preceding Metaphysical Examples, and will be evident a little further below.

->*Or otherwise.*<-

<margin>*Cause of all things.*</margin> The cause of all things is from nothing <note>Meaning it is not caused by anything else; it is the First Cause.</note>.
But God is the cause of all things.
Therefore.

->*The Major Premise is Proved.*<-

If the cause of all things is not from nothing, then it is from something.
<margin>*Effect.*</margin> Therefore it is the effect of another cause, which is impossible.

But we shall pursue all these matters more extensively in *Combined Theology*; therefore, let it suffice to have shown the method of proceeding in a few instances.

### Example of Opposites
->*In Ethics.*<-

<margin>*Virtue. Vice.*</margin> Every Virtue is desirable.
No vice is desirable.
Therefore no vice is a virtue.

->*Otherwise.*<-

Every virtue is praiseworthy.
Justice is a virtue.
Therefore it is praiseworthy.

->*The Major Premise is Proved.*<-

If virtue is not praiseworthy, then it is blameworthy, therefore to be shunned, therefore a vice.
But this is absurd.
Therefore.

---

<margin>*The Mean. The Extreme.*</margin> Every mean <note>The "Golden Mean" or middle path.</note> is virtuous.
Liberality is the mean between greed and prodigality.
Therefore liberality is virtuous; because virtue consists in the middle.

<margin>*Abundant. Deficient.*</margin> All Extremes are vicious.
Everything abundant and everything deficient are extremes.
Therefore they are vicious.
Every good is to be embraced.
Therefore every evil is to be shunned. And so concerning the rest.

You should determine that you must proceed in no other way regarding the remaining opposites.

### Examples of Opposites
->*In Arithmetic & Geometry.*<-

<margin>*Unity. Number.*</margin> Every unity denotes indivisibility into many.
No number denotes indivisibility into many.
Therefore no number, as a multitude, is unity.

<margin>*Odd. Even.*</margin> Every odd number signifies inequality.
Every seven is odd.
Therefore every seven signifies inequality.
If not, then the odd would be even; which is impossible.

<margin>*Perpendicular. Oblique.*</margin> Every line forming two right angles on any plane is not oblique.
A certain line falling normally <note>At a right angle.</note> upon another is perpendicular, forming two right angles.
Therefore it is not oblique.

<margin>*Equidistant. Angular.*</margin> Every straight line not inclined toward another is equidistant from another equidistant line.
Every straight line forming a right angle with another is not inclined.
Therefore every angular straight line is equidistant <note>The author is likely referring to parallel lines intersected by a transversal at right angles.</note>.

# or COMBINATORICS, BOOK II.

<margin>The Curved. The Straight.</margin>
Every line not lying equally between its points is curved.
Every circular line does not lie equally between its points.
Therefore, every circular line is curved.

Innumerable similar examples could be demonstratively deduced from Arithmetic. But let these suffice. Let the reader see the rest in *Combined Arithmetic and Geometry*.

### From Music.

<margin>Consonance.</margin> <margin>Dissonance.</margin>
Every consonance is a proportioned and harmonious union of different voices falling pleasantly upon the ears.
No dissonance, as such, is a proportioned and harmonious union of voices.
Therefore, no dissonance is a consonance.

<margin>Low/Grave. High/Acute.</margin>
Every low or deep-toned sound <note>original: "barytonum"</note> relaxes.
Every high or sharp-toned sound <note>original: "oxytonum"</note> intends <note>stretches or increases tension</note>.
Therefore, a high-toned sound cannot be a deep-toned sound; or, the high cannot be the low.

->*Otherwise.*<-

Every deep-toned sound is a relaxation of sound.
Every *hypate* <note>The lowest note in ancient Greek musical scales</note> voice is deep-toned.
Therefore, every *hypate* voice is a relaxation of sound. If not, it would be simultaneously deep and high, which is impossible.

Know that you must proceed in exactly the same manner in matters of Astronomy, Medicine, Dialectics, and Rhetoric. Indeed, all these things will be demonstrated in their own time and place with innumerable examples, God willing.

You have here, Reader, a method and reasoning described in few words, by which you are equipped for reasoning with innumerable arguments from the Art of Opposites. Indeed, this Art of Opposites approaches with such great variety that it deserves to be called a certain "Dialectical Proteus" <note>Proteus was a Greek sea-god capable of changing into many forms; here it refers to the versatility of the logic</note>; and thus nothing seems denied to him who masters it in rightly proving all the dogmas of rational science.

---

## CHAPTER IV.
### On the Respective Principle of the Cause.

A Cause is the most general, primitive, and necessary principle, without which there would be no effect in the nature of things; it is that from which something comes to be, and in it are implicitly contained all generic, specific, and particular principles diffused throughout the parts of substance. This will be clear to anyone examining the order of nature; and to the cause itself are reduced all terms of "From Which" <note>original: "A Quo"</note>, such as the Agent, Power, and every causal reason: such as the Efficient, Formal, Material, and Final causes; all acts of origin, such as to begin, to commence, and every antecedent, precedent, and finally all the categories <note>original: "praedicamenta"</note>.

It must be noted in this place, however, that all causes are principles, but the reverse is not true, as is evident in the nine categories, which are certain principles, but the definition of "Cause" does not apply to them except by reason of the substance in which they exist. From this, these Rules result:

1. Concerning a cause in respect to its principle (simply speaking), the answer is always in the affirmative under the reasoning of greater perfection; conversely, it is always negative under the reasoning of lesser perfection. For example: Every Substance is better, greater, more durable, more powerful, more perfect, etc., than every accident <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a non-essential quality, like color or shape</note>, because it is the cause of all accidents in a threefold genus of cause: namely, Efficient, Material, and Final; as is evident regarding the Efficient in God and the Creature; and regarding the Formal in the Soul with respect to the body.

2. Every Final and Efficient cause is nobler and more perfect than its effect, because the cause is independent, while the effect is dependent and subordinate.

3. Every cause is prior to its effect; understand this fundamentally, not formally, since it is a relative term.

4. Every principle is first in its own kind.

Therefore, every Cause is first divided into the *Uncreated and the Created*. The former is God, the Best and Greatest, who is the cause of all things, either effectively or permissively, in whom all causes of Causes and the primordial types of Causes are enfolded. The Created cause is again manifold: Efficient, Formal, Material, Final, or Exemplary.

<sig>H</sig>
<meta>catchword: Efficient</meta>
