<keywords>syllogism, geometry, music theory, consonance, dissonance, barytone, oxytone, causality, substance, accident, efficient cause, formal cause, material cause, final cause, logic</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the systematic explanation of philosophical principles, specifically focusing on the four Aristotelian causes which were fundamental to seventeenth-century science and logic.</meta>

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE

*The Efficient Cause* is that by whose action an effect follows; just as fire, by heating water, renders it hot.

*The Created Efficient Cause* is further divided into either Universal or Particular. The former is the Sun, which, together with the planets, is the producing cause of all lower things. The Particular cause, however, is that which produces only one specific effect, such as a human begetting a human, a lion a lion, or a tree a tree; these are again subdivided as either essentially or accidentally subordinate. An *essentially subordinate cause* is one that cannot act without another cause; such as the Moon without the other planets, or a human without the Sun <note>In the cosmology of this period, the Sun was believed to provide the vital heat necessary for all biological reproduction.</note>. An *accidentally subordinate cause* is like the movement of a stone by means of a stick.

*The Formal Cause* is that which gives a thing its intrinsic being through itself; just as the soul is the cause of a living thing. It is distinguished from the Efficient and Final causes, which give being to a thing from the outside. It is either essential or accidental: the former applies to all substantial forms; the latter to accidental forms, such as active qualities.

*The Material Cause* is that which receives the form and preserves it, because—except for the rational soul—no thing has being in itself, but rather in another, namely in matter. Indeed, if the material subject is corrupted, the forms are also destroyed; they last only as long as they endure within the received matter. The same must be said of "accidents" <note>In philosophy, an 'accident' is a quality that can change without the thing itself ceasing to exist, like the color of a shirt.</note>, which can neither exist nor be preserved naturally without the "secondary matter" in which they inhere.

*The Final Cause* is that which incites and moves the agent to act, namely for the sake of something. Thus, Health moves the sick person to take medicine, as the cause of Health; likewise, the end <note>original: "finis" - meaning the goal or purpose.</note> of a building is a good and comfortable habitation.

Indeed, the final cause of judgment and of all Magistrates is Justice. This is again either Impulsive (intended so that good results may follow) or subordinate; as in the case of a beggar, who intends to get money, and through the money, bread.

*The Exemplary Cause*, according to the Platonists, is the idea in the Divine mind; it is also called an improper and metaphorical final cause.

There are also other subdivisions of causes: Into the *proximate cause*, such as the Sun; and the *remote cause*, such as a human in the order of generation. There is also the *cause in itself* <note>original: "per se"</note> and the *cause by accident* <note>original: "per accidens"</note>. A cause in itself produces an effect by its own power; a cause by accident produces it when joined to another, such as a tool in the hand of a Craftsman. This latter is manifold: arising from chance or fortune, or from occasion, or example. One cause is *in act* (actually functioning), another is *in potency* (having the power to cause, even if it does not actually do so); just as a Teacher can be a teacher even if he is not currently teaching, and so on for the rest.

Therefore, the four stated causes are the essential principles:
*Efficient cause*: From Which.
*Formal cause*: Through Which.
*Material cause*: In Which.
*Final cause*: On account of Which, and for the sake of which.

<meta>catchword: Terms</meta>

In this Combinatory Abacus, one must only select those terms which properly fit the individual subjects: for Genus and Species, insofar as they are physical principles, do not apply to God, nor to the Angels; you should know that this must be similarly observed in other subjects.

You have here, therefore, 270 Syllogisms which conclude demonstratively concerning the respective Principle; these you will be able to vary in countless other ways through the nine-fold set of questions <note>The "ennead" of questions: a standard Lullian tool (Whether? What? Whence? etc.)</note>, as will be made clear in its proper place; so that even from this you may gather the immense fertility of this Combinatory Art. Concerning the variety and division of principles, however, see the more copious treatise in the following book on Combined Theology.

# CHAPTER V.

### *On the Respective Principle of the Medium.*

The way to an end is the "medium" <note>The middle or intermediary</note>. Therefore, the Medium is that which is cast between the principle <note>the beginning</note> and the end, and it savors of the nature of both; indeed, it can be called the end of those things preceding it, and the principle of those things following it. It is a respective, general, primitive, and necessary principle, without which no conjunction of things, no bond, or union would be given, but all things would be empty, which would be absurd. For the Medium is the subject through which the end flows into the principle, and the principle into the end, by a certain reciprocal action. And it is threefold.

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING

<margin>The threefold Middle.</margin>
First. The *Middle of Conjunction*, which knits extremes together: This is either substantial, accidental, or rational. The first is found between two substances; as between the Creature and God; Christ, the God-man <note>original: "θεάνθρωπος" (theanthropos)</note>, is the middle between God and man. The second is found among all compositions of things, where the union of matter and form is the middle. The third is of reason, just as the "middle term" in any logical proposition stands between the extreme terms.

Second. The *Middle of Relation*: as love is the middle between the lover and the beloved; and it encompasses the middle between the active and the passive <note>The original uses grammatical suffixes (.are., .tivum., .bile.) to show how a root word connects an action to an object</note>, such as "to do good" <note>beneficare</note> between the "beneficiary" and the "capable of receiving benefit," and so with the rest.

Third. The *Middle of Extremes*: as Liberality is the middle between prodigality and greed in Moral matters. In quantity, the middle is the point between extremes. In Order, as the number two is the middle between 1 and 3. In Dignity, as the Cherubim are between the Seraphim and the Thrones. In Leadership, between Dominations and Powers. Archangels, between Virtues and Angels. An Angel, between God and Man. An Analogous middle is the Ape between man and any other brute beast. The Zoophyte <note>A "plant-animal" like coral or sea anemones</note>, between animal and plant. The Sponge, between plant and inanimate thing. Generic Substance, between the corporeal and incorporeal. The Body, between the animate and inanimate. The Living, between the sensitive and insensitive. The Animal, between man and brute. The Species, between individuals; and so concerning countless others, as is evident in the Diagram.

# or of COMBINATORICS, BOOK II.

### ->Reducible terms for the Middle, drawn from the preceding Diagram, arranged into syllogistic form and combined with one another.<-

### *The Supernatural Middle.*

1. Wherever two extremes are given, there is also given a middle of conjunction connecting both.
But between God and man there is Christ, subsisting in both natures, Divine and human.
Therefore, Christ is the middle of conjunction, connecting and containing within himself the Divine and human nature.

### *The Angelic Middle.*

2. Whenever a transition is given from a most pure uncreated act <note>original: "actu purissimo increato"; referring to God as pure existence without potentiality</note> to a passive potentiality <note>referring to the capacity of matter or lower beings to be acted upon</note>, there is necessarily a middle pure created act.
But such a middle act is the Angelic intellect.
Therefore, the Angelic intellect is the middle between the Divine intellect (the most pure act) and human passive potentiality.

3. Wherever active and passive illumination are given, there is also given a middle.
But in the Cherubim, Principalities, and Archangels <note>ranks within the traditional celestial hierarchy</note>, active and passive illumination are given; for the higher beings illuminate the middle ones, and these illuminate those subject to them.
Therefore, the Cherubim, Principalities, and Archangels are the middles of the three Hierarchies.

### *The Generic Middle.*

Whenever Generic differences are given, a middle is also given between them.
But between Substance, Body, Living Thing, and Animal, generic differences are given: namely, Spiritual and corporeal; animate and inanimate; sensitive and insensitive; rational and irrational.
Therefore, the Spiritual, the Body, the Living Thing, and the Animal are middles.

### *The Logical Middle.*

Wherever two extreme terms are given, there is also given a middle.
In every proposition, two extreme terms are given.
Therefore, in every proposition, a middle is given. <note>In logic, the "middle term" is what connects the subject and predicate in a syllogism.</note>

### *The Physical Middle.*

In every generation <note>the process of coming into being</note>, when a transition is given from that which was to that which is, a middle is necessarily given there as well.
But privation <note>the absence of a form that is yet to be acquired</note> is the transition from that which was to that which is.
Therefore, privation is the middle among the three principles—that is, between matter and form.

### *The Moral Middle.*

Wherever there is excess and deficiency, a middle is given between them.
Between the extremes of generosity, there are the excess of prodigality <note>wasteful extravagance</note> and the deficiency of avarice <note>greed</note>.
Therefore, generosity is the middle between prodigality and avarice. <note>This reflects the Aristotelian "Golden Mean," where virtue is the balance between two vices.</note>

### *The Arithmetic Middle.*

Wherever there are two adjacent odd numbers, a middle even number is given between them.
But 1 and 3, 3 and 5, etc., are adjacent odd numbers.
Therefore, middle even numbers are given between them, namely 2 and 4.

### *The Geometric Middle.*

Wherever extremes are given, a middle is given.
In a Circle, extremes are given.
Therefore, in a circle, a middle is given, namely the center.

### *The Astronomical Middle (by event).*

Wherever an Astronomical opposition is given, a middle is given.
But in every Eclipse, an Astronomical opposition is given—namely, of the Sun and the Moon with the Earth mediating, or of the Sun and the Earth with the Moon mediating.
Therefore, in every Eclipse, a middle is given. This same argument also holds true in every opposition of the Planets.

You have here, Reader, several examples, so that you may see from them how to proceed in the remaining matters. For a Middle is given in every equation, connection, conclusion, measurement, participation, action, and perfection. All of these things will be demonstrated more at length in their proper time and place.

It argues that all connections in the universe require a mediating element to bridge extremes.</summary>

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE,

## CHAPTER VI.
### Concerning the Relative Principle of the End.

<margin>Without the principle of the end, there would be no desire.</margin>
The End <note>Latin: *Finis*; in this context, it means "purpose," "goal," or "boundary."</note> is a general, primitive, and necessary principle, without which there would be no desire in the nature of things, nothing would be good, there would be no action, and no impulse of a willed thing toward attaining its limit. The End is, therefore, that in which a principle finds rest; for principles rest in the end for the sake of which they exist. The ultimate end is God, blessed forever, for whose sake all things exist. Beneath this end, however, are innumerable other ends in which the principles of substance find rest when they reach that for the sake of which they exist. Thus, the End is fourfold:

First. The *End of perfection*, such as Blessedness <note>Latin: *Beatitudo*, the state of supreme spiritual happiness or union with the divine.</note>, which is the end of human life; to this are referred all things regarding perfection, goodness, greatness, etc.

Second. The *End of termination*, which is the rest achieved in a natural place; for the place toward which a thing moves is the end of that motion, such as a stone falling downward. To this are referred all finished things and all natural desires that tend toward rest as their end. To this is also referred the end of the "extreme" <note>The outer limit or final boundary.</note>, which belongs either to substance (as the end of generation is a human being); or to accident (as the end of a quantified body is the extension of its parts); or to time (thus the end of time is the successive motion of things); or to place (thus the end of place is to be somewhere); or to quality (thus the end of heat and cold is to alter the subjects they inhabit).

Third. The *End of privation*, which is the destruction of things; just as death is the end of life. To this are referred all corruptions and deprivations, whether natural, moral, or artificial.

Fourth. The *End of intention*, which is that toward which something is directed; as the end of Medicine is health, and the end of Law is Justice.

There are also secondary ends, which naturally rest within their own principles. In this way, metals have rest in their end, which is subordinate to the end of man; for if man did not exist, those principles would have no end in which to rest. Thus, the whole World was created for the sake of man. In like manner, man, who is the substantial end of the soul and body, is a Substance, which is the substantial end of Form and Matter.

Accidental ends are of this sort: merit is the end of acquired virtues, and the grace of God is the end of virtues given freely.

---

->Compound Terms reducible to the End proposed in syllogistic form, and combined among themselves.<-

<margin>
Simple Terms.
The Knowable.
The Desirable.
The Acceptable.
The Consumable.
The Exhaustible.
The Extreme.
The Completable.
The Delightful.
The Perfectible.
The Terminable.
The Proposable.
Rest.
Status.
Target.
Judicial sentence.
</margin>

1. Everything that falls under cognition has the character of an end.
All science falls under cognition.
Therefore, it has the character of an end.

2. That toward which all things tend is the ultimate end of all things.
God is He toward whom all things tend.
Therefore, God is the end of all things.

3. That which is desired by all is the end of our will.
The Good is what is desired by all.
Therefore, it is the end of our will.

4. Every limit toward which we intrinsically tend regards some end.
All human actions are of such a kind.
Therefore, all human actions tend toward some end; since no action occurs without a limit.

---

| | |
|:---|:---|
| 5. Everything that has a completion has the character of an end. <br> All perfection has a completion. <br> **Therefore, all perfection has the character of an end.** | Therefore, everything terminable has the character of an end. Which can also be said of everything exhaustible and consumable. |
| 6. Everything that has the character of an extreme has the character of an end. <br> Everything terminable has the character of an extreme. | 7. Everything that is born has an end, namely through corruption. <br> All things capable of being generated are of this sort. <br> **Therefore, they have an end.** |

**8.** Everything for the sake of which something is done, has the nature of an end <note>Latin: *finis*, meaning a goal or purpose</note>.
All things in the nature of things are done for the sake of something: For nothing is done, or has been done, in vain.
Therefore, all things have the nature of an end.

**9.** If there were no end established in things, there would be a process unto infinity; therefore there would be no rest, no state of being, no determined scope of things.
But all these things are absurd.
Therefore, of necessity, there is an end in things.

**10.** Every knowable object is the end of the cognitive faculty, or of the knower.
Everything that is understood is a knowable object.
Therefore, everything that is understood is the end of the cognitive faculty, or of the knower.

| Every power or (active principle) has as its end a (passive) object. |
| :--- |
| **Active Principles:** |
| That which makes good <note>original: *Bonificativum*</note> |
| That which makes great <note>original: *Magnificativum*</note> |
| That which makes to endure <note>original: *Durare factivum*</note> |

| That which makes glorious <note>original: *Glorificativum*</note> |
| **But every one of these is an agent, or they are agents under the reasoning of power. Therefore they have as their end a (passive) object.** |
| **That is to say:** |
| That which can be made good <note>original: *Bonificabile*</note> |
| That which can be made great <note>original: *Magnificabile*</note> |
| That which can be made to endure <note>original: *Durificabile*</note> |
| That which can be made powerful <note>original: *Potentificabile*</note> |

| That which can be made virtuous <note>original: *Virtutificabile*</note> |
| That which can be made true <note>original: *Verificabile*</note> |
| That which can be made glorious <note>original: *Glorificabile*</note> |

->Which, in the Lullian manner <note>Referring to Ramon Llull, the 13th-century mystic and logician whose "Great Art" inspired this work</note>, are reduced to a single Syllogism in this way:<-
->Every *active principle* has as its final object a *passive principle*.< -
->Every Agent under the reasoning of power is an *active principle*.< -
->Therefore every Agent under the reasoning of power has as its final object a *passive principle*.< -

# CHAPTER VII.

## Concerning the Relative Principle of Majority.

<margin>Majority is twofold.</margin> Majority is distinguished from Magnitude <note>or Greatness</note> in that the latter is an absolute principle of all Beings, enfolding magnitudes in itself without any relation; but the former becomes a Relative principle, insofar as it descends comparatively in order to other lower things; and as this applies to the orders of all Beings, so it has the nature of Majority toward all lower things; and it is therefore general and necessary, as that without which all things would be equal, which is impossible.

Furthermore, Majority is primarily twofold: *substantial* and *accidental*. The former is a substantial part of substance; the latter is a certain communicated image and likeness of substantial Majority. For just as Goodness is greater through its own effect than through a likeness and externally communicated greatness; so Majority through its own essence is simply a substantial part; just as communicated Goodness is an accidental part; hence if Majority were not a substantial part, Goodness would not be greater by essence than Goodness by accident, since it would have nothing from which to receive its substantial likeness.

These things having been thus premised, we shall deduce this Majority through all kinds of things and grades of Beings, so that the great offspring of arguments which buds forth from there may be more clearly revealed.

---

| Entities Compared | Relationship | Reason (Because) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1. Every Being, over Non-Being. | **is Greater or has Majority** | Because it is better to be than not to be; because to be is good; not to be is evil. |
| 2. Every Positive, over the Negative. | **is Greater or has Majority** | Because the positive affirms some goodness, which the negative denies. |
| 3. Every essential property, over a common accident. | **is Greater or has Majority** | Because a property perfects essentially, but an Accident only accidentally. |
| 4. Every real Accident, over an intentional one. | **is Greater or has Majority** | Because the former has real existence, the latter only objective existence, and does not exist without the intellect. |
| 5. Every most general genus, over a subordinate genus. | **is Greater or has Majority** | Because the former extends itself more widely than the latter. |
| 6. Every genus, over its own species. | **is Greater or has Majority** | Because it extends to more things, and because it essentially perfects its species. |
| 7. Every species, over its individuals. | **is Greater or has Majority** | Because it confers the perfect reasoning of essence upon the individuals. |

It uses Lullian logic to categorize active powers and their corresponding passive objects, establishing a framework for comparing different grades of being.</summary>

| :--- | :---: | :--- |
| Every Efficient Cause <note>The agent or force that brings something into being.</note>, than its effect. | | Because by reason of origin it is prior; thus the Final Cause <note>The ultimate purpose or goal.</note> is greater than the material, and the Final is greater than the Efficient in created things, as well as the Formal and Material. |
| Every end, than the means. | | Because the end exists for itself, but the medium exists for the sake of the end, namely, for something else. |

| Every act, than a potentiality. | | Because an act exists in reality, but potentiality is only in the state of becoming. |
| Everything necessary, than the contingent. | | Because the former is such that it cannot not be, while the latter can both be and not be. |
| Everything possible, than the impossible. | | Because the former has the power to exist, while the impossible has none at all. |
| Everything closer to God, than that which is more remote. | | Because the former is more excellent than the latter. |
| Every perfection more similar to God, than the dissimilar. | | Because the former possesses greater goodness than the latter. |
| Every Theological Virtue, than a Moral one. | | Because the former has a nobler object <note>God</note> than the latter <note>human conduct</note>. |

<image-desc>The table above is presented in the original manuscript with two large ornate braces. One brace groups the list of philosophical premises on the left toward the central phrase "Is Greater.", and a second brace groups that central phrase toward the list of justifications on the right.</image-desc>

->*From these things, it is necessarily concluded:*<-

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| ->*God.*<- | ->*The Heavens.*<- |
| 1. **God, the Best and Greatest** <note>original: "Deum Optimum Maximum"</note> is, without any comparison, greater than all Beings contained in the universe of things; in comparison to Him, all things are nothing. | 3. **The Heavens** containing are greater than the things contained. <br>The Firmament, or the Heaven of fixed stars, is greater than the sphere of Saturn. <br>Saturn's sphere, greater than Jupiter's. <br>Jupiter's, greater than Mars'. <br>Mars', greater than the Sun's. <br>The Sun's, greater than Venus', Mercury's, and the Moon's. <br>The Moon's, greater than the Elementary Heaven. <br>The Elementary, greater than the terrestrial globe. |
| --- | --- |
| ->*The Order of Things.*<- | ->*The Elements regarding their Virtues.*<- |
| An Angel is indeed greater than a man. <br>A man is greater than a beast. <br>A beast is greater than a plant. <br>A plant is greater than a Mineral. <br>Any mineral is greater than "prime matter" <note>The fundamental, formless substance of the universe</note>, which is nearly nothing. | 4. **Fire** is greater than the moist, because it is more active. <br>The moist is greater than the dry, because it perfects the dry so that it may subsist. |

# or of COMBINATORICS, BOOK II.

*By reason indeed of Knowledge and Virtues, which are their goods.*

The Wise man excels all.
The Theologian is greater than the Philosopher.
The Philosopher is greater than the Philologist <note>In this context, a lover of learning or literature.</note>.
The Philologist is greater than the Grammarian.
The Ethicist is greater than the Philosopher and the Philologist.
The Architect is greater than the Mason.

Monarchy is greater than Aristocracy.
Aristocracy is greater than Democracy.
Democracy is greater than Economics <note>original: "Oeconomiâ"; here referring to household management or the private domestic sphere.</note>.
Greater than all these is the man who is best instructed in both theological and moral virtues.
Conversely, one vicious man is greater than another in opposite ways.

---

->*By reason of the goods of the body.*<-

One man is greater than another in beauty, proportion, strength, health, natural talents, voice, and lung capacity <note>original: "pectore", literally "chest".</note>.

---

->*Regarding the good of fortune.*<-

One man is greater than another in Honor, dignity, fame, and celebrity of name, the favor of Great Men, and similar things: for in all these there is a Greatness compared to other Greatnesses.

---

->*Brute animals according to species, regarding size.*<-

6. Quadrupeds are greater than birds, regarding the quantity of their mass.
Swimming creatures are greater than quadrupeds and birds.
All these are greater than insects, except for dragons <note>Likely referring to large serpents or mythical beasts categorized as "insects" or "creeping things" in early modern taxonomy.</note>.

---

->*Regarding natural endowments or qualities.*<-

The Lion, in strength.
The Elephant, in wit.
The Horse, in alacrity.
The Tiger, in speed.
The Dog, in loyalty.
The Fox, in cunning.
The Ox, in endurance of labors.
The Livestock, in simplicity.
The Goat, in wantonness.
The Donkey, in laziness.

The Nightingale, in song.
The Wren <note>original: "Regulus".</note>, in cunning.
The Peacock, in beauty and variety of feathers.
The Hoopoe, in the variety of its crest.
The Goose, in stupidity; and so concerning the rest.
<gloss>By reason of the Object.</gloss>

The Cuttlefish, in malice.
The Octopus, in wondrous transformation; and so you should think that one must proceed concerning the remaining natures and properties.

There are also other plants greater than others,

->There is also a Majority with respect to absolute principles.<-

<note>The diagram illustrates how the nine fundamental attributes of the "Great Art"—Goodness, Greatness, Duration, Power, Wisdom, Will, Truth, Virtue, and Glory—apply to all things. It shows that within any specific category, one thing is "greater" than another in its own kind.</note>
**All** [Goodness, Greatness, Duration, Power, Wisdom, Will, Truth, Virtue, Glory]
**In its own kind, one is greater than another in its own kind: such as,**
Uncreated [is greater than] Created.
Angelic [is greater than] Human.

Sensitive [is greater than] Vegetable.
Vegetable [is greater than] Mineral.

**Therefore.**
**And let these remarks suffice concerning Majority.**

---

->**CHAPTER VIII.**<-

->*On the Relative Principle of Equality.*<-

<margin>The principle without which nothing would be equal to another.</margin>

Equality is a most general, primitive, and necessary Substantial Principle; for without it, no concord <note>harmony</note> would exist. It is substantial in that accidental equalities are sustained within it, and in it the goals of concord, goodness, and greatness find rest; these are the essential parts of Substance. Thus, a human being, under the principle of equality, generates a human being who is good and great. It is taken Accidentally, however, when the excess and defect of diverse qualities are reduced to equality, as when a Physician doses medicines "ana" <note>original: "anaticè," meaning in equal parts</note> or by equal weight.

It is divided into Uncreated and Created. The Uncreated is found in the Most Holy Trinity, in which there is the highest equality of persons with respect to Divine perfections, and the highest consubstantiality in nature and essence.

Created equality is again manifold: *Substantial and accidental.* *Substantial* is founded in the very essence of the whole substance with respect to perfection; as in an Angel, there is a perfect equality of will and intellect in their being. And in bodily things, all parts, with respect to substance, are equal in being. *Accidental equality* is considered either by intensity, extension, or measurement; or by reason of time, place, purpose, habit, quality, number, or age. For just as the beginning and the end relate to the Middle, so do Majority and Minority relate to Equality.

---

->*Compound terms reducible to Equality, arranged and combined in syllogistic form.*<-

| Simple Terms. | |
| :--- | :--- |
| Equal, Just <note>original: "Æquum," implying fairness or equity</note>. | Whenever to each is given what is his own, in that instance equality is given. |
| Just. | Commutative justice gives to each what is his own. |
| Similar. | Therefore, in Justice, equality is given. |
| Proportioned. | Whenever anything is measured or weighed under an equality of proportion, equality is given there. |
| Measured. | This occurs in Medicinal matters. |
| Weighed. | Therefore. |
| Peer <note>original: "Par," meaning an equal or a pair</note>. | All things that are without excess or defect are equal. |
| | This occurs in every measure and weight. |
| | Therefore, in these there is equality. |

Argue in the same way concerning the equality of intensity of qualities, times, places, positions, habits, proportions, ages, and other things which practice will teach; for whatever things are reduced to concord are also reduced to equality.

It uses a hierarchy of minerals and a logical table to show how "Majority" and "Equality" function as tools for categorization.</summary>

# or COMBINATORICS, BOOK II.
PART 2

### CHAPTER IX.
->Concerning the final Relative Principle of Minority.<-

<margin>The principle without which nothing is less than another, nor is there privation.</margin>
Minority <note>Latin: *Minoritas*; the state of being lesser or smaller</note> is a general and necessary principle, because without it no privation would occur, nothing would be given as less than another, and nothing would be evil or imperfect. It describes every falling away from goodness, greatness, duration, power, etc., and it has a place in all created things, God alone being exempt from all minority: for in as many ways as "Majority" is spoken of, so too is "Minority."

<margin>Simple terms.</margin>
->Composite terms reducible to Minority.<-

<margin>Smallness or insignificance.</margin>
Every falling away from Majority, Greatness, or goodness is an effect of minority.
Therefore <note>The author uses "Ergo" to conclude short logical syllogisms for each term.</note>.

<margin>Inferiority.</margin>
Everything inferior in respect to a superior is something "less."
Therefore.

<margin>Indigence.</margin>
Every lack of necessary things is subject to minority.
But all Indigence and want is of such a kind.
Therefore.

<margin>Misery.</margin>
Every deficiency of good possesses minority.
But all afflictions, calamities, and miseries are such.
Therefore.

<margin>Servitude.</margin>
Everything subjected to the dominion of another possesses the character of minority.
All servitude is such.
Therefore.

<margin>Want.</margin>
Everything pressed by the necessity of things possesses the character of minority.
Every needy and poor person is such.
Therefore.

<margin>Subjectability.</margin>
Everything that is subjected to another—whether ethically, physically, or logically—possesses the character of minority.
But such is servitude and the "subjects" of Logic <note>Referring to the 'subject' in a sentence or a logical proposition as being 'under' the predicate.</note>.
Therefore.

<margin>Obligation.</margin>
Everything that is obligated to provide something is a minority.

<margin>Defect.</margin>
Such is debt, money owed to others, and the rest.
Therefore.
Everything that departs from the due order possesses minority.

<margin>Infirmity.</margin>
Every defect, both in the genus of nature and of morals, is such.
Therefore.
Every defect of strength possesses the character of minority.
All infirmity, disease, and things contrary to health are such.
Therefore.

<margin>Fragility.</margin>
Everything poorly connected and compacted possesses the character of minority.
Every fragile thing is such.
Therefore.

<margin>Impotence.</margin>
Everything unfit for acting possesses the character of minority.
All impotence is such.
Therefore.

<margin>Imperfection.</margin>
Everything declining from its due perfection possesses the character of minority.
Every imperfect thing is such.
Therefore.

<margin>Emptiness.</margin>
Every place lacking a body <note>i.e., physical matter</note> possesses the character of minority.
Every vacuum is such.
Therefore.

<margin>Insufficiency.</margin>
Everything destitute of the faculty for working possesses the character of minority.
All insufficiency is such.
Therefore.

<margin>All privations.</margin>
Everything deprived of some good through the transition from one state to another possesses minority.
All privations are such.
Therefore.

And these are the things which I thought should be said concerning the eighteen principles—both absolute and relative—in the order in which Llull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), the philosopher who invented this system of logic.</note> described them. From these, the Reader will find innumerable seats of arguments <note>term: seats of arguments (sedes argumentorum); a classical rhetorical term for the "places" or categories where one finds logical proofs.</note> from every branch of knowledge, and the approach to the true method of the Art is gradually opened, as will be clear from what follows.

<summary>This page defines "Minority" as the fundamental principle of lack, inferiority, and imperfection, listing eighteen specific conditions (like misery, debt, and weakness) that fall under this category. It concludes the overview of the eighteen Lullian principles, framing them as the essential tools for logical discovery.</summary>

<header>THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING,
PART IV.</header>

# Concerning the Nine Universal Subjects,
### which are:

->*God. Angel. World/Heaven. Elements. Man. Sensitive nature. Vegetative nature. Inanimate nature. Accidents.* <note>In this context, "Accidents" refers to the philosophical term for qualities or properties that are not essential to a thing's nature, such as color or size.</note><-

<image-desc>A horizontal row of nine symbolic icons: a triangle (representing the Trinity/God), a winged cherub head (Angel), a circle with a center dot (the World/Sun), a square (the four Elements), a human figure (Man), a quadruped animal (Sensitive nature), a stylized tree (Vegetative nature), a lumpy cloud or stone-like shape (Inanimate nature), and a 3x3 grid of dots (Accidents/Categories).</image-desc>

## PREFACE.

Before we turn our pen to the exposition of the Universal Subjects which are considered in our Combinatory Art <note>original: "Ars Combinatoria" — a method of finding new knowledge by combining basic concepts, popularized by Ramon Llull and later Athanasius Kircher</note>, I have deemed it necessary to first place before the eyes of the Curious Reader a division and subdivision of the whole of corporeal and incorporeal Being or substance. This is so that he may contemplate, as if in a mirror, the orders of all Beings sprouting in their natural series, which we shall describe most extensively in the following Treatises of the sciences, displayed in a single glance. Indeed, I judged that this could in no way be done more skillfully or beautifully than through the likeness of a tree-like figure, adapted both for delivering the method and as an aid for a firmer memory. For just as a Tree, having risen from the earth through its roots as if from an abyss of seminal power, gradually expands into a trunk, and then into a progeny of innumerable branches subdivided again and again, displaying the joy of a vivid nature in a most abundant yield of fruit; so I might not incongruously compare the entire circuit of Nature, or the complex of created things, to a Tree. This tree, emerging from nothingness through the creative Power of almighty God, first expands into a chaotic Being, then into various grades of differing beings, and is divided into various genera and species like branches. It reaches from the lowest through the middle stages up to the highest—even to the very summit of the most wise Creator’s supramundane mind—by ascending through the order of created things, a feat which Philosophers cannot sufficiently admire.

But let us explain its branches. The stem or trunk, tending in a straight path toward the high summit, is so arranged that, beginning from the root—from the transcendental Being, I say, the principle of things—it expands through the "most general genus" <note>original: "genus generalissimum" — the highest category in logic that cannot be a species of anything else</note>, to use the language of the Logicians, to the subordinate genera, and from here to the species and individuals through the division of branches. It is arranged with such artifice that the differences of genera and species, descending in a lateral order from the top down to the last one, hold the logic of a reciprocal predication, now as the subject, now as the predicate. On the right side, this is done through universal affirmative propositions, which the word *Every* indicates; on the opposite side, however, the differences on the other branch are spoken of through reciprocal predication, but through universal negative propositions, which the word *None* indicates. Thus, from each of these, innumerable Syllogisms <note>A form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises</note> of things to be demonstrated may arise, by which the entire series of Nature is demonstrated directly from the subdivision and subordination of beings; a beautiful Epitome and Recapitulation <note>original: "Anacephaleosis"</note> of the whole of Dialectics <note>The art of investigating the truth through logical discussion</note>. Truly, since these things shine forth more clearly than the midday light in the Tree itself, I have thought it superfluous to dwell on them longer; for the Reader will perceive its most abundant fruits with easier effort through practice itself than I could easily describe with a copious winding of words. Let us therefore pursue the goal we have set for ourselves.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Combinatory Art" (a system of logic and classification), moving from the general introduction into the specific subjects of study, beginning with the divine and the angelic.</meta>

# or of the Combinatory Art, Book II.

### CHAPTER I.

->Concerning the first universal Subject, who is God.<-

The first subject of our Combinatory Art <note>original: "Artis nostræ Combinatoriæ"</note> is God, the Best and Greatest; an adequate object, I say, toward which the entire study and diligence of theologians is directed, to be explained with a sharp mental focus as far as the weakness of human intellect allows. However, concerning this most worthy subject of all, we shall treat it most abundantly in the following book, which is titled *Combinatory Theology*. In that book, the reader will find explained all those things which can in any way be referred back to Him.

### CHAPTER II.

->Concerning the second universal Subject, which is the Angel.<-

<margin>Subject II. Concerning the Angelic nature.</margin>
The second Subject is the Angel. We shall discuss how their operations and properties become known through the application of our Art once we have first set forth the nature of Angelic action through various subdivisions.

First, therefore, as far as Angels are concerned, properties are considered in them which are either negative or affirmative.

Negative properties are those which remove material composition, and all place and time—by which all corporeal things are both composed and measured; such as simplicity in Angels (though limited), sempiternity <note>original: "æviternitas"; refers to existence that has a beginning but no end</note>, and placelessness.

Affirmative properties are spiritual limitation (by which they are distinguished from the unlimited God), speed of motion, the highest power of intellect, and the highest power of will.

<margin>The state of Angels.</margin>
Specifically, Angels are divided into the good and the bad. In good Angels, one considers causes, effects, properties, and distinctions. The efficient cause of the goodness of Angels is the gracious election of God, to which is always subordinated the will and consent to remain in holiness and justice. Their properties are the good of virtue and the good of condition. The good of virtue is in respect to the intellect and the will; in respect to the intellect, it is the highest clarity and truth of knowledge; in respect to the will, it is the highest desire and impulse toward the Highest Good, and consequently toward the good descending from the First. The good of condition is happiness, which is glory and joy.

<margin>The effects of Angels.</margin>
The effects of good Angels are either in respect to God or to Creatures. The former have as their end the glorification and execution of Divine commands; the latter involve the care of all things, and are either General or Special.

<margin>The offices of Angels.</margin>
General offices are, first, to preserve the orders of Beings without confusion; to care for the temperaments of the Elements; such as restraining floods, the force of winds, and pestilential vapors.

Special offices are on behalf of either good men or bad men. On behalf of good men, they care for the goods of the soul, the body, or of fortune.

The goods of the soul, which they watch over to promote, are: to illuminate the intellect, to inspire good things, to deliver from the temptations of the devil, to move the will toward good actions, to implant the study of virtue, to love God, and to prevent consent to sin.

The goods of the body, which they assist in preserving, are: to moderate the state of the whole life, to defend from every danger to life, to repel the devil at death, and to introduce the soul into paradise after death.

The goods of fortune, which they promote, are: to advance the abundance of temporal things, to defend against harms, and to remove dangers.

But the effects in respect to bad men are: to punish their sins and to execute the justice of God provoked by sins, whether by temporal or eternal punishment.

The distinction of Angels is taken first from the grades of superiors and inferiors, which are the nine Choirs of Angels.

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING,

<margin>The nine Choirs of Angels are divided into three Hierarchies: and they are,</margin>

**1st Hierarchy**
*   **Seraphim, Cherubim, Thrones.** <note>The highest order of angels in Christian theology, believed to be closest to God.</note>
*   *Functions:* To illuminate and inflame the lower orders. To pour in knowledge. To care for tranquility.
*   *End Goal:* To assist God perpetually.

**2nd Hierarchy**
*   **Dominions, Powers, Principalities.**
*   *Office:* Whose office is: To rule over all opposing powers. To deliver the manner of exacting what is good and lawful. To instill the notion of leading and governing.
*   *End Goal:* To arrange and govern nature by universal laws and dictates.

**3rd Hierarchy**
*   **Virtues, Archangels, Angels.**
*   *Functions:* To watch over the virtues of the soul. To announce. To guard.
*   *End Goal:* To have the care of guarding particular things and even the smallest matters.

*   Presiding over the powers of the Elements.
*   The Terrestrial Globe.
*   The individual levels of Beings.
*   Kingdoms.
*   Provinces.
*   Cities.
*   Men.

-> They preside and assist. <-

---

### *On the Effects of Demons.*

<margin>The conditions of demons.</margin>
By a similar reasoning, the Cause, effects, properties, and distinction are considered in Demons. The efficient cause of their malice is the contempt of God, and the highest peak of honor, which they ambitiously sought from the first instant of their creation. Their properties are: the depravation of the intellect and the will; in the intellect indeed, being perpetually confused and perplexed, they doubt concerning God and divine judgments. In the will truly, they are carried away by irrevocable hatred and envy toward God, the Angels, and all creatures, especially the Elect, and finally toward themselves, with extreme unhappiness, ignominy, and pain.

<margin>The effects of demons.</margin>
The effects of Demons are either real or deceptive <note>original: "praestigiosi," referring to illusions or "prestidigitation" meant to trick the senses</note>. The real effects are: toward God, perpetual slander of Him with detestation of the divine name; toward all creatures, both rational and irrational—toward the latter, while they desire to overturn them all and destroy the whole World; toward those happy men, to pervert their souls, to deprave and hinder the intellectual, volitional, and executive power, to harm the human body in a thousand ways; to ruin all good things, and to harm honor and reputation, and to besiege the minds and bodies of men.

But the effects of Demons shine forth most powerfully toward the good, the elect, and the righteous, whom they are accustomed to tempt in a thousand ways in faith, repentance, and good works; but toward the reprobate, while they confirm them in the begun condition of an evil life, they lead them away from every good, and bring them to innumerable evils, which they perform in countless ways through deceptive and divinatory arts: which are:

---

<image-desc>A breakdown of demonic divination methods. The text groups specific practices (Necromancy, Augury, etc.) by the medium used (dead bodies, birds/lots, or the four elements: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth) to provide answers to those consulting them.</image-desc>

*   **Necromancy.** } By which they give answers to those consulting them through the bodies of the dead.
*   **Augury & Sorcery.** } By which they show future things through birds and lots.
*   **Pyromancy, Aeromancy, Hydromancy, Geomancy.** } By which they exhibit an answer concerning future events to those consulting them through the diverse signs of the elements (**Fire, Air, Water, Earth**).

# OR OF THE COMBINATORY ART, BOOK II.

To these are added Consultative Astrology, by which they give answers regarding stolen property through the inspection of the heavens and signs.

<margin>Various types of divinations.</margin>

In a word: Demons assume every possible form to lead souls away from God; while through various Superstitions, sorceries, eye-salves, ointments, love potions, characteristic rings, amulets, numbers, sounds, Sacrifices, purifications, solitudes, lots, bindings, whisperings, horrific imaginings, ecstasies of the mind, consecrations, devotions, dreams, and ceremonies, they agitate, possess, and master the hearts of mortals in wondrous ways; and they even take up the Most Holy Sacrament of the Altar <note>The Eucharist</note> for the execution of their malice and in contempt of God, displaying in all things a certain mimicry of divinity.

---

To these can be added the Relative Principles: Whether, how, why, etc., they differ, agree, are contrary, begin, mediate, end, or are greater, equal, or lesser. Of what kind they are in relation to the protection of the celestial orbs, the elements, men, animals, plants, mixtures, and metals. Whether there are many Angels distributed into different regions of the World; where there is a most fertile field for disputing about the Angels of the firmament, of the individual planets; and of those of fire, air, water, earth, and the subterranean realms.

To anyone who would ask, therefore, whether Angels truly exist; you shall respond by authority and by congruence <note>logical fitness or consistency</note>; in this manner: and first indeed by authority:

<margin>Whether Angels exist.</margin>
That truly and undoubtedly exists, the truth of which the Sacred Scriptures demonstrate to us in many places.

But the Holy Scriptures demonstrate in many places that there are Angels.

Therefore, Angels truly and undoubtedly exist.
By congruence, you shall prove it thus:

Wherever there are two extremes, of which one is a spiritual substance infinitely perfect and the creator of all, and the other is a corporeal-intellectual being <note>man</note>, there is also given a middle creature and a complete substance between the purely infinite spiritual and the corporeal-intellectual.

But God exists, and a corporeal-intellectual creature enclosed in the World exists, namely man, composed of a body and an incomplete substance, the soul.

Therefore, there is also a middle and complete intellectual substance, finite, created, and subsisting through itself, which is an Angel.

<meta>This page continues the author's systematic categorization of all knowledge using his "Great Art of Knowing." Having previously discussed God, he now moves to the second and third subjects of his system: Angels and the Physical Universe.</meta>

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWING

Again:
In the nature of things, there exists a substance that is purely material or corporeal, and one that is a mixture of the spiritual and the corporeal—that is, Man; therefore, there must also exist a purely intellectual created substance. But this can be nothing other than an Angel. Therefore, [they exist].

With these things supposed, let us now set down some examples of those terms which are reduced to the category of Angels.

It is asked, therefore: what is an Angelic substance? I respond that it is a created intellectual substance, incorporeal and complete. I prove this through the [First] Principles as follows:

<margin>Through Goodness.</margin>
1. Every incorporeal *goodness* that is complete, self-subsisting, and finite, is a created, complete intellectual substance. Every Angel is a certain incorporeal *goodness*, self-subsisting and finite. Therefore, an Angel is a complete, finite intellectual substance.

<margin>Through Greatness.</margin>
2. Every subsisting incorporeal *greatness* <note>original: "magnitudo"</note>, devoid of all extensive quantity <note>meaning it has no physical size or dimensions</note>, is a created, complete, finite intellectual substance. But an Angel is such a thing. Therefore, [it is an intellectual substance].

<margin>Through Duration.</margin>
3. Everything that subsists completely and lasts by an *eviternal* <note>term: "eviternity" (aeviternitas) - a state of duration between time and eternity, specifically for permanent created beings like angels</note> duration through its being and essence alone, is an intellectual, incorporeal, created substance. An Angel is such a thing. Therefore.

4. Whenever an effect is produced solely by the <margin>Power.</margin> immediacy of the person <note>original: "suppositi" - a philosophical term for an individual substance or person</note> and by the highest force of intellect and will, there is necessarily the highest *power* of the agent. But in Angels, the celestial spheres are moved solely by the immediacy of the person and the highest force and efficacy of intellect and will, and all corporeal things can be moved locally, from which incomprehensible effects are produced. Therefore, the highest power resides in them.

<margin>Through Knowledge.</margin>
5. Everything that *understands* not through external appearances <note>original: "species"</note> but through innate ones is a complete, incorporeal intellectual substance. An Angel is such a thing. Therefore.

In the same way, you may press the definition of an Angel through the remaining essential principles and the other attributes annexed to them; these will be demonstrated more diffusely in their own time and place in the combined Metaphysics. Truly, the remaining matters regarding Angelic nature, contained in the diagram of resolution, I shall demonstrate through the terms of the principles—both absolute and relative—which we set down above, showing what is proper to each principle.

### CHAPTER III.
->Concerning the World and the Heavens.<-

<margin>Subject III: Concerning the world and the Heavens.</margin>
<margin>The spheres are moved by Angels.</margin>
The World is a corporeal mass, embracing within itself the Heavens, the elements, mixtures, and whatever things exist, in the most absolute perfection, connection, and order. It is the third subject of our Art, to which all things that exist are recalled, like the integral parts of a great machine belonging to the whole. Such things are: that great and nearly infinite expanse called Heaven, and in it an innumerable multitude of spheres <note>original: "globorum"</note> which, according to the stable archetypal laws of God and with the help of intelligences <note>referring to angels as the "movers" of the planets</note>, are driven by motions suitable and appropriated to each for the good of all nature.

The World is divided principally into three parts: first, into Heaven and the celestial spheres, the most powerful part of the World. Second, into the Elements. Third, into Mixtures, which are completed by a fourfold order of Beings: the levels of existence, vegetation, sensation, and reason.

The essential terms or predicates are:

But now let us propose a certain paradigm of all that has been said, from which the Curious Reader may easily understand how, by the principles of our Art, the status of other proposed questions can be examined and proven.

To one asking, therefore, whether the World is eternal: you shall confirm the negative opinion through all the principles of our Art in this manner.

1. God alone is eternal. Therefore, nothing else among created things can be eternal. For it would coincide with God in respect to the goodness of infinite duration, which is impossible.

<margin>Through B. <note>Goodness</note></margin> 2. Every *goodness* that is good by participation is limited.
The World is a *goodness* that is good by participation.
Therefore it is limited. Therefore it is not eternal.
3. Every *effect* which has flowed forth from the essential good to the outside cannot be eternal.
But the World is an *effect* which has flowed forth from the goodness of God to the outside.
Therefore the World cannot be eternal.
The Major premise is proven:
God alone is eternal. Therefore nothing else among created things can be eternal. For it would coincide with God in respect to the goodness of infinite duration, which is impossible.
<margin>Through B.</margin> Everything *good by participation* cannot be eternal.
The World is a certain good by participation.
Therefore it cannot be eternal.
The reason for the Major premise is: Because participated goodness is finite; however, essential eternity is infinite. No finite thing can be eternal: Therefore the World can be neither infinite nor eternal.

<margin>Through M. <note>Greatness/Magnitude</note></margin> Everything that has *quantitative parts* and ratios of composition cannot be eternal.
The World has quantitative parts and is composed. Therefore it cannot be eternal.

Everything that involves *time* cannot <margin>Through D. <note>Duration</note></margin> be eternal. For eternity is the negation of time.
But the world involves *time*.
Therefore it cannot be eternal.
Everything that is *subject to various changes* <margin>Through D.</margin> cannot be eternal.
The World is subject to various changes.
Therefore [it is not eternal]. For eternity is the "interminable, simultaneous, and perfect possession of a whole life," <note>A famous definition of eternity by the philosopher Boethius.</note> which things do not apply to the world.
Everything that has *limited power* <margin>Through P. <note>Power</note></margin> cannot be eternal.
The World has limited power.
Therefore it cannot be eternal. Because eternity is the very omnipotence of God.
Nothing that is devoid of *intellect* and <margin>Through S. <note>Wisdom</note></margin> cognition can be eternal. For it would exist of itself, without a pre-known end.
But the World is devoid of intellect and cognition. Therefore it cannot be eternal. Because eternity is the very omniscience of God.
Everything that involves a *contradiction* <margin>Through Vo. <note>Will</note></margin> cannot be eternal.
But a World established from eternity by the will of God involves a contradiction.
Therefore it cannot be eternal. Because the goodness of God, which is the infinite perfection of the will, is eternity itself, with which it is impossible for a creature to coincide.
If the World were eternal, much more <margin>Through Vi. <note>Virtue</note></margin> would its *virtues* <note>powers</note> be eternal.
But no created *virtue* is eternal, nor can it be.
Therefore neither is that which follows from it. Because the efficacy of eternity is greater than the world.
If the World is eternal, eternity is not <margin>Through Ve. <note>Truth</note></margin> eternity.
But this is absurd.
Therefore also that which follows from it.
If the World were eternal, all things in it would *glory* <note>rejoice/find fulfillment</note> as if in the ultimate end. <margin>Through Gl. <note>Glory</note></margin>
But not all things in the world glory as if in the ultimate end.
Therefore the World is not eternal.

->Through the Relative Principles you shall prove the assumed proposition thus.<-

1. Everything that is dependent and temporal cannot be eternal. <margin>Through == <note>Equality/Relation</note></margin>
The form of the World is dependent and subject to time.
Therefore the World is not eternal.
Or otherwise:
Eternity differs from the World.
Therefore the world is not eternal. For if it does not differ, it is either equal or unequal. Not equal, because otherwise the World would be God himself, which is absurd.
Therefore it is unequal; therefore not eternal.

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE

<margin>Through Love <note>original: "Per ♡" (Heart symbol). In Kircher's system, symbols represent specific transcendental principles like Goodness or Greatness.</note></margin> 2. *Everything that accords with corruptible things cannot be eternal.*
*The world accords with corruptible things.*
*Therefore, it is not eternal. Because eternity and immensity exist together as one.*

<margin>Through Infinity <note>original: "Per ∞"</note></margin> 3. *Everything composed of contraries is not eternal.*
*The world is composed of contraries.*
*Therefore, it is not eternal. Because nothing that is eternal can be a composite.*

<margin>Through the Beginning <note>original: "Per α" (Alpha)</note></margin> 4. *Everything that has a beginning cannot be eternal.*
*The world has a beginning.*
*Therefore, it cannot be eternal. Because eternity lacks any beginning.*

<margin>Through the Sun <note>original: "Per ☉"</note></margin> 5. *Wherever there is no medium of participation between the finite and the infinite, there can be no eternity.*
*The world cannot be a medium of participation between the finite and the infinite.*
*Therefore [it is not eternal]. Because eternity does not admit succession.*

<margin>Through the End <note>original: "Per ω" (Omega)</note></margin> 6. *Wherever there is an end and a consummation, there can be no eternity.*
*The world, according to the witness of Holy Scripture, will have an end and a consummation.*
*Therefore, the world cannot be eternal. Because eternity lacks an end.*

<margin>Through Magnitude <note>original: "Per M"</note></margin> 7. *Everything that has a defined limit to its magnitude cannot be eternal.*
*The world has a defined limit to its magnitude.*
*Therefore, it cannot be eternal.*

<margin>Through Equality <note>original: "Per Æ." (Aequalitas)</note></margin> 8. *Everything that does not have a constant and firm state of equality, but is subject to continuous changeability, cannot be eternal.*
*The world does not have a constant and firm state of equality because of the continuous changeability of things.*
*Therefore, it cannot be eternal. Because eternity alone is always equal to itself in its immutability.*

<margin>Through the Lesser <note>original: "Per Min." (Minoritas)</note></margin> 9. *Everything lesser than God cannot be eternal.*
*The world is lesser than God.*
*Therefore, it cannot be eternal.*

You see, therefore, with what great ease the impossibility of the world's eternity is demonstrated through our eighteen universal principles. But so that the reader may also see how this same proposition can be further proven through the nine universal subjects, we provide these examples.

->*The impossibility of the world's eternity is demonstrated through the nine universal subjects.*<-

<margin>Through God <note>original: "Per △" (Triangle symbol for the Trinity)</note></margin> 1. *Every effect of God directed outward cannot be eternal.*
*The world is an effect of God directed outward.*
*Therefore, it cannot be eternal. Because God alone is eternal.*

<margin>Through the Moon/Soul <note>original: "Per ☾"</note></margin> 2. *Everything contained within the world cannot be eternal.*
*An Angel is contained within the world and is its inhabitant.*
*Therefore, an Angel is not eternal.*

<margin>Through the Sun/Intellect <note>original: "Per ☉"</note></margin> 3. *That which is a part of the world cannot be eternal.*
*The Heaven is a part of the world.*
*Therefore, the Heaven cannot be eternal.*

<margin>Through the Square/Elements <note>original: "Per □"</note></margin> 4. *No principles <note>meaning "foundational elements" here</note> can be eternal.*
*All the Elements are principles of the world.*
*Therefore, no Elements can be eternal.*

<margin>Through Man <note>original: "Per <image-desc>human figure</image-desc>"</note></margin> 5. *Every part of the world that is limited and perceptible by the senses cannot be eternal.*
*Man is a limited and sensible part of the world.*
*Therefore, he is not eternal.*

<margin>Through the Animal <note>original: "Per <image-desc>animal figure</image-desc>"</note></margin> 6. *Nothing possessing sensation can be eternal.*
*Every animal possesses sensation.*
*Therefore, no animal can be eternal.*

<margin>Through the Plant <note>original: "Per <image-desc>tree symbol</image-desc>"</note></margin> 7. *No vegetable thing can be eternal, since it is a part of the world.*
*Every tree is a vegetable thing and a part of the world.*
*Therefore, no tree can be eternal.*

<margin>Through the Mineral <note>original: "Per <image-desc>alchemical/mineral symbol</image-desc>"</note></margin> 8. *No mineral or mixture, whether perfect or imperfect, can be eternal.*
*All metals are minerals.*
*Therefore, they cannot be eternal, because of the innumerable transmutations they undergo.*

<margin>Through the Nine Accidents <note>original: "Per <image-desc>grid of nine dots</image-desc>"</note></margin> 9. *No accident <note>a philosophical term for a non-essential quality, like color or size</note> can be eternal.*
*But the nine categories are accidents which complete a substance.*
*Therefore, they cannot be eternal.*

Furthermore, if you wish to establish the impossibility of the world's eternity through the nine categories <term>Prædicamenta: the Aristotelian categories of being</term>, or through the Ennead of Inquiry <term>Enneadem Erotematicam: a nine-fold system of questioning</term>, you shall do it in this manner:

<margin>Through Substance.</margin> 1. *Every Substance contained in the world cannot be eternal, because it is limited.*
*All Beings, whether they simply exist, live, feel, or understand, are contained within the world. Therefore [they are not eternal].*

<margin>Through Qualities.</margin> 2. *Whatever flows from any limited subject, or completes it, cannot be eternal.*
*All qualities, whether intrinsic or extrinsic, flow from or complete a limited subject.*
*Therefore, they cannot be eternal.*

<margin>Through Quantity.</margin> 3. *Nothing terminated by quantitative extension can be eternal.*
*Every body is limited by quantitative extension. Therefore.*

<margin>Through Relation.</margin> 4. *Nothing which speaks of a categorical respect to another can be eternal.*
*All categorical Relations are of such a kind.*
*Therefore [they are not eternal]. Because correlatives are the mutual affections of individual things, which cannot be eternal.*

<margin>Through action.</margin> 5. Everything that has its own limit cannot be eternal.

<margin>Through passion <note>original: "passionem" - here meaning the state of being acted upon or undergoing change.</note>.</margin> 6. Everything that suffers or is acted upon by another cannot be eternal.
All such "passions" are of this nature.

All bodies are located or contained by a place. Therefore.

<margin>Through "when" <note>The category of Time.</note>.</margin> 8. Everything subject to the changes of time cannot be eternal.
All things in the World are subject to the changes of time. Therefore.

<margin>Through position.</margin> 9. Everything that has a certain fixed arrangement of its situation cannot be eternal.
All bodies of the World, and external actions—such as sitting, standing, or lying down—have a certain fixed arrangement of situation.
Therefore.

<margin>Through condition <note>original: "habitum" - referring to the state or "clothing" of a thing.</note>.</margin> 10. Everything consumed by age cannot be eternal.
Every condition is consumed by age. For indeed, all things shall grow old like a garment.
Therefore they cannot be eternal.

If, therefore, the World were eternal, it would also be infinite; which is against the Faith. And if it were infinite, all parts of the World would likewise be infinite; since all parts of the infinite are conceived under the rationale of the infinite, as the Schools <note>The Scholastic philosophers of the universities.</note> teach. But who does not see how absurd this is? Therefore, the World cannot be eternal.

You have here, Reader, an Example or Paradigm by which the impossibility of the World's eternity is proven through our 18 principles and through nine universal subjects by 36 syllogisms; so that even from this small specimen, you may clearly perceive the fertility of our Art in discovering means [of proof]. What has been said of this one question can be said of countless others.

---

### Questions concerning the Heavens.

The Paradigm we gave concerning the World can be given by the same reasoning concerning the nature and property of the Heavens. Now, the Heaven consists of that ethereal Expanse and the Stars, which revolve around the Earth within it according to the laws established by the Author <note>God.</note> with the help of intelligences <note>Angelic or spiritual movers believed to guide planetary spheres.</note>.

From this we deduce: First, that the Heaven is only threefold: sublunary <note>below the moon</note>, sidereal <note>the stars</note>, and empyrean <note>the highest heaven of fire/light</note>. Second, that the Heaven is not at all solid, but like a gentle breeze, which they call Ether. Third, that the Heaven and Stars are subject to corruption and alteration. Fourth, that the Stars of heaven are not radically distinct from sublunary matter. Fifth, that the Stars, besides their primary and secondary qualities, are also endowed with specific qualities by which they act upon lower things. Sixth, that all Stars have their own Atmospheres consisting of liquid and solid, and each enjoys its own center. Seventh, that all enjoy light partly their own and partly borrowed. Eighth, that all Stars, however innumerable, are distinguished from one another by species and specific qualities. Ninth, that the Stars influence both lower things and each other for the good of the universe. Tenth, that the Earth, fixed in the middle, is subject to the influences of all. All of which we promise to show, God willing, through the principles of our Combinatory Art with the greatest variety of arguments in our *Combinatory Cosmography and Astronomy*.

---

## CHAPTER IV.
### On Elementary Nature, which is the fourth universal subject.

<margin>The principle or subject of the nature of elements.</margin> <insert>E</insert>lements are the principles composing all corporeal nature, whether you look at the Heavens and Stars or the sublunary World; for all things came forth from that first chaotic mass in which they lay involved, by the power of Almighty God, into distinct orders and grades of being; so that we would be imagining a "fifth element" <note>The "quintessence" proposed by Aristotle.</note> in the Heavens for no reason.

<margin>Qualities of the elements.</margin> According to the opinion of all philosophers, there are four Elements: Fire, Air, Water, Earth. They differ among themselves, however, in that Fire behaves as an active principle. Water as a passive principle. The remaining two, Earth and Air, behave as a result [of the others]. In our General Art, "Fire" is taken as all heat, whether formal, virtual, or analogical in whatever way it is taken. "Water" is everything that is liquid. "Air" is every thin and subtle substance which maintains a certain likeness and analogy to the liquidity of water. "Earth" is everything solid, hard, or soft but not liquid, in whatever way it is considered. We deal first with the Elements in general, then in particular.

<margin>Composition of elements.</margin> Elements are the principles that concur in the material composition of all things: for it cannot happen that, given a mixture, it is not constituted of the hot, the moist, the cold, and the dry: just as...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Great Art of Knowledge, specifically focusing on the Four Elements (Earth, Water, Air, Fire) and how they interact through the author's system of universal principles.</meta>

# THE GREAT ART OF KNOWLEDGE

Just as one element cannot exist without another, so no mixture can be identified that is not formed by the mutual cooperation of the elements. Above all, the following must be considered in the Elements: quality, motion, figure, location, property, form, effect, change, alteration, opposition, and mixture; then the qualities proper to each, which are heat, moisture, cold, and dryness, and the secondary qualities that arise from these primary ones, as follows:

| Primary | Secondary |
| :--- | :--- |
| Heat. Cold. | Density. Rarity. |
| Moisture. Dryness. | Hardness. Softness. |
| | Coarseness. Subtlety. |
| | Aridity. Slipperiness. |
| | Viscosity. Brittleness. |
| | Roughness. Smoothness. |

Furthermore, you shall demonstrate the nature and property of the Elements through the principles of our Art in this manner:

### Through Absolute Principles

<margin>Through B <note>B stands for Bonitas (Goodness)</note></margin>
1. Everything without which a corporeal <note>physical or bodily</note> nature cannot exist is necessarily a great *good* in nature.
> The Elements are such things.
> Therefore, they are a great *good* in nature.

<margin>Through M <note>M stands for Magnitudo (Greatness/Magnitude)</note></margin>
2. Everything that *extends* itself through every corporeal substance is supremely great.
> The Elements are such things.
> Therefore the elements are great; because they enfold the entire corporeal substance. These must then be proven by induction: for they are in the Heavens, the Stars, man, beasts, plants, and Minerals.

<margin>Through D <note>D stands for Duratio (Duration/Durability)</note></margin>
3. Everything without which a corporeal nature cannot subsist is necessarily most *durable*.
> Such are the Elements.
> Therefore [they are most durable]. Because without elements, corporeal things would have no permanence.

<margin>Through P <note>P stands for Potentia (Power)</note></margin>
4. That which corporeal nature cannot operate without is of the highest *power*.
> The Elements are such things.
> Therefore the elements are of the highest *power*.

<margin>Through S <note>S stands for Sapientia (Wisdom/Knowledge)</note></margin>
5. Everything that concurs in the composition of bodies cannot do so without a *certain instinctive knowledge*: that is, an innate and natural appetite, which I always understand to be present in similar things.
> Such are the Elements. Because the heaviness of the Earth, when mixed, fixes things so they may subsist; fire excels in heat so they may be preserved; etc.
> Therefore, they cannot concur in the composition of bodies without *instinctive knowledge*.

<margin>Through Vo <note>Vo stands for Voluntas (Will/Appetite)</note></margin>
6. Everything that disposes toward the best end of nature *has a natural appetite* to achieve it.
> The Elements are such things. Because they seek the natural place due to them. Just as a stone is carried downward toward the Earth, and fire upward. Therefore [they have natural appetite].

<margin>Through Vi <note>Vi stands for Virtus (Virtue/Power)</note></margin>
7. That without which nothing corporeal can act possesses the highest *virtue*.
> Without the Elements, nothing corporeal can act.
> Therefore the Elements possess great virtue.

<margin>Through Ve <note>Ve stands for Veritas (Truth)</note></margin>
8. That without which nothing corporeal can exist and operate possesses the reasons of the highest *truth*.
> Without the Elements, nothing corporeal can exist and operate.
> Therefore the Elements possess the reasons of the highest *truth*.

<margin>Through Gl <note>Gl stands for Gloria (Glory)</note></margin>
9. That which finds *rest* in its own works, that is its highest *glory*.
> The elements find their rest in mixtures <note>combined substances</note>.
> Therefore they glory in mixtures.

### Through Relative Principles

<margin>Through = <note>Difference/Distinction</note></margin>
1. Those things differ among themselves where one can be separated from the other.
> But one Element can be separated from another.
> Therefore they differ among themselves. The minor premise is proven through chemical experiments.

<margin>Through ♡ <note>Concordance/Agreement</note></margin>
2. Those things which have a certain *suitability* toward each other are also said to *accord*.
> But the element of water *agrees* in qualities partly with air and partly with earth; fire partly with air and partly with earth. For water agrees with air in moisture and with earth in coldness; fire agrees with air in heat and with earth in dryness.
> Therefore they accord.

<margin>Through ⚬-⚬ <note>Contrariety/Opposition</note></margin>
3. All things that cannot exist together in one and the same subject under the same most intense degree are *contraries*.
> The heat of fire and the moisture of water, as well as the moisture of air and the dryness of earth, cannot exist together. Therefore [they are contraries].

<margin>Through α <note>Beginning/Principle</note></margin>
4. All *originating principles* necessarily have *originated things*, which are the mixed bodies.
> The Elements are *originating principles*,
> Therefore they have originated things, which are the mixtures.

<margin>Through ☉ <note>Medium/Middle</note></margin>
5. All things that participate equally from the extremes are *mediating* things.
> Among the elements, Air participates in heat from fire and moisture from water; Earth participates in dryness from fire and moisture from water equally.
> Therefore they are *Media* <note>middle points</note> between fire and water.

6. All
